Examination instructions, as printed
  • Answer SIX questions only.
  • All questions carry equal marks.
  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage
  • Blank pages if any, to be struck by (X) at the end of each question.

Instruction 2 on the paper states that all questions carry equal marks, and six answered questions are required against a printed 'Total Marks - 100'. Every question is therefore recorded at 16 marks, so six answered questions total 96 against the printed 100. The discrepancy is printed on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected.

Understand this first

There are two different things in this question and candidates run them together. A certificate is a piece of paper that says a survey was passed. A survey is the event that makes the paper true. Harmonization is not about the paper at all - it is about making all the surveys happen at the same time, so that one surveyor attendance discharges SOLAS, Load Lines and MARPOL together, on one anniversary date, in one three-month window, on one five-year clock. Once that is clear, the second half of limb (b) stops being a memory test. A certificate has expired; the survey that would renew it cannot be done here; the ship is therefore uncertificated and undetainably so. The regulation's answer is not to repair anything - it is to ask the flag State for a short, purpose-limited extension that gets the ship to a port where the survey can happen, and nothing more. Three months, one voyage, and the replacement certificate still runs from the old expiry date. The system is deliberately unforgiving about time, because the alternative is a fleet that renews its certificates whenever convenient.

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 43 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. What an oil tanker carries, and under which convention

A tanker's certificates fall into four families, and grouping them by instrument rather than reciting them as a list is what earns limb (a): the examiner is asking which convention issues each one. Everything below is for a tanker on international voyages.

2. SOLAS 1974/1988 certificates

CertificateHookValidity
Cargo Ship Safety Construction CertificateSOLAS I/12, chapters II-1 and II-25 years
Cargo Ship Safety Equipment Certificate + Record of Equipment (Form E)SOLAS I/125 years
Cargo Ship Safety Radio Certificate + Form RSOLAS I/12, chapter IV5 years
(Alternative) Cargo Ship Safety Certificate, Form C - the three above combinedSOLAS I/125 years
Safety Management Certificate; Document of Compliance held by the CompanySOLAS chapter IX / ISM Code5 years each
International Ship Security CertificateSOLAS chapter XI-2 / ISPS Code5 years
Continuous Synopsis RecordSOLAS XI-1/5no expiry - amended as particulars change
Minimum Safe Manning DocumentSOLAS V/14no fixed expiry - reissued when manning changes
Exemption Certificate, where grantedSOLAS I/12not beyond the certificate it qualifies

An oil tanker is additionally subject to the Enhanced Survey Programme under SOLAS XI-1/2 and the ESP Code, which is not a certificate but a survey regime whose report file is carried on board and is what the Cargo Ship Safety Construction Certificate now rests on.

3. MARPOL certificates - where the tanker differs most

CertificateAnnexValidity
International Oil Pollution Prevention Certificate with Form B supplementAnnex I - Form B is the oil-tanker form; a non-tanker carries Form A5 years
International Pollution Prevention Certificate for the Carriage of Noxious Liquid Substances in Bulk, or an IBC Code Certificate of Fitness, where so certifiedAnnex II5 years
International Sewage Pollution Prevention CertificateAnnex IV5 years
International Air Pollution Prevention Certificate + supplementAnnex VI5 years
Engine International Air Pollution Prevention Certificate + NOx Technical FileNOx Technical Code under Annex VIengine life, subject to survey
International Energy Efficiency CertificateAnnex VI regulation 24ship's life, subject to change of flag or major conversion
DCS Statement of Compliance - fuel oil consumption reportingAnnex VIissued annually

Garbage attracts no certificate under Annex V - only a Garbage Management Plan and Record Book. It is worth saying so expressly, because a candidate who lists an "IGPP certificate" invents one.

4. Other conventions

  • International Load Line Certificate - Load Line Convention 1966 as modified by the 1988 Protocol. Five years.
  • International Tonnage Certificate (1969) - Tonnage Convention. No expiry: it stands until the ship's tonnage characteristics change or she changes flag. This is the standard trap in a validity question.
  • Maritime Labour Certificate and the Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance, Parts I and II - MLC 2006, for ships of 500 GT and above on international voyages. Five years, with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary.
  • International Anti-fouling System Certificate - AFS Convention. Valid for the life of the system unless it is changed.
  • International Ballast Water Management Certificate - BWM Convention. Five years.
  • Certificate of Insurance or other Financial Security in respect of Civil Liability for Oil Pollution Damage - CLC 1992, where the tanker carries more than 2,000 tonnes of persistent oil in bulk as cargo. Its validity is the period of the insurance, so it is usually one year and is the certificate most often found expired.
  • Bunker oil pollution damage insurance certificate - Bunkers Convention 2001, above 1,000 GT; and, where the convention applies, the wreck removal insurance certificate at 300 GT and above.
  • Ship Sanitation Control (Exemption) Certificate - International Health Regulations 2005, not an IMO instrument. Six months.

The Certificate of Class is deliberately not in any of these lists. It is issued under the society's own rules and is a commercial and contractual document, not a statutory one - although the same society, acting as a Recognized Organization, will normally have issued most of the statutory certificates above on the flag State's behalf.

5. Harmonization under SOLAS 74/88 - what it actually changed

Before harmonization each convention ran its own survey clock. A ship could hold a SOLAS certificate surveyed in March, a Load Line certificate surveyed in September and an IOPP certificate surveyed in December, each with its own expiry, its own surveyor attendance and its own off-hire. The 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and to the Load Line Convention introduced the Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, in force 3 February 2000, and MARPOL was amended to match. Its four elements are:

  • One anniversary date for the ship - the day and month corresponding to the expiry date of the certificate - which governs every harmonized certificate she holds.
  • A common five-year maximum period of validity for all of them, so they expire together.
  • A common survey pattern - initial, annual, intermediate, renewal, plus additional surveys after damage or repair - with a window of three months either side of each anniversary date in which the annual or intermediate survey may be held.
  • Flexibility on issue and extension, so that a genuine operational difficulty does not force a ship to trade uncertificated.

The purpose is economic as much as regulatory: one surveyor attendance discharges several conventions, off-hire falls, and the ship's certificates can no longer drift apart. Note the term of art - the intermediate survey takes the place of the second or third annual survey; it does not sit in addition to it.

6. A certificate that has just expired, in a port that cannot survey

This is the practical half of limb (b) and it is answered from SOLAS regulation I/14. The first point to make, before any procedure, is the hard one: an expired statutory certificate is a detainable deficiency, and the ship does not sail on the strength of the Chief Engineer's opinion that she is fit. The regulation supplies three distinct reliefs and they are not interchangeable:

SituationRelief under regulation I/14Limit
Renewal survey COMPLETED, but the new certificate cannot be issued or placed on board before expiryThe Administration or a Recognized Organization acting for it may endorse the existing certificate, which is then accepted as validA further five months from the expiry date
Certificate expired and the ship is not in a port in which she is to be surveyed - the case in this questionThe Administration may extend the period of validity, solely to allow the ship to complete her voyage to the port where she will be surveyed, and only where that is proper and reasonableThree months, and no more
Ship engaged on short voyages, not covered aboveA period of graceOne month from the stated expiry date

Two conditions on the three-month extension are routinely missed and both are worth a mark. First, it is granted for the voyage only: on arrival at the survey port the ship may not leave again on the strength of the extension - she must first hold the new certificate. Second, the extension does not buy time: when the renewal survey is completed, the new certificate is valid to a date not exceeding five years from the original expiry date, not five years from the survey. The lost time is lost.

7. The actions to take, in the order you would take them

  • Stop and establish the facts. Which certificate, which convention, the exact expiry date, whether the underlying survey has in fact been completed. The relief available depends entirely on that last question - a completed survey is a five-month endorsement, an uncompleted one is at best a three-month voyage extension.
  • Inform the Company and the Designated Person Ashore immediately. Under the ISM Code this is a non-conformity and it is reported as one; it is not a matter for the ship to resolve locally.
  • Apply to the flag Administration, through the Recognized Organization, for the appropriate relief - endorsement, extension or period of grace - stating the survey position, the port of intended survey and the voyage proposed. The RO cannot grant the three-month extension of its own motion; that power is the Administration's.
  • Request an occasional or additional survey where a surveyor can in fact attend, or arrange attendance at the next port. Inadequate facilities at this port is the premise of the question, not a permanent answer.
  • Notify the port State authority rather than let a PSCO discover it. A disclosed and covered expiry is a different conversation from a concealed one.
  • Notify the P&I club, the hull underwriters and the charterers. Trading with an invalid statutory certificate can prejudice cover and will usually put the ship off-hire; the club needs to know before the voyage, not after.
  • Satisfy yourself that the ship is materially compliant and record it. Testing and recording the machinery and equipment the certificate covers, and logging the result, is what makes the application for extension credible and is what a surveyor will look for on arrival.
  • Do not sail until the extension or endorsement is in hand and on board, and then sail only to the nominated survey port.

The principle underneath all of it: the certificate is evidence of compliance, not the compliance itself - but it is the only evidence a port State is obliged to accept, and the relief for an expired one comes from the Administration, in writing, before departure.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The limbs are 8 and 8, so the answer must be genuinely halved. Limb (a) is an enumeration and it is marked as one: the marks are in coverage and correct attribution, not in prose. Write it as tables grouped by instrument. Limb (b) is two questions welded together - a definition of harmonization and a scenario - and both must be answered. A candidate who explains harmonization beautifully and then writes two lines on the expired certificate throws away half of eight marks.

The validity trap

The question asks for validity, so the marks are in the exceptions, not the rule. Almost everything is five years. What is examined is what is not:

  • International Tonnage Certificate (1969) - no expiry at all.
  • International Energy Efficiency Certificate - the ship's life, unless she changes flag or undergoes a major conversion.
  • International Anti-fouling System Certificate - the life of the system.
  • CLC and Bunkers insurance certificates - the period of the insurance, typically one year, renewed each 20 February with the club year.
  • Ship Sanitation Control (Exemption) Certificate - six months, and not an IMO instrument at all.
  • DCS Statement of Compliance - issued annually.
  • Continuous Synopsis Record and the Minimum Safe Manning Document - no expiry; they are amended or reissued on change.

Form A or Form B - the one-word tanker answer

If you write only one tanker-specific thing in limb (a), write this: the IOPP certificate carries a Form B supplement for an oil tanker and a Form A for a non-tanker. It is the cleanest demonstration that you have read the question's word "tanker" and not answered about ships in general. Add the Enhanced Survey Programme under SOLAS XI-1/2 and the CLC certificate above 2,000 tonnes of persistent oil, and the tanker point is fully made.

Harmonization: the number that carries the concept

One anniversary date, one five-year cycle, one three-month window. If you remember nothing else about the harmonized system, those three numbers reconstruct it. The 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and Load Lines brought it in, it took effect on 3 February 2000, and MARPOL was amended so that its certificates ran on the same clock.

Five months, three months, one month - keep them apart

PeriodWhen it appliesWhat it is for
Five monthsThe renewal survey is COMPLETE but the certificate cannot be issued or placed on boardPaperwork relief - the ship has been surveyed and passed
Three monthsThe certificate has expired and the ship is NOT in a port where she can be surveyedVoyage relief - to reach the survey port, and nothing else
One monthShort-voyage ships, where neither of the above appliesA period of grace

The question puts you squarely in the middle row. The examiner is testing whether you reach for the three-month voyage extension and whether you know its two conditions: it takes you to the survey port and no further, and the replacement certificate still runs five years from the original expiry date.

What is NOT on the list at this sitting

Do not list the International Certificate on Inventory of Hazardous Materials or a Ready-for-Recycling Certificate as statutory here. The Hong Kong Convention entered into force on 26 June 2025, a year after this examination. The EU Ship Recycling Regulation required an IHM on EU-calling ships before that, but it is a regional instrument, not a convention certificate, and the question asks for conventions.

Common mistakes

  • Listing certificates without naming the convention. The stem asks twice for the convention and its reference; an unattributed list cannot score full marks.
  • Including the Certificate of Class among the statutory certificates. It is a class document. The society may also be the RO, and saying so is what shows you understand the distinction rather than having missed it.
  • Giving every certificate five years, and so missing the Tonnage, IEEC, AFS, insurance and sanitation exceptions.
  • Answering limb (b) as though harmonization means only "all certificates expire together". The survey window and the common survey pattern are the operative parts.
  • Treating the expired-certificate scenario as an engineering problem. It is a certification problem: the answer is an application to the Administration, not a repair.
  • Sailing on the extension past the survey port. The regulation forbids exactly that.

Examiner traps

  • "Who grants the extension?" The Administration. The RO may endorse a certificate where the survey is complete, but the three-month voyage extension is the flag State's power.
  • "How long is the new certificate valid after an extension?" Five years from the original expiry date - not from the survey. Candidates almost always give the survey date.
  • "Can she load and sail from the survey port on the extension?" No. On arrival she must have the new certificate before leaving.
  • "Does the intermediate survey replace an annual survey or add to it?" It replaces the second or third annual survey.
  • "What is the window?" Three months before to three months after the anniversary date.
  • "Which certificate expires most often in practice?" The insurance certificates - CLC and Bunkers - because they run with the policy year, not the harmonized cycle.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Show me the Form B supplement to the IOPP certificate and tell me what is in it.
  • Your Cargo Ship Safety Equipment Certificate expires next week and you are on a fourteen-day passage. What do you do, and who do you write to?
  • What is the difference between an annual, an intermediate and a renewal survey?
  • What is an occasional survey and who asks for it?
  • Which of your certificates would still be valid if the ship changed flag tomorrow?
  • Who issued your statutory certificates, and under what authority?

Memory framework

Four families, three numbers, one date. The families are SOLAS, MARPOL, other conventions, and insurance. The three numbers are 5 months / 3 months / 1 month of relief under regulation I/14. The one date is 3 February 2000, when the harmonized system took effect. Then the tanker overlay: Form B, ESP, CLC.

Regulation and source map

  • SOLAS I/12 - issue and endorsement of certificates.
  • SOLAS I/14 - duration and validity; the five-month endorsement, the three-month voyage extension, the one-month period of grace.
  • SOLAS XI-1/2 and the ESP Code - the Enhanced Survey Programme for oil tankers and bulk carriers.
  • SOLAS chapter IX - the ISM Code, DOC and SMC. Chapter XI-2 - ISPS and the ISSC.
  • 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and to the Load Line Convention - the harmonized system, in force 3 February 2000.
  • MARPOL Annex I regulations 6 to 10 - IOPP certificate, Form B supplement, surveys. Annex VI regulation 24 - the IEEC.
  • Tonnage Convention 1969 - the International Tonnage Certificate, which does not expire.
  • MLC 2006 Title 5 - the Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC Parts I and II.
  • CLC 1992 and Bunkers 2001 - compulsory insurance certificates.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

The MIW corpus holds the SOLAS Consolidated Edition 2024, which by its own cover incorporates amendments in effect on 1 July 2024. This examination sat in June 2024, so the operative consolidated edition at the sitting was the previous one. Regulations I/12 and I/14 are relied on here as unchanged across that boundary - they have stood in their harmonized form since the 1988 Protocol took effect in 2000 - but the earlier edition has not been read, and the reliance is recorded as an accepted limitation rather than presented as verified.

SOLAS Chapter I is not among the provisions verified against primary text in the corpus SOLAS log, so the regulation numbering and the extension periods are carried as authoritative secondary. No SOLAS wording is quoted anywhere. The model-certificate form letters are flagged for verification in the corpus's own construction record and are named subject to that flag.

The domestic statute at this sitting is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, under which the Directorate General of Shipping issues or delegates these certificates for Indian ships. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 is not law at this sitting and appears nowhere in this answer.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) What an oil tanker carries, and under which convention
  2. __________(a) SOLAS 1974/1988 certificates
  3. __________(a) MARPOL certificates - where the tanker differs most
  4. __________(a) Other conventions

Flashcards 8

1 Frame by instrument. 2 SOLAS certificates. 3 MARPOL certificates, with Form B. 4 Other conventions and insurance. 5 What harmonization did. 6 The regulation I/14 relief. 7 The actions in order. Sections 1-4 are limb (a); 5-7 are limb (b).

Limb (b) is two questions in one sentence - define harmonization AND handle the expired certificate. Splitting 5 from 6 and 7 is what makes both visible.

The IOPP certificate's supplement is Form B - the oil-tanker form; a non-tanker carries Form A. Add the Enhanced Survey Programme under SOLAS XI-1/2 and the ESP Code, and the CLC 1992 insurance certificate where she carries more than 2,000 tonnes of persistent oil in bulk as cargo.

Three tanker-specific items turn a generic certificate list into an answer to the question actually asked.

International Tonnage Certificate 1969 - no expiry. IEEC - the ship's life, unless she changes flag or is majorly converted. AFS certificate - the life of the system. CLC and Bunkers insurance certificates - the insurance period, usually one year. DCS Statement of Compliance - annual. Ship Sanitation Control (Exemption) Certificate - six months. CSR and the Minimum Safe Manning Document - no expiry; amended on change.

The question asks for validity, so the marks sit in the exceptions, not in the five-year rule.

Four things: one anniversary date for the ship governing every harmonized certificate; a common five-year maximum validity so they expire together; a common survey pattern - initial, annual, intermediate, renewal, additional - with a window of three months either side of the anniversary date; and flexibility on issue and extension. In force 3 February 2000, with MARPOL amended to match. The intermediate survey replaces the second or third annual survey.

Candidates say 'all certificates expire together' and stop. The survey window and the common survey pattern are the operative parts.

Establish whether the renewal survey has been completed. If it has, the Administration or RO may endorse the existing certificate for up to five months from expiry. If it has not, apply to the Administration - not the RO - to extend validity for no more than three months, solely to reach the survey port. Report it as an ISM non-conformity, notify the port State, P&I and charterers, and do not sail until the extension is on board.

The relief you can ask for depends entirely on whether the survey is done. Candidates reach for the three-month extension in every case.

Five years from the original expiry date of the old certificate, not five years from the renewal survey. And on arrival at the survey port the ship may not leave again on the strength of the extension - she must first hold the new certificate.

Both conditions are in the regulation and both are routinely missed. The extension buys a voyage, not time.

The International Certificate on Inventory of Hazardous Materials or a Ready-for-Recycling Certificate. The Hong Kong Convention entered into force on 26 June 2025, a year after this sitting. The EU Ship Recycling Regulation required an IHM earlier, but it is a regional instrument, not a convention certificate.

This is the paper's date-sensitive certificate. A later MIW answer that lists it belongs to a later sitting.

No. It is issued under the classification society's own rules and is a commercial and contractual document. But the same society, acting as a Recognized Organization authorised by the flag State, will normally have issued most of the statutory certificates on the flag's behalf - which is why the two are confused.

Naming the distinction AND the overlap is what shows understanding rather than a memorised exclusion.

Quick revision
15-second recall
An oil tanker carries SOLAS certificates (Safety Construction, Safety Equipment with Form E, Safety Radio - or the combined Form C; SMC and DOC under chapter IX; ISSC under XI-2; CSR and Safe Manning Document, neither expiring), MARPOL certificates (IOPP with the Form B tanker supplement, NLS or IBC CoF, ISPP, IAPP, EIAPP, IEEC at Annex VI reg 24, DCS Statement of Compliance), plus Load Line, Tonnage (no expiry), Maritime Labour Certificate with DMLC I and II, AFS, BWM, and the CLC insurance certificate above 2,000 tonnes of persistent oil. Most run five years. Harmonization came from the 1988 Protocols, in force 3 February 2000: one anniversary date, one five-year cycle, a survey window of three months either side, and the intermediate survey replacing the second or third annual. If a certificate has just expired and the port cannot survey, apply to the Administration under regulation I/14 for an extension of not more than three months, to complete the voyage to the survey port only - and the new certificate still runs five years from the original expiry date.
Keywords
SOLAS I/12SOLAS I/141988 Protocol3 February 2000harmonized systemanniversary datethree-month windowintermediate surveyIOPP Form BIEECESP CodeCLC certificateInternational Tonnage Certificateno expiryfive-month endorsementthree-month extensionone-month graceRecognized Organizationdetainable deficiencyMerchant Shipping Act 1958
Critical numbers
  • Harmonized system in force 3 February 2000, under the 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and Load Lines
  • Five years - the common maximum validity of the harmonized certificates
  • Three months either side of the anniversary date - the annual and intermediate survey window
  • Five months - endorsement where the renewal survey is complete but the certificate is not yet issued
  • Three months - the maximum extension to complete a voyage to the survey port
  • One month - the period of grace for short-voyage ships
  • 500 GT for the Maritime Labour Certificate; 2,000 tonnes of persistent oil for CLC; 1,000 GT for Bunkers
  • Six months - the Ship Sanitation Control (Exemption) Certificate
Critical regulation
SOLAS regulation I/14 for duration, validity and every form of relief - the five-month endorsement, the three-month voyage extension and the one-month period of grace - read with regulation I/12 for issue and endorsement. The harmonized system itself comes from the 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and to the Load Line Convention, in force 3 February 2000.
Major trap
Giving every certificate a five-year validity. The International Tonnage Certificate does not expire, the IEEC runs for the ship's life, the AFS certificate for the life of the system, the insurance certificates for the insurance period and the sanitation certificate for six months. Second trap: sailing on the three-month extension past the survey port - the regulation expressly forbids it, and the replacement certificate still runs from the original expiry date, so the extension buys a voyage, not time. Third trap, specific to this sitting: listing an IHM or Ready-for-Recycling certificate. The Hong Kong Convention entered into force on 26 June 2025, a year after this examination.

Also on the platform: QP2406 Q3 - what a port State does when it finds a certificate defect of exactly this kind · QP2406 Q5 - the ISM audit cycle that sustains the Safety Management Certificate in this list

Understand this first

Strip the jargon and this is two questions about who decides. Limb (a): who decides what the law is? In a civil law country the legislature decided in advance and wrote it in a code, so the judge looks it up and the lawyer argues about how it applies. In a common law country the judges have been deciding it case by case for centuries, so the judge's own judgment becomes law and the lawyer's job is to find the cases and fight. Whoever is passive in one system is active in the other, and every other difference - precedent, cross-examination, the length of judgments, whose expert the court believes - falls out of that. Limb (b): who decides whether an international convention reaches you, the seafarer, on a ship in Mumbai? Two separate acts. First the State agrees to be bound internationally, which every State does the same way. Then the convention has to get inside that State's legal system, and that is where the systems part company. A monist State lets it in automatically on publication. A dualist State makes Parliament pass an Act first, and until it does, the convention is a promise between governments that no court will enforce. India is dualist, so the answer to "does SOLAS bind my ship?" is strictly no - the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 does.

Memory cue. CODE v CASES, then TWO STEPS and TWO AXES. Code against cases is the whole of limb (a) (1) - and whoever is passive in one system is active in the other: civil judge active (2), common lawyer active (3). Limb (b): two steps - bound internationally (5), then enforceable domestically (6); and two axes - monist/dualist is NOT common/civil (6). Land it on India: Article 253, MS Act 1958 (7).

Cover49 core points beneath them

Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 49 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The single difference from which the rest follows
  • Fix the sense: 'civil law' here means the Romano-Germanic family, not civil versus criminal
  • Civil law starts from a comprehensive enacted code; common law from accumulated decided cases
  • Precedent is binding at common law (stare decisis), persuasive only in civil law
  • Judicial law-making is part of the common law function and no part of the civil law one
  • Statutory interpretation is narrow and literal at common law, purposive in civil law
  • Present everything that follows as flowing from this one difference
2. The judge
  • Common law judge - neutral umpire; rules on admissibility and law; does not investigate
  • Long reasoned judgments, because they will bind later courts and are themselves a source of law
  • Appointed late, from senior advocates
  • Civil law judge - active investigator; directs the inquiry, questions witnesses, appoints the court's own expert
  • Shorter formal judgments applying the code, not intended to create law
  • A career judiciary, trained as judges from the start
3. The lawyer
  • Common law advocate drives the case - claims, evidence, witnesses, strategy, discovery
  • Examination in chief and cross-examination; oral forensic skill is the valued competence
  • Cites cases as binding authority
  • Civil law advocate assists the court within an inquiry the judge directs
  • Proposes evidence rather than presenting it; questions usually put through the judge
  • Written pleading and doctrinal reasoning; cites the code, with commentary and prior decisions as persuasive
  • The practical consequence: the same collision is litigated completely differently, which is why forum clauses are negotiated hard
4. Adversarial and inquisitorial - the models these roles create
  • Name both models expressly - this is what 'the roles of the lawyer and Judge' is really asking for
  • Adversarial: two parties contest before a passive tribunal; truth from the contest
  • Inquisitorial: the court investigates; truth from the inquiry
  • Neither exists in pure form - say so, because that is the mark of understanding rather than memorising
5. How a State becomes bound in the first place
  • Consent to be bound - ratification, acceptance, approval or accession - plus entry into force
  • Identical for every State; a question of the law of treaties, not of the domestic system
  • Whether it then has effect INSIDE the State is a separate question answered by that State's constitution
  • A State that has ratified but not implemented is in breach and may not plead its internal law - but no shipowner can sue on the convention in its courts
6. Monism and dualism
  • Monist - one legal order; the treaty becomes domestic law automatically on publication and often outranks ordinary statute
  • Dualist - two legal orders; no domestic effect until an implementing Act is passed, and the statute governs if it diverges
  • Examples: France, the Netherlands, Japan monist; the United Kingdom, India, Australia, Canada dualist
  • THE KEY POINT: monism and dualism do NOT map onto civil law and common law - two independent axes
  • The Scandinavian States are civil law and dualist; the United States is common law with self-executing and non-self-executing treaties
  • The correlation exists because the common law world inherited a separation-of-powers rule, not a sources-of-law rule

  • Common law system, dualist State

  • Monist leak: the same Article read differently in different States, with no supranational tribunal
  • IMO's answer is institutional, not legal - the III Code and the mandatory Member State Audit Scheme

Model written answer

1. The single difference from which the rest follows

Both families answer the same question - where does a judge find the law? - and they answer it in opposite directions. A civil law system starts from a comprehensive written code enacted by the legislature; the judge's task is to find the applicable article and apply it to the facts. A common law system starts from the accumulated body of decided cases; the judge's task is to find the principle those cases establish and apply it, and in doing so may make new law. Everything else in limb (a) - precedent, the role of the judge, the role of the lawyer, the shape of the trial - follows from that one difference and should be presented as following from it.

Common lawCivil law
Primary sourceDecided cases, supplemented by statuteCodified statute - civil, commercial, penal and procedural codes
OriginEngland, spread through the Commonwealth and the United StatesRoman law through the Napoleonic and German codifications; most of continental Europe, Latin America, much of Asia and Africa
PrecedentBinding - stare decisis; a higher court's ratio binds lower courtsPersuasive only - a consistent line of decisions carries weight but does not bind
Judicial law-makingExpressly part of the function - the common law develops through judgmentsNot the function - the judge applies the code; law-making belongs to the legislature
ProcedureAdversarialInquisitorial
Statutory interpretationNarrow and literal, because statute is read against a background of case lawPurposive, because the code is meant to be comprehensive and gaps are filled by reasoning from principle

2. The judge

In a common law court the judge is a neutral umpire. He or she does not investigate, does not call witnesses of the court's own motion, and does not decide what evidence should be sought. The judge rules on admissibility, controls the procedure, decides questions of law, directs the jury where there is one, and then decides the case on the material the parties have chosen to put forward. The judgment is reasoned at length, because it must be, since it will bind later courts - the judgment is itself a source of law. Judges are typically appointed late in a career, from among senior practising advocates.

In a civil law court the judge is an active investigator. The judge directs the inquiry, decides what evidence is needed, questions witnesses directly, and may appoint the court's own expert - who is the court's expert, not either party's. The judgment applies the code and is characteristically shorter and more formal, because it is not intended to create law for the future. Judges are usually career judges, trained and appointed as judges from the start rather than drawn from the bar.

3. The lawyer

The roles are the mirror image of the judicial ones.

Common law advocateCivil law advocate
Control of the caseDrives it. Chooses the claims, the evidence, the witnesses and the strategyAssists the court. The judge directs the inquiry; the advocate frames submissions within it
EvidenceInvestigates, gathers, discloses on discovery, and presentsProposes evidence to the judge, who decides what will be taken
WitnessesExamines in chief and cross-examines the opponent's witnessesRarely cross-examines; questions are usually put through the judge
Skill valuedOral advocacy and forensic techniqueWritten pleading and doctrinal reasoning from the code
Use of authorityCites cases as binding authorityCites the code, with academic commentary (la doctrine) and prior decisions as persuasive support

The practical consequence for a shipowner is real rather than academic. The same collision, litigated in a common law forum, produces long disclosure, witness statements and days of cross-examination; in a civil law forum it produces a court-appointed expert whose report will largely determine the outcome. That is why forum and jurisdiction clauses are negotiated as hard as they are.

4. Adversarial and inquisitorial - the models these roles create

It is worth naming the two procedural models expressly, because the examiner's phrase "the roles of the lawyer and Judge" is really asking for them. Adversarial: two parties contest before a passive tribunal, and the truth is expected to emerge from the contest. Inquisitorial: the court investigates, and the truth is expected to emerge from the inquiry. Neither model exists in a pure form anywhere - English civil procedure has become markedly more judge-managed, and civil law systems have widened the parties' role - and saying so is the mark of an answer that has understood the distinction rather than memorised it.

5. How a State becomes bound in the first place

Limb (b) needs one step before the common law / civil law contrast can be applied. A convention binds a State internationally when that State has consented to be bound - by ratification, acceptance, approval or accession - and the convention has entered into force. That is a question of international law and the same for every State. Whether the convention then has any effect inside that State, on its citizens and in its courts, is an entirely separate question answered by that State's own constitution. A State that has ratified but not implemented is in breach internationally and can be held to account for it - it may not plead its own internal law as an excuse - but a shipowner still cannot sue on the convention in its courts.

6. Monism and dualism

MonistDualist
The theoryInternational and domestic law are one legal orderThey are two separate legal orders
On ratificationThe treaty becomes part of domestic law automatically, usually on publication in the official journalThe treaty binds the State internationally but has no domestic effect until legislation is passed
What a court appliesThe treaty itself, often ranking above ordinary statuteThe implementing statute - and if the statute and the treaty diverge, the statute governs
Typical examplesFrance, the Netherlands, Japan, and most of Latin AmericaThe United Kingdom, India, Australia, Canada, and the United States for non-self-executing treaties

Here is the point most answers get wrong, and stating it correctly is worth a mark on its own: monism and dualism do not map onto civil law and common law. They are two independent axes. The common law world is largely dualist because it inherited the British constitutional rule that the executive makes treaties and only Parliament makes law - a separation-of-powers point, not a sources-of-law point. Many civil law States are monist. But the Scandinavian civil law States are firmly dualist, and the United States - a common law system - treats some treaties as self-executing and others not. The correlation is a historical tendency, not a rule.

7. India as the worked example

India is a common law system and a dualist one, which makes it the natural example for an Indian examination.

  • The executive negotiates, signs and ratifies. There is no requirement of parliamentary approval to bind India internationally.
  • Article 253 of the Constitution empowers Parliament to make law for the whole or any part of the territory of India for implementing any treaty, agreement or convention with another country - and notably to do so even on subjects in the State List, so the federal division of powers is not an obstacle to implementation.
  • Until Parliament acts, the convention has no force in Indian courts. A ratified but unimplemented convention creates an international obligation and nothing more domestically, although Indian courts will use it as an aid to construing ambiguous legislation and will lean against a reading that puts India in breach.
  • For shipping, the implementing vehicle at this examination is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, whose Parts give domestic effect to SOLAS, MARPOL, the Load Line Convention, STCW and the collision regulations, supported by rules made under the Act and by DG Shipping circulars and merchant shipping notices that carry the detail. The Directorate General of Shipping administers it and authorises Recognized Organizations to survey and certify on India's behalf.
  • Where a convention is given effect by a dedicated statute rather than by the Merchant Shipping Act, that statute does the work - the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 for arrest and maritime claims, and the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 for marine insurance, are the two a Chief Engineer meets most often.

The practical consequence, and the one worth stating because it is the engineer's experience of the whole subject: the ship does not comply with SOLAS - she complies with the flag State's rules made under its implementing Act. Where the two differ, it is the national rule the surveyor enforces and the national rule the master must follow, and the divergence is India's problem at the IMO, not the ship's problem in port.

8. The contrasting mechanism in a monist State

Take the same convention into France. On ratification and publication in the Journal Officiel the convention becomes part of French law directly, and under the French constitution a duly ratified treaty ranks above ordinary statute. No implementing Act is needed to make it enforceable, and a French court will apply the convention text itself. Two practical consequences follow. Implementation is faster, because no legislative slot is required. But it is also less adapted: penalties, enforcement machinery and administrative detail are not supplied by the convention, so a monist State still legislates for those, and a convention drafted for uniformity can sit awkwardly in a domestic legal order that has not been reshaped to receive it.

9. Why the difference matters at sea

Uniformity is the entire point of maritime conventions, and both routes leak. The dualist route leaks through delay and divergence - years between ratification and implementation, and implementing statutes that shade the convention's meaning. The monist route leaks through interpretation - the same Article read differently by courts in different States, with no supranational tribunal to reconcile them. IMO's response to both is not legal but institutional: the III Code and the mandatory IMO Member State Audit Scheme, which audit whether a State has in fact given effect to the instruments it has ratified, and the flag State implementation machinery that sits alongside it. That is the honest closing point - the difference between the two systems is managed, not eliminated.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

8 and 8. Limb (a) has three named sub-tasks - the main difference, the role of the lawyer, the role of the judge - so it needs three visible answers, and the safest way to make them visible is a comparison table for each. Limb (b) says "State and describe with examples", which is an instruction, not decoration: an answer with no named country cannot score full marks. Give India in detail because it is an Indian paper, and one monist State by way of contrast.

The mark-winning sentence in the whole question

Monism and dualism are not the same distinction as civil law and common law. Most candidates draw a single line down the page - common law on one side, civil law and monism on the other - and it is wrong. They are two independent axes: the Scandinavian States are civil law and dualist; the United States is common law and treats some treaties as self-executing. Write the correlation as a historical tendency and then say why: the common law world is largely dualist because it inherited the British rule that the executive makes treaties and only Parliament makes law, which is a separation-of-powers point rather than a sources-of-law point.

The two-step that limb (b) is really testing

  1. Becoming bound internationally - ratification, acceptance, approval or accession, plus entry into force. Identical for every State, and governed by the law of treaties.
  2. Becoming enforceable domestically - automatic in a monist State on publication; requiring an implementing Act in a dualist one.

A State that has completed step one and not step two is in breach internationally and cannot plead its own internal law as an excuse - but a shipowner still has nothing to sue on in its courts. Getting that gap right is the substance of the limb.

India, in five lines you can write from memory

  • Common law system, dualist State.
  • The executive ratifies; Article 253 empowers Parliament to legislate to implement - even on State List subjects.
  • Until it legislates, the convention has no domestic force, though courts use it to construe ambiguous statutes.
  • For shipping the vehicle is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, with rules and DG Shipping circulars supplying detail.
  • Also the Admiralty Act 2017 for arrest and maritime claims and the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 for marine insurance.

The statute trap on this sitting

The governing statute at a June 2024 examination is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced on 15 March 2026 - twenty-one months after this paper - and must not appear in the answer. If you have read a later MIW answer that cites the 2025 Act, it belongs to a later sitting. This is the single most likely temporal error on this question, precisely because the 2025 Act is the natural thing for a candidate revising in a later year to reach for.

Do not name cases

The doctrine of precedent and the Indian dualist position are both classically illustrated by decided cases, and it is tempting to name one. Do not, unless you are certain. A misattributed authority is worse than no authority in a law answer, and the principle scores the mark without it. This answer names none, for exactly that reason.

Common mistakes

  • Answering only the first sentence of limb (a) - the sources-of-law difference - and never reaching the judge and the lawyer, which are two-thirds of what is asked.
  • Saying the civil law judge "is like a prosecutor". He is not; he is an investigator for the court, and in a civil case there is no prosecution at all.
  • Presenting the adversarial and inquisitorial models as pure. Neither is, and modern English civil procedure is heavily judge-managed.
  • Writing limb (b) with no country named, when the stem says "with examples".
  • Equating dualism with common law, which is the error the whole limb is built to catch.
  • Saying a ratified convention automatically binds Indian citizens. It does not - Parliament must legislate first.
  • Reaching for the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 at a June 2024 sitting.

Examiner traps

  • "Is precedent binding in a civil law system?" No - persuasive only. A consistent line of decisions carries real weight, but it does not bind.
  • "Who appoints the expert?" In a civil law court, the court does, and that expert's report will usually decide the case. In a common law court each party brings its own.
  • "India has ratified convention X. Can I rely on it in an Indian court tomorrow?" No, not until it is implemented - though the court may use it to construe an ambiguous Act.
  • "Give a common law State that is not dualist." The United States, for treaties its courts hold to be self-executing.
  • "Give a civil law State that is not monist." The Scandinavian States.
  • "Which Article of the Constitution?" 253. And its significance is that it reaches State List subjects, so federalism is not a defence to non-implementation.
  • "Does SOLAS bind your ship?" Strictly, the flag State's rules made under its implementing Act bind her. That is the answer that shows you understood limb (b).

Likely oral follow-up

  • Your flag State's rule is stricter than the convention. Which do you follow, and why?
  • How does an IMO convention amendment reach your ship? Take tacit acceptance through to the DG Shipping circular.
  • Which Indian Act implements MARPOL, and who enforces it?
  • What is the III Code and the Member State Audit Scheme for?
  • You are arrested in a civil law port. How will the proceedings differ from what you would expect at home?

Memory framework

CODE versus CASES; JUDGE versus LAWYER; then TWO STEPS and TWO AXES. Code against cases is the source. Whoever is passive in one system is active in the other - civil law judge active, common law lawyer active. Then limb (b): two steps (bound internationally, then enforceable domestically) and two axes (monist / dualist is not common law / civil law). Finish with India: Article 253, Merchant Shipping Act 1958.

Regulation and source map

  • Article 253, Constitution of India - Parliament's power to legislate to implement treaties, including on State List subjects.
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 - the implementing vehicle for the IMO conventions in India at this sitting.
  • Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 - arrest and maritime claims.
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 - marine insurance, including general average at section 66.
  • Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties 1969 - signature, ratification, accession, entry into force, and the rule that internal law is no excuse for non-performance.
  • IMO Instruments Implementation Code (III Code) and the mandatory IMO Member State Audit Scheme - the institutional answer to divergent implementation.
  • MLC 2006 Article XIII - amendment by the ILO Special Tripartite Committee, an example of a convention whose amendment route is not IMO tacit acceptance at all.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

This is the one question on the paper that rests mainly on legal theory rather than on a held instrument. The comparative material is carried as authoritative secondary and no textbook, author or case is named. MIW holds no law reports, and no case is cited anywhere in this answer.

The Constitution of India is not held by the MIW corpus. Article 253 is cited by number and effect only, and no constitutional wording is quoted. The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 is likewise not held; its Parts are described by function rather than by section number.

The classification of named States as monist or dualist is given as the general position. A State's practice can differ between treaty types and can change over time, so the countries are offered as examples rather than as a fixed table.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) The single difference from which the rest follows
  2. __________(a) The judge
  3. __________(a) The lawyer
  4. __________(a) Adversarial and inquisitorial - the models these roles create
  5. __________(b) How a State becomes bound in the first place
  6. __________(b) Monism and dualism
  7. __________(b) India as the worked example
  8. __________(b) The contrasting mechanism in a monist State

Flashcards 8

1 The structural difference (code v cases). 2 The judge. 3 The lawyer. 4 Adversarial and inquisitorial. 5 Becoming bound internationally. 6 Monism and dualism. 7 India worked through. 8 A monist contrast. 9 Why it matters at sea. Sections 1-4 are limb (a); 5-9 are limb (b).

Limb (a) names three sub-tasks and limb (b) demands examples. Separate sections are what make all of them visible to the examiner.

Common law: a neutral umpire. Rules on admissibility and questions of law, decides on the material the parties choose to put forward, and writes a long reasoned judgment that binds later courts. Appointed late from senior advocates. Civil law: an active investigator. Directs the inquiry, decides what evidence is needed, questions witnesses, appoints the court's own expert, and writes a shorter formal judgment applying the code. A career judiciary.

The role of the judge is one of three explicitly named sub-tasks in limb (a) and cannot be left implicit.

Common law: drives the case - chooses claims, evidence, witnesses and strategy; gives discovery; examines in chief and cross-examines; cites cases as binding authority. Oral advocacy is the valued skill. Civil law: assists a court-directed inquiry - proposes evidence for the judge to take, rarely cross-examines, and puts questions through the judge; cites the code with commentary and prior decisions as persuasive. Written pleading is the valued skill.

Whoever is passive in one system is active in the other. That symmetry is the cleanest way to remember both roles at once.

Monist: international and domestic law are one legal order, so a ratified and published treaty becomes part of domestic law automatically and often ranks above ordinary statute - France, the Netherlands, Japan. Dualist: two separate legal orders, so the treaty binds the State internationally but has no domestic effect until an implementing Act is passed, and if statute and treaty diverge the statute governs - the United Kingdom, India, Australia, Canada.

This is the core of limb (b). Without it the limb becomes a vague essay about international cooperation.

No - they are two independent axes. The Scandinavian States are civil law and firmly dualist. The United States is common law and treats some treaties as self-executing. The correlation exists only because the common law world inherited the British constitutional rule that the executive makes treaties and only Parliament makes law - a separation-of-powers point, not a sources-of-law point.

This is the single mark-winning sentence in the question, and the error the limb is built to catch.

The executive ratifies or accedes, binding India internationally. Article 253 of the Constitution then empowers Parliament to legislate to implement it, including on State List subjects. Parliament does so through the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, under which rules are made and DG Shipping circulars and merchant shipping notices supply the operational detail. DG Shipping administers it and authorises Recognized Organizations to survey and certify. Until Parliament acts, the convention has no force in an Indian court - though a court will use it to construe an ambiguous statute.

Limb (b) says 'with examples'. This is the example an Indian examiner is waiting for.

The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced on 15 March 2026 - twenty-one months after this examination - and must not be cited on this paper. The Coastal Shipping Act, 2025 and the Bills of Lading Act, 2025 are likewise after this sitting.

This is the paper's standing statute trap, and this question walks straight into it because limb (b) invites the Indian example.

No. India is dualist, so what binds an Indian ship is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and the rules made under it, which give effect to SOLAS. Where the national rule and the convention differ, the surveyor enforces the national rule and the master follows it; the divergence is India's problem at the IMO, not the ship's problem in port.

Saying this converts limb (b) from theory into the answer of someone who has actually sailed, and it is the natural oral follow-up.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Civil law = codified statute, judge finds the article, precedent persuasive only, judge investigates, lawyer assists the court, inquisitorial. Common law = decided cases, precedent binding (stare decisis), judge a neutral umpire whose judgment is itself law, lawyer drives the case and cross-examines, adversarial. For limb (b), two steps: a State becomes bound internationally by ratification or accession plus entry into force; it becomes enforceable domestically automatically in a monist State on publication, but only by an implementing Act in a dualist one. Monism and dualism are a separate axis from civil and common law - Scandinavia is civil law and dualist, the USA is common law with self-executing treaties. India is common law and dualist: the executive ratifies, Article 253 lets Parliament legislate even on State List subjects, and shipping conventions arrive through the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, its rules and DG Shipping circulars.

Critical numbers
  • Article 253 of the Constitution of India - the treaty-implementation power
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 - the implementing statute at this sitting
  • Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017; Marine Insurance Act, 1963
  • The IMO Member State Audit Scheme became mandatory on 1 January 2016
  • Two axes, not one: civil/common law and monist/dualist are independent
Critical regulation
Article 253 of the Constitution of India is the provision that makes limb (b) an Indian answer rather than a general one: Parliament may legislate for the whole or any part of India to implement a treaty, and may do so even on State List subjects. The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 is what it has produced for the IMO conventions at this sitting.
Major trap
Equating dualism with common law and monism with civil law. They are independent axes - the Scandinavian civil law States are dualist and the United States is a common law State with self-executing treaties. Second trap: writing limb (b) with no country named, when the stem expressly says "with examples". Third trap, specific to this sitting: citing the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025. It commenced on 15 March 2026, twenty-one months after this examination - the governing statute here is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. Fourth: naming a case you are not certain of. A misattributed authority is worse than none, and the principle scores without it.

Understand this first

Port State control is built on a single idea: the certificate is a claim, and the port State is entitled to test the claim - but only so far. The officer starts by reading the papers and looking around. That is all he is entitled to do, until something he sees contradicts what the papers say. That contradiction is what "clear grounds" means, and it is a protection for the ship as much as a power for the officer - without it, every inspection could become an unlimited one. So limb (a) is really asking: what is the evidence that unlocks the deeper inspection? And the second half adds the sharpest version of it: when the contradiction is not in a machine but in the management system, everything else on board becomes doubtful at once, which is why a major non-conformity can detain a ship whose machinery is fine. Limb (b) then turns the question round to your side of it. Every one of those five deficiencies is a machine that probably works but cannot be proved to work at the moment the officer asks - so the preventive action is never "repair it", it is "test it and record it". And limb (c) closes the loop: if the port State found what the certificate denied, somebody certified wrongly, and the society that did it has to answer for that.

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. PR17 - what it is, and the scenarios that lead to it

Memory cue. Two definitions, five failures, one conflict. Definitions: clear grounds = EVIDENCE + SUBSTANTIALLY + FAMILIARITY (2); ISM deficiencies = the SYSTEM, not the item (4). Five failures: power, steering, oil, fire, bilge - and one sentence for all of them, tested and recorded or it is a deficiency (5). One conflict: the certificate said compliant, the port State found otherwise, and the RO signed it (6).
