Show the core points to cover
1. The governing instrument at this sitting
  • A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023 - operative at this sitting
  • The enabling provisions: SOLAS I/19, MARPOL art 5 and reg 11, Load Lines art 21, STCW X, MLC reg 5.2.1
  • Do not name a 34th Assembly resolution - that Assembly sat December 2025
2. "Clear grounds" - the definition
  • The initial inspection is limited to certificates, documents and a general impression of condition
  • Clear grounds = evidence that ship, equipment or crew does not correspond substantially with convention requirements
  • OR that master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures for safety or pollution prevention
  • 'Substantially' excludes the trivial; 'evidence' excludes suspicion
  • They unlock the more detailed inspection - and they are a limit on the officer as much as a power
3. The circumstances that create clear grounds
  • Documents - certificates absent, expired, invalid or apparently falsified; Oil Record Book gaps
  • Equipment - principal equipment or arrangements absent, or present but visibly unmaintained
  • Condition - observed serious deterioration, heavy corrosion, plainly unsafe conditions
  • People - crew unable to demonstrate familiarity with essential operations
  • Operations - unsafe operations observed, SMS procedures not followed
  • Third-party information - pilot, port authority, previous port State, crew complaint, incident report
  • Manning - not manned per the Safe Manning Document, or hours of rest not observed
4. ISM-related deficiencies
  • The distinguishing feature: they point at the system, not at an item - so nothing on board can be relied on
  • SMC or DOC absent, invalid, expired or not matching the ship
  • No SMS documentation, or uncontrolled or superseded documentation, or in a language the crew cannot read
  • Master unable to demonstrate his authority under the SMS, or unaware of the master's review
  • DPA unidentified or no contact details available
  • No evidence of internal audits - or audits that never raise a non-conformity
  • Drills not carried out or not recorded; maintenance not per the SMS; critical equipment not identified
  • Non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences not reported, investigated or closed
  • A major non-conformity - serious threat to personnel, ship or environment, requiring immediate corrective action, including systemic failure to implement the Code
  • Consequence can reach the Company's DOC and the rest of the fleet, not just this ship
5. Five detainable deficiencies, their causes and their prevention
  • State the test first: unsafe to proceed to sea, unreasonable threat to the environment, or deficiencies so numerous they show systemic failure
  • Emergency source of power - never load-tested, changeover untested; prevent by testing ON LOAD and recording it
  • Steering gear and emergency steering - tests done on paper, changeover never exercised; prevent by real drills and posted instructions
  • OWS / 15 ppm equipment - coalescer exhausted, monitor uncalibrated, stopping device disabled, ORB not reconciling; prevent by never permitting a bypass and reconciling the ORB yourself
  • Fire pumps and fixed systems - emergency pump unprimed, main isolated, bottle survey lapsed; prevent by proving two jets and locking isolating valves open
  • Bilge arrangements and machinery space condition - alarms disabled, quick-closing valves wired open, remote stops untested, oil-soaked lagging; prevent by scheduled in-port testing and a standing cleanliness discipline
  • Close with the unifying sentence: these are testing and recording failures before they are mechanical failures
6. PR17 - what it is, and the scenarios that lead to it
  • Say what it is: an IACS Procedural Requirement, binding on member societies - class practice, not an IMO instrument
  • The trigger: a port State detention of a ship the society classed or statutorily certified as a Recognized Organization
  • Scenario - detainable deficiency in an item the RO surveyed and certified, so certificate and condition conflict
  • Scenario - a certificate issued when the ship did not meet the requirement, outside the window, or without proper survey
  • Scenario - detention shortly after a survey by the society
  • Scenario - a major SMS non-conformity where the society also issued the DOC or SMC
  • Scenario - several deficiencies showing systemic failure the last survey should have found
  • Scenario - class status itself wrong: overdue surveys, conditions of class not imposed or not followed up
  • What follows: investigation, report to flag and Company, corrective action, and the detention recorded against the society's published RO performance
  • State the evidence limit openly - MIW holds no IACS text; no wording is quoted
Model written answer

1. The governing instrument at this sitting

Port State control is exercised under the enabling provisions of the conventions themselves - SOLAS I/19, MARPOL article 5 and regulation 11, Load Lines article 21, STCW X, MLC 2006 regulation 5.2.1 - and the procedure is set out in the IMO Assembly's Procedures for Port State Control. The edition in force at this examination is resolution A.1185(33), adopted on 6 December 2023, six months before this sitting. Naming the current resolution rather than an older one, or rather than a regional memorandum of understanding, is worth doing in the first line.

2. "Clear grounds" - the definition

The initial inspection is limited: the PSCO checks the certificates and documents, and forms a general impression of the ship's condition. Clear grounds are what entitle the officer to go further, into a more detailed inspection. They exist where there is evidence that the ship, her equipment or her crew does not correspond substantially with the requirements of the relevant conventions, or that the master or crew is not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to the safety of ships or the prevention of pollution.

Two words in that definition carry the weight. Substantially - a trivial discrepancy is not clear grounds. And evidence - the officer needs something observed or documented, not a suspicion. Clear grounds are the threshold that protects the ship from an unlimited inspection, and a Chief Engineer who understands that will argue about the threshold rather than about the finding.

3. The circumstances that create clear grounds

  • Documents. Certificates absent, expired, invalid, incomplete or apparently falsified; required documents or records not on board; the Oil Record Book with unexplained gaps or entries inconsistent with the machinery fitted.
  • Equipment. Absence of principal equipment or arrangements required by the conventions - or equipment present but visibly not maintained.
  • Condition. The officer's own observations of serious hull, machinery or equipment deterioration, heavy corrosion, poor housekeeping, or conditions that are plainly unsafe.
  • People. The master or crew unable to demonstrate familiarity with essential shipboard operations - an engineer who cannot describe the operation of the oily-water separator or start the emergency generator is himself a clear ground.
  • Operations. Unsafe operations observed, or procedures required by the SMS not being followed.
  • Third-party information. A report from a pilot, a port authority, another Administration, a previous port State, a crew complaint or an incident report.
  • Manning. Evidence that the ship is not manned in accordance with her Minimum Safe Manning Document, or that hours of rest are not being observed.

4. ISM-related deficiencies

These are a distinct class, and their significance is that they do not point at a piece of equipment - they point at the management system that should have kept the equipment right. A single failed pump is a deficiency; a failed pump plus no record that it was ever tested is an ISM deficiency, and it is far more serious because it implies every other item is equally unmanaged. Clear grounds for an ISM-related more detailed inspection include:

  • The Safety Management Certificate or the Company's Document of Compliance absent, invalid, expired, or not matching the ship or the ship type.
  • No SMS documentation on board, or documentation that is uncontrolled, superseded, or in a language the crew cannot read.
  • The master unable to demonstrate his authority and responsibility under the SMS, or unaware of his obligation to conduct the master's review.
  • The Designated Person Ashore unidentified, or no contact details available to the crew.
  • No evidence of internal audits, or audits carried out with no non-conformities ever raised - which is itself evidence the audits are not real.
  • Drills and emergency exercises not carried out or not recorded at the intervals the SMS requires.
  • Maintenance not carried out in accordance with the SMS, or critical equipment not identified as critical.
  • Non-conformities, accidents or hazardous occurrences not reported, investigated or closed out.
  • A major non-conformity found - which is defined by the Code as a deviation posing a serious threat to personnel or the ship, or a serious risk to the environment, requiring immediate corrective action, and includes the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code.

Where the evidence indicates that the SMS is not effectively implemented, the port State may detain the ship and, in a serious case, report the matter to the flag Administration and the Company - and the consequence can reach the Document of Compliance itself, not merely this ship.

5. Five detainable deficiencies, their causes and their prevention

A deficiency is detainable where it is sufficiently serious to warrant the ship being detained until it is rectified - broadly, where the ship is unsafe to proceed to sea, presents an unreasonable threat of harm to the marine environment, or the deficiencies are so numerous that they show the system has failed. The five below are chosen from the engine room, because the question addresses the Chief Engineer.

Detainable deficiencyCausePreventive action as Chief Engineer
Emergency source of electrical power inoperative or unable to take its load - SOLAS II-1/42Batteries never load-tested, only float-charged; the emergency generator started monthly on no load and never on the emergency switchboard; automatic starting and changeover untested; fuel stale or the day tank drained for use elsewhereTest on load and record it; test the automatic start and the blackout changeover, not just the engine; treat the emergency generator as critical equipment in the SMS with its own PMS job and its own spares; log every test with the load carried
Steering gear failure, or the emergency steering arrangements unproven - SOLAS II-1/29Steering gear tests treated as a paper exercise before departure; the alternative power supply and the changeover from bridge to local control never exercised; communications and the compass repeater in the steering flat not checked; no steering gear drill with the crew who would actually be thereCarry out and record the pre-departure tests properly, and hold the emergency steering drill at the interval required; post the changeover instructions in the steering flat; verify the alternative power supply actually starts and takes over; train the ratings who will operate it in the dark
Oily-water separator or 15 ppm alarm inoperative, bypassed or its records inconsistent - MARPOL Annex ICoalescer elements exhausted and not replaced; the 15 ppm monitor uncalibrated or its cell fouled; the automatic stopping device disabled because it kept stopping; bilges accumulating faster than the equipment can process; Oil Record Book entries that do not reconcile with tank soundings - the classic finding that turns a deficiency into a prosecutionNever permit a bypass, in any circumstance, and say so to the whole department; maintain the coalescer and monitor on the PMS with genuine spares; prove the automatic stopping device and record the proof; reconcile the Oil Record Book against soundings yourself before arrival; attack the source of the bilge water rather than the volume - leaking glands and coolers
Fire pumps, fire main or fixed fire-extinguishing system not operational - SOLAS II-2/10The emergency fire pump unprimed, its suction valve seized or its self-priming defective; the fire main corroded or isolated with valves shut for other work; the fixed system's bottle pressures out of date, its release arrangements untested, or its distribution valves inaccessibleRun the emergency fire pump under load and prove two jets at the required points; walk the fire main and lock the isolating valves open; keep the fixed-system survey current and the release station clear, labelled and drilled; include the fire pumps in the pre-arrival self-inspection
Bilge pumping arrangements defective, or the machinery space in an unsafe condition - excessive oil, fuel leaks, defective quick-closing valves or remote stopsBilge wells full and their alarms disabled to stop the nuisance; quick-closing valves for the tanks wired open or their air reservoir empty; remote stops for pumps and fans never tested because testing means stopping machinery; oil-soaked lagging and unrepaired fuel leaks accepted as normalTest the quick-closing valves and remote stops on a scheduled basis, in port, and record them; never disable an alarm - repair the cause; renew oil-soaked lagging and fit spray shields on high-pressure fuel lines; keep the bilges dry and clean as a standing departmental discipline, because a clean engine room ends more inspections than any document

One theme runs through the whole table and it is what the examiner is looking for behind the five examples: almost every detainable engine-room deficiency is a testing and recording failure before it is a mechanical failure. The equipment is usually capable of working. What is missing is the proof, at the moment the officer asks for it, that anyone had confirmed it recently.

6. PR17 - what it is, and the scenarios that lead to it

A Procedural Requirement is an IACS instrument that binds IACS member societies in how they carry out their work; it is not an IMO instrument and it does not carry the force of a convention. Its authority is class practice and the IACS framework, and a candidate should describe it in those terms rather than saying that SOLAS requires it. PR 17 is the Procedural Requirement engaged when a ship classed by, or statutorily certified by, an IACS member society acting as a Recognized Organization is detained by a port State. It puts the society under an obligation to look into what happened, because a detention on an item the society certified is prima facie evidence that the society's own survey was inadequate. The scenarios that lead to it are therefore all variations on one theme:

  • A detention where the detainable deficiency lies in an item the RO surveyed and certified on the flag State's behalf - structure, machinery, LSA, FFA, load line or a MARPOL item - so the certificate and the ship's actual condition are in conflict.
  • A detention on a statutory certificate the RO issued that turns out to have been issued when the ship did not meet the requirement, or issued outside the survey window, or issued without the survey having been properly carried out.
  • A detention shortly after a survey by the society - the closer in time, the stronger the implication that the survey missed what the port State found within weeks.
  • A detention arising from a major non-conformity in the SMS where the society is also the body that issued the Document of Compliance or Safety Management Certificate, so the audit as well as the survey is called into question.
  • A detention that reveals a systemic failure rather than a single defect - several deficiencies of a kind that the last survey should have found - which is what turns an individual report into a review of the surveyor, the office or the society's procedures.
  • A detention where the ship's class status was itself wrong - overdue surveys not recorded, conditions of class not imposed or not followed up, or class continued when it should have been suspended.

What follows is an investigation by the society into whether its survey or audit was deficient, a report to the flag Administration and to the Company, corrective action where the society's own procedures or personnel are at fault, and the recording of the detention against the society - which matters commercially, because port State control regimes publish detention performance by Recognized Organization and a flag State chooses its ROs partly on that record.

State the limit of the source honestly. MIW holds no IACS Procedural Requirement text, so the identity of the number 17 is carried as class-practice reference rather than from a read document, and no IACS wording is reproduced. The scenarios above do not depend on that: they are the circumstances in which a Recognized Organization must respond to a port State detention touching what it certified, and that is what the question is asking for.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

6 + 6 + 4, and the shape of the answer should follow the marks exactly. Limb (a) has two defined terms and both must be defined, not just the famous one - a candidate who writes brilliantly on clear grounds and adds one line on ISM deficiencies loses half of six marks. Limb (b) asks for three things about five items - the deficiency, its cause, and the preventive action - which is fifteen pieces of information for six marks, so a table is not a stylistic preference here, it is the only way to fit them. Limb (c) is four marks and asks for scenarios, plural: a list of circumstances, not an essay.

Name the resolution

Open with A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023. Most candidates write "as per PSC procedures" or name a regional MOU. Naming the Assembly resolution, and the right edition of it, takes one line and immediately separates the answer. Do not name a 34th Assembly resolution on this paper - the 34th Assembly sat in December 2025, eighteen months after this examination.

The definition, in the words that matter

Clear grounds = evidence that the ship, her equipment or her crew does not correspond substantially with the requirements of the conventions, or that the master or crew is not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to safety or pollution prevention. Learn those three phrases. "Substantially" is what keeps a trivial finding from opening the ship up; "evidence" is what distinguishes clear grounds from suspicion; and the familiarity limb is the one candidates forget, even though an engineer who cannot operate his own separator is the commonest clear ground of all.

Initial, more detailed, expanded - keep the three apart

InspectionWhat it coversWhat triggers it
InitialCertificates and documents; the officer's general impression of conditionAny visit - no grounds needed
More detailedAn in-depth examination of the areas where the concern liesClear grounds
ExpandedA wider prescribed scope, typically on older or higher-risk ships including tankersThe ship's type, age and risk profile, not necessarily any deficiency

The question asks only about the second, but knowing that the third exists and is triggered differently is a common oral follow-up on a tanker paper.

Why the ISM half of limb (a) is the harder half

An equipment deficiency says one thing is wrong. An ISM deficiency says the system that should find things being wrong is itself not working - so nothing else on board can be relied on either. That is why a major non-conformity can detain a ship whose machinery is in reasonable order, and why the consequence can reach the Company's Document of Compliance and the rest of the fleet, not just this ship. Say that explicitly; it is the difference between listing ISM findings and understanding them.

The theme that carries limb (b)

Almost every detainable engine-room deficiency is a testing and recording failure before it is a mechanical failure. The emergency generator usually runs; what is missing is proof that anyone ran it on load. The quick-closing valves usually work; what is missing is a record of the last test. Write that sentence into the answer, because it converts five separate examples into one argument, and it is what a Chief Engineer is supposed to understand that a junior officer does not.

The one deficiency that is different

The oily-water separator is not like the other four. A bypass is not a maintenance failure - it is a deliberate act, it is a criminal offence in most jurisdictions, the Oil Record Book becomes a falsified document, and the consequences fall personally on the Chief Engineer, not on the company. Say so. An answer that lists "OWS not working" alongside "fire pump not working" as though they were the same kind of problem has missed the most important distinction in the limb.

PR17 - how to write four marks safely

Three moves. First, say what kind of thing it is: an IACS Procedural Requirement, binding on member societies, class practice and not an IMO instrument - so never write "SOLAS requires PR17". Second, give the trigger: a port State detention of a ship the society classed or statutorily certified as a Recognized Organization. Third, list the scenarios, which are all variants of the same conflict - the certificate said one thing and the port State found another, on an item the society was responsible for. Then note what follows: investigation, report to flag and Company, corrective action, and the detention recorded against the society's published performance.

Common mistakes

  • Defining clear grounds and never defining ISM-related deficiencies. Half of limb (a).
  • Listing five deficiencies without causes or preventive actions, when the stem asks for all three.
  • Giving deck deficiencies. The stem says "as Chief Engineer" - answer from the engine room.
  • Treating the OWS bypass as an ordinary maintenance deficiency.
  • Writing PR17 as though it were an IMO or SOLAS requirement.
  • Naming a regional memorandum of understanding as the source of the definition instead of the Assembly resolution.
  • Confusing a more detailed inspection with an expanded inspection.

Examiner traps

  • "Which resolution?" A.1185(33) at this sitting. Not a 34th Assembly resolution - that Assembly sat in December 2025.
  • "Are clear grounds needed for the initial inspection?" No. They are needed to go beyond it.
  • "Is an unfamiliar crew a clear ground?" Yes - expressly, and it is the limb candidates omit.
  • "What makes a deficiency detainable?" That the ship is unsafe to proceed to sea, or presents an unreasonable threat of harm to the environment - or that the deficiencies are so numerous they show a systemic failure.
  • "Can a ship be detained on ISM alone?" Yes, on a major non-conformity, even with the machinery in fair order.
  • "Who issues PR17 and to whom?" The Recognized Organization, following a detention, reporting to the flag Administration and the Company - not the port State, and not the flag.
  • "Why does the society care?" Because detention performance by RO is published, and flag States choose their ROs on that record.

Likely oral follow-up

  • A PSCO asks you to start the emergency generator and put it on load. Talk me through it.
  • Show me your Oil Record Book and explain the last three entries.
  • You disagree with the detention. What can you do about it?
  • What is a Condition of Class and who imposes it?
  • Your ship is detained on an item your class surveyor passed last month. What happens next, and to whom?
  • How would your internal ISM audit have caught this before the port State did?

Memory framework

Two definitions, five failures, one conflict. Limb (a) is two definitions - clear grounds (evidence, substantially, familiarity) and ISM deficiencies (the system, not the item). Limb (b) is five failures and one sentence: tested and recorded, or it is a deficiency. Limb (c) is one conflict: the certificate says compliant, the port State found otherwise, and the RO issued the certificate. Every PR17 scenario is that sentence with a different item in it.

Regulation and source map

  • A.1185(33) - Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023. The operative edition at this sitting.
  • A.1186(33) - Survey Guidelines under the HSSC, 2023.
  • SOLAS I/19; MARPOL article 5 and regulation 11; Load Lines article 21; STCW chapter X; MLC 2006 regulation 5.2.1 - the enabling provisions for port State control.
  • ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92), in force 1 January 2015 - definitions of non-conformity and major non-conformity, and the SMS elements a PSCO tests.
  • SOLAS II-1/29 steering gear; II-1/42 emergency source of electrical power; II-2/10 fire pumps and fixed systems.
  • MARPOL Annex I regulations 14 and 15 - oil filtering equipment, the 15 ppm limit and the Oil Record Book.
  • IACS Procedural Requirement 17 - class practice and the IACS framework, NOT an IMO instrument. Text not held by MIW.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

Limbs (a) and (b) rest on held, identity-verified evidence. A.1185(33) is held by the MIW corpus with its identity verified against IMO A.33 records, and it was the operative PSC procedure at this sitting, having been adopted six months earlier. The later revision from the 34th Assembly session of December 2025 is eighteen months after this examination and is not relied on.

Limb (c) is the honest weak point and is flagged rather than disguised. MIW holds no IACS Procedural Requirement text - the corpus records IACS acquisition as deferred - so the identity of PR 17 by number is carried as class-practice reference rather than from a read document, and no IACS wording is reproduced anywhere. The scenarios given do not depend on the number-to-title mapping: they are the circumstances in which a Recognized Organization must respond to a port State detention involving statutory items it certified, which is what the question asks for. If you intend to name the number in an answer, check it against the current IACS Procedural Requirement series first.

No numeric steering-gear threshold is asserted anywhere in this answer. The corpus SOLAS log records a specific modelling defect conflating the 120 mm main and 230 mm auxiliary steering gear thresholds, and the safe response to a recorded defect of that kind is to state the requirement in function and omit the number.

No regional memorandum of understanding is named and no detention statistic, targeting factor or ship-risk-profile band is asserted. MIW holds no MOU documentation. The domestic statute at this sitting is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, under which Indian port State control is exercised; the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 is not law at this sitting and appears nowhere in this answer.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) The governing instrument at this sitting
  2. __________(a) "Clear grounds" - the definition
  3. __________(a) The circumstances that create clear grounds
  4. __________(a) ISM-related deficiencies
  5. __________(b) Five detainable deficiencies, their causes and their prevention
  6. __________(c) PR17 - what it is, and the scenarios that lead to it
Flashcards 8

1 Name A.1185(33). 2 Define clear grounds. 3 The circumstances that create them. 4 ISM-related deficiencies as a distinct class. 5 Five detainable deficiencies with cause and prevention, as a table. 6 PR17 - what it is, the scenarios, and the evidence limit. Sections 1-4 are limb (a), 5 is limb (b), 6 is limb (c).

Limb (a) names two terms and limb (b) asks three things about five items. Only a sectioned and tabulated answer makes all of it visible in the marks available.

Evidence that the ship, her equipment or her crew does not correspond substantially with the requirements of the relevant conventions, or that the master or crew is not familiar with essential shipboard procedures relating to the safety of ships or the prevention of pollution. They are what entitles the PSCO to move from the initial inspection to a more detailed inspection.

'Substantially' excludes the trivial and 'evidence' excludes suspicion. The crew-familiarity limb is the one candidates omit, and it is the commonest clear ground in practice.

An equipment deficiency says one thing is wrong. An ISM deficiency says the system that should find things being wrong is not working - so nothing else on board can be relied on either. That is why a major non-conformity can detain a ship whose machinery is in reasonable order, and why the consequence can reach the Company's Document of Compliance and the rest of the fleet rather than just this ship.

This is the sentence that turns a list of ISM findings into an understanding of why the class exists.

That it is sufficiently serious to warrant detention until rectified - broadly, the ship is unsafe to proceed to sea, she presents an unreasonable threat of harm to the marine environment, or the deficiencies are so numerous that they demonstrate the management system has failed. The last of the three is the one candidates forget, and it is how a ship is detained on an accumulation of individually minor findings.

Stating the test before the five examples is what makes the examples look chosen rather than recalled.

Emergency source of electrical power inoperative or never proven on load; steering gear or emergency steering defective or untested; oily-water separator or 15 ppm equipment inoperative, bypassed, or with an Oil Record Book that does not reconcile; fire pumps, fire main or fixed fire-extinguishing system not operational; bilge pumping arrangements, quick-closing valves or remote stops defective, with an unsafe machinery-space condition. The theme: every one is a testing and recording failure before it is a mechanical failure.

The unifying sentence is what a Chief Engineer is expected to supply and a junior officer is not.

A bypass is not a maintenance failure - it is a deliberate act. It is a criminal offence in most jurisdictions, it makes the Oil Record Book a falsified document, and the consequences fall personally on the Chief Engineer rather than on the company. Never permit one, in any circumstance.

An answer that lists 'OWS not working' beside 'fire pump not working' as the same kind of problem has missed the most important distinction in the limb.

An IACS Procedural Requirement - an instrument binding on IACS member societies in how they carry out their work. It is class practice and the IACS framework, not an IMO instrument, so never say SOLAS requires it. It is engaged when a port State detains a ship that an IACS member society has classed or statutorily certified as a Recognized Organization, because a detention on an item the society certified is evidence that the society's own survey was inadequate.

Saying what kind of instrument it is comes before saying what it does, and it is where most of the four marks are lost.

All variants of one conflict - the certificate said compliant and the port State found otherwise, on something the society was responsible for: a detainable deficiency in an item the RO surveyed and certified; a certificate issued when the ship did not comply; a detention shortly after a survey; a major SMS non-conformity where the society issued the DOC or SMC; or the class status itself wrong. What follows: investigation, report to flag and Company, corrective action, and the detention recorded against the society's published RO performance.

The scenarios are the four marks. Grouping them round one conflict makes them reconstructable rather than memorised.

Quick revision
15-second recall
A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023, is the operative edition. Clear grounds = evidence that ship, equipment or crew does not correspond substantially with the conventions, or that master or crew are not familiar with essential shipboard procedures for safety or pollution prevention - they unlock the more detailed inspection. ISM-related deficiencies point at the management system, not at equipment: no SMS documentation, DOC or SMC invalid, master unable to demonstrate his authority, DPA unknown, no internal audits, drills not recorded, maintenance not per the SMS, non-conformities not closed - and a major non-conformity can detain the ship alone and reach the Company's DOC. Five detainable engine-room deficiencies: emergency generator not proven on load, steering gear / emergency steering untested, OWS or 15 ppm inoperative or bypassed with the ORB not reconciling, fire pumps or fixed system not operational, bilge arrangements, quick-closing valves and remote stops defective - all of them testing and recording failures before they are mechanical ones. PR17 is an IACS Procedural Requirement - class practice, not IMO - engaged when a port State detains a ship the society classed or certified as an RO, on an item the society was responsible for.

Understand this first

There is a temperature above which sulphuric acid stays a vapour and passes harmlessly out of the cylinder, and below which it condenses as liquid acid on the liner. That temperature is the acid dew point, and the entire question is about the margin between it and the liner wall. For most of the history of the low-speed engine that margin was comfortable and nobody thought about it. Then two things happened at once, for good reasons that had nothing to do with corrosion. Engines got longer in the stroke, slower, de-rated, and were run at part load for weeks - all of which puts less heat into the liner, so the wall got cooler. And firing pressures went up for efficiency, which raises the partial pressures of water vapour and sulphur trioxide in the cylinder and so raises the dew point itself. The margin was squeezed from both ends and it closed. Once you see it as a margin, the three lettered sections stop being a list of remedies and become the only three things you can possibly do: make the wall hotter (A), change the wall so it holds its heat and holds its oil (B), or accept that acid will land and neutralise it (C). And the last insight, which most candidates never reach: the neutralising is a balance, not a maximum, because the calcium that carries the alkalinity turns into abrasive ash if there is no acid for it to react with.

Knowledge map
Cold Corrosion of Cylinder Liners in Super-Long-Stroke Engines
  1. 1What cold corrosion is
    • Sulphur in the fuel burns to SO2, part oxidises to SO3
    • SO3 plus combustion water vapour gives sulphuric acid vapour
    • It condenses on any surface below the acid dew point - far above the water dew point
  2. 2Why the new generation of engines made it worse
    • Longer stroke, lower speed, bigger bore-to-stroke - more surface to cool, greater spread along the stroke
    • De-rating and habitual part load - less heat in, so lower wall temperature
    • Higher maximum combustion pressure - raises partial pressures and so raises the dew point
  3. 3How it presents, and how to tell it from other wear
    • Location - upper liner, ring-swept region near top dead centre
    • Clover-leafing between the lubricator quills - a neutralisation signature, not a mechanical one
    • Matt, etched, micro-pitted surface; graphite standing proud in severe cases
  4. A
    1. 4Limb A - increased jacket cooling water temperature
      • Raised jacket water outlet set point - more of the stroke above the dew point
      • Load-dependent control - raise temperature as load FALLS; a fixed high setting is wrong at full load
      • Separate or staged circuits so the upper liner is controlled independently of the cover
  5. B
    1. 5Limb B - new cylinder liner designs
      • Insulating tubes or inserts in the cooling bores - raise upper wall temperature locally
      • Re-profiled bore cooling - fewer, shallower or shorter bores in the upper region
      • Insulating collar at the top of the liner
  6. C
    1. 6Limb C - new cylinder lubricators
      • Electronic timed pulse injection at a chosen crank angle - timing and quantity become independent variables, per cylinder
      • Oil delivered just before the ring pack sweeps up, and distributed by the rings
      • Feed rate proportional to load and to the sulphur of the fuel actually burned, adjusted at every fuel change
  7. 7The other failure mode - too much treatment
    • Unconsumed calcium detergent forms hard ash deposits on crown, top land and ring grooves
    • Those deposits are abrasive and polish the honed surface, destroying oil retention
    • Ring sticking and blow-by follow, raising liner temperature unevenly
  8. 8Monitoring - what closes the loop
    • Scrape-down analysis: iron AND residual base number, trended together - the key diagnostic
    • Iron high, BN near zero - corrosion, under-dosed. Iron high, BN high - not corrosion, and more oil will worsen it
    • Liner wall temperature monitoring where fitted
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. What cold corrosion is
  2. Why the new generation of engines made it worse
  3. How it presents, and how to tell it from other wear
A
  1. Limb A - increased jacket cooling water temperature
B
  1. Limb B - new cylinder liner designs
C
  1. Limb C - new cylinder lubricators
  1. The other failure mode - too much treatment
  2. Monitoring - what closes the loop

Memory cue. The margin closed from both ends; three ways to reopen it. CAUSE: wall temperature DOWN while the acid dew point goes UP (2). MITIGATION: HOTTER surface (4), BETTER surface (5), NEUTRALISED surface (6). BALANCE: too much treatment polishes what too little corrodes (7). DIAGNOSTIC: iron and residual BN, together (8).

Remember8 route headings
Cover50 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 50 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What cold corrosion is
  • Sulphur in the fuel burns to SO2, part oxidises to SO3
  • SO3 plus combustion water vapour gives sulphuric acid vapour
  • It condenses on any surface below the acid dew point - far above the water dew point
  • Liquid acid attacks the iron wherever the oil's alkaline reserve does not neutralise it
  • Say the name is a misnomer - the liner is below the dew point, not cold
2. Why the new generation of engines made it worse
  • Longer stroke, lower speed, bigger bore-to-stroke - more surface to cool, greater spread along the stroke
  • De-rating and habitual part load - less heat in, so lower wall temperature
  • Higher maximum combustion pressure - raises partial pressures and so raises the dew point
  • EGR and other NOx measures - recirculate water vapour and acid species into the charge
  • More effective cooling - removes heat from exactly the region that needs to stay hot
  • The one-sentence answer: wall temperature down, dew point up, margin squeezed from both ends
3. How it presents, and how to tell it from other wear
  • Location - upper liner, ring-swept region near top dead centre
  • Clover-leafing between the lubricator quills - a neutralisation signature, not a mechanical one
  • Matt, etched, micro-pitted surface; graphite standing proud in severe cases
  • Wear rate rising out of proportion to hours, often after a change of service pattern
  • Scrape-down: iron up, residual base number down
  • Contrast: abrasive wear wants filtration, scuffing wants film, corrosion wants heat and alkalinity
4. Limb A - increased jacket cooling water temperature
  • Raised jacket water outlet set point - more of the stroke above the dew point
  • Load-dependent control - raise temperature as load FALLS; a fixed high setting is wrong at full load
  • Separate or staged circuits so the upper liner is controlled independently of the cover
  • Reduced upper-liner cooling by design - the same measure from the other end (see limb B)
  • Liner wall temperature sensors so control acts on the variable that matters
  • State the limits: boiling and steam pockets, thermal stress, system pressure and venting, cooler duty, less forgiving water treatment
5. Limb B - new cylinder liner designs
  • Insulating tubes or inserts in the cooling bores - raise upper wall temperature locally
  • Re-profiled bore cooling - fewer, shallower or shorter bores in the upper region
  • Insulating collar at the top of the liner
  • Anti-polishing ring (flame ring) - scrapes carbon off the piston crown top land, which would otherwise polish the liner and wipe the film
  • Controlled surface finish - honing pattern, wave-cut or profiled grooves for oil retention and circumferential distribution
  • Higher-alloy grey cast irons and surface treatments
  • Sensor pockets for liner wall temperature; repositioned and improved lubricator quills
  • Unify them: keep the top of the liner hot and keep the oil on it
6. Limb C - new cylinder lubricators
  • Electronic timed pulse injection at a chosen crank angle - timing and quantity become independent variables, per cylinder
  • Oil delivered just before the ring pack sweeps up, and distributed by the rings
  • Feed rate proportional to load and to the sulphur of the fuel actually burned, adjusted at every fuel change
  • A minimum feed rate always respected for film maintenance
  • Load-change dependent dosage during manoeuvring, load changes and running in
  • Higher base number oils where the service is corrosion prone - more reserve per unit of oil
  • Since the 0.50 per cent limit of 1 January 2020, BN is chosen against fuel sulphur AND engine duty together, decided by scrape-down analysis rather than a table
7. The other failure mode - too much treatment
  • Unconsumed calcium detergent forms hard ash deposits on crown, top land and ring grooves
  • Those deposits are abrasive and polish the honed surface, destroying oil retention
  • Ring sticking and blow-by follow, raising liner temperature unevenly
  • And the oil is wasted, at real cost
  • Under-treatment corrodes, over-treatment polishes - both end as high wear, which is why wear rate alone cannot diagnose
8. Monitoring - what closes the loop
  • Scrape-down analysis: iron AND residual base number, trended together - the key diagnostic
  • Iron high, BN near zero - corrosion, under-dosed. Iron high, BN high - not corrosion, and more oil will worsen it
  • Liner wall temperature monitoring where fitted
  • Scavenge port inspections - surface condition, film, ring condition, crown and top land deposits
  • Periodic wear measurement at fixed reference positions, trended not absolute
  • Feed rate records read against fuel sulphur, load history and analysis results
  • Every actual number comes from the builder's service letters and the oil supplier for that installation - carry none between ships
Model written answer

1. What cold corrosion is

Cold corrosion is sulphuric acid attack on the cylinder liner running surface, and it happens for one reason: part of the liner is colder than the acid can stay a vapour. Sulphur in the fuel burns to sulphur dioxide and, in part, oxidises further to sulphur trioxide. The sulphur trioxide combines with the water vapour that combustion also produces, and forms sulphuric acid. Acid vapour condenses on any surface below its dew point - and the sulphuric acid dew point is far above the water dew point, high enough to sit within the normal range of liner wall temperatures. Where the liquid acid lands and is not neutralised by the alkaline reserve in the cylinder oil, it attacks the iron.

The name is a slight misnomer and it is worth saying so: the liner is not cold in any absolute sense. It is below the acid dew point at that point on the stroke, which is a different and much higher threshold.

2. Why the new generation of engines made it worse

The stem asserts a causal chain and the answer should trace it rather than assume it. Two things happened at once, and they push in the same direction.

ChangeWhy it was madeEffect on cold corrosion
Longer stroke, lower speed, larger bore-to-stroke ratioPropulsive efficiency - a larger, slower propeller. Driven by EEDI and fuel costA longer, larger liner has more surface area to cool and a greater temperature spread along the stroke, so the upper liner can sit below the dew point over more of the cycle
De-rating and habitual part-load runningSlow steaming for fuel economy and emissionsLess heat into the liner, so lower wall temperature - directly below the dew point
Higher maximum combustion pressureThermal efficiency and specific fuel consumptionRaises the partial pressures of water vapour and sulphur trioxide, and so raises the acid dew point itself
Exhaust gas recirculation and other NOx measuresMARPOL Annex VI Tier III complianceRecirculated gas carries water vapour and acid species back into the charge, increasing the acid available to condense
More effective cooling systemsComponent life and thermal loadingRemoves heat efficiently from exactly the region that most needs to stay hot

So the wall temperature came down while the dew point went up. The margin between them - which is all that ever protected the liner - was squeezed from both ends. That is the whole explanation, and stating it in one sentence is what distinguishes an answer that understands the problem from one that lists remedies.

3. How it presents, and how to tell it from other wear

  • Location. Attack concentrated in the upper part of the liner, in the region swept by the piston rings near top dead centre, where the wall is coolest relative to the acid dew point and the oil film is most stressed.
  • Pattern. The classic clover-leafing - lobed zones of heavy wear between the lubricator quills, where oil distribution is thinnest and the acid is therefore least neutralised. Clover-leafing is the signature of a neutralisation failure, not of a mechanical one.
  • Surface appearance. A dull, matt, etched or micro-pitted surface rather than the polished or scored appearance of adhesive or abrasive wear. In severe cases the graphite flakes stand proud as the iron matrix is dissolved around them.
  • Rate. Wear rates rising sharply and out of proportion to running hours, often after a change of service pattern rather than after any mechanical event.
  • Corroborating evidence. Rising iron in the scrape-down oil with the residual base number falling to little or nothing - the direct measurement that the alkaline reserve is being consumed faster than it is being supplied. That is the link to used-oil analysis.

The distinction matters because the remedies are opposite. Abrasive wear is answered by better filtration and cleaner air and fuel; adhesive wear or scuffing by more oil and a better film; cold corrosion by a hotter surface and more alkalinity. Treating corrosion as scuffing by simply increasing the feed rate of an unsuitable oil wastes oil and fixes nothing.

4. Limb A - increased jacket cooling water temperature

The most direct measure: if the liner is below the dew point, raise the liner. Jacket cooling water outlet temperatures on modern low-speed engines are held significantly higher than was once normal, and the measures used to achieve and control that are:

  • A raised jacket water outlet temperature set point, so the whole liner runs hotter and more of the stroke sits above the acid dew point.
  • Load-dependent liner temperature control - the crucial refinement. A fixed high temperature is wrong at full load, where thermal loading and the risk of local boiling in the cooling passages become the limit. So the control system raises the jacket temperature as load falls, holding the liner wall in its target band across the whole operating range. This is what makes habitual slow steaming survivable.
  • Separate or staged cooling circuits, so the upper liner - the part that matters - can be controlled independently of the cylinder cover and the rest of the jacket, rather than all of it being cooled to suit the hottest component.
  • Reduced cooling of the upper liner by design, achieved through the bore-cooling geometry discussed under limb B; temperature control and liner design are the same measure approached from two ends.
  • Liner wall temperature monitoring by embedded sensors, so the control acts on the temperature that matters rather than on the coolant temperature that is merely convenient to measure.

The limits must be stated, because an examiner is testing whether the candidate knows that this measure has a ceiling. Raising jacket water temperature reduces the margin to boiling and steam pocket formation in the cooling passages, which would cause local overheating and thermal cracking; it increases thermal stress in the liner and cover; it demands attention to system pressure and to venting; and it shifts load onto the freshwater cooler and the central cooling system. The higher-temperature circuit also makes water treatment and inhibitor control less forgiving. It is a controlled measure, not a free one - and none of it works without correct venting, correct treatment and a control system that is actually in service rather than in manual.

5. Limb B - new cylinder liner designs

The liner itself was redesigned to hold heat where it is needed and to keep the oil film where it is put.

Design featureWhat it does
Insulating tubes or inserts in the cooling boresReduce heat transfer from the upper liner locally, raising wall temperature in the top region without changing the coolant temperature - and without altering the liner's structural design
Re-profiled bore cooling - fewer, shallower or shorter bores in the upper regionThe same result by geometry: cooling is concentrated where thermal load demands it and withdrawn from where corrosion demands heat
Insulating collar or jacket at the top of the linerRaises the temperature of the very top of the stroke, the region most exposed to condensation
Anti-polishing ring (flame ring) at the top of the linerA slightly reduced-bore ring that scrapes carbon deposits from the piston crown top land. Those deposits otherwise polish the honed surface, destroy oil retention and wipe the oil film off the upper liner - which turns a lubrication problem into a corrosion problem. It also confines combustion gas above the ring pack
Controlled surface finish - honing pattern, wave-cut or profiled groovesRetains oil in the surface so a film survives at top dead centre where hydrodynamic support has collapsed, and helps distribute oil circumferentially between the quills, which is the direct attack on clover-leafing
Higher-alloy grey cast irons and surface treatmentsImprove resistance to acid attack and to scuffing; the graphite structure is chosen for oil retention as well as for strength
Liner wall temperature sensor pocketsMake the controlling variable measurable, which is what allows load-dependent temperature control to be closed-loop rather than open-loop
Repositioned and increased lubricator quills, and improved quill designDeliver oil into the top of the stroke and distribute it circumferentially, directly addressing the between-quill zones where clover-leafing forms

The unifying idea is worth stating: these are not eight separate inventions. They are all attempts to keep the top of the liner hot and keep the oil on it, and each of them fails if the others are neglected.

6. Limb C - new cylinder lubricators

The third route accepts that some acid will condense and concentrates on neutralising it where and when it lands. This is where the largest change has occurred, and it has three parts.

  • Electronic, timed, pulse injection. The mechanical lubricator driven from the engine and delivering a continuous or crank-angle-fixed dose has been replaced by an electronically controlled system that injects a measured pulse at a chosen crank angle, so the oil arrives on the liner immediately before the ring pack sweeps it upward and is distributed by the rings themselves. Timing and quantity become independent variables, controllable per cylinder.
  • Feed rate proportional to load AND to fuel sulphur. The governing principle is that the alkalinity delivered must match the acid produced. Acid production is proportional to fuel burned and to the sulphur content of the fuel actually being burned, so the feed rate is set against the sulphur of the fuel in service and adjusted whenever the fuel changes - which now happens routinely on entering and leaving an emission control area. A minimum feed rate is always respected for film maintenance, because below it the oil stops lubricating however alkaline it is.
  • Load-change dependent dosage. A temporary increase in feed rate during and after load changes, manoeuvring, and running in, when the ring pack and liner temperatures are unsettled and the film is most at risk.
  • Higher base number cylinder oils. Where the service is cold-corrosion prone, an oil with a greater alkaline reserve delivers more neutralising capacity per unit of oil, so acid can be neutralised without flooding the cylinder with oil. Oils are now selected for the combination of fuel sulphur and engine duty, not for the fuel alone.

The relationship between fuel sulphur and cylinder oil changed direction after the global sulphur limit of 0.50 per cent m/m outside emission control areas took effect on 1 January 2020. Lower sulphur means less acid, and so in principle a lower base number - but on a cold-corrosion-prone engine the acid that does form still condenses, and an oil chosen only for the low sulphur can leave the liner with too little reserve. That is why the modern rule is that base number is selected against fuel sulphur and engine duty together, and why scrape-down analysis rather than a table decides it.

7. The other failure mode - too much treatment

This is where a good answer separates itself, because the examiner's word is "mitigation" and every mitigation here has a cost. Over-lubricating, or using an oil more alkaline than the service needs, is not a safe error:

  • The calcium-based detergent additives that carry the alkalinity form hard ash and deposits when their reserve is not consumed by acid. Those deposits build on the piston crown, the top land and the ring grooves.
  • Those deposits are themselves abrasive, and they polish the honed liner surface, destroying the oil retention the surface finish was designed to give - so an over-alkaline oil can produce bore polishing and scuffing while trying to prevent corrosion.
  • Deposits cause ring sticking and blow-by, which raises liner temperature unevenly and damages the film.
  • And the oil is wasted, at significant cost, on an engine whose cylinder oil consumption is a major operating expense.

So cold corrosion control is a balance, not a maximum: enough alkalinity to neutralise the acid actually formed, delivered where it is needed, and no more. Under-treatment corrodes the liner; over-treatment polishes it. Both end as excessive wear, which is why the wear rate alone does not tell you which mistake you are making.

8. Monitoring - what closes the loop

None of the three measures can be set correctly without measurement, and this is the part that belongs to the Chief Engineer rather than to the designer:

  • Scrape-down oil analysis - iron content, which measures the wear actually occurring, together with the residual base number, which measures whether the alkaline reserve is being over-supplied or exhausted. Iron high and residual BN near zero means corrosion and under-dosing; iron high with residual BN high means the problem is not corrosion at all and more oil will make it worse. This single pair of numbers, trended, is the most useful diagnostic on the subject.
  • Liner wall temperature monitoring, where fitted - the direct measure of whether the temperature-control strategy is achieving its target.
  • Scavenge port inspections - liner surface condition, the presence and evenness of the oil film, ring condition and free movement, and the deposit condition of the piston crown and top land.
  • Periodic liner wear measurement at the same reference positions, trended over time rather than judged against any absolute figure.
  • Cylinder oil feed rate records read alongside fuel sulphur, load history and the analysis results, so the feed rate is a controlled variable and not a setting somebody adjusted once.

The engine builder's own service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation govern every actual number. No feed rate, base number, jacket temperature or wear limit should be carried between installations, and none is quoted here for exactly that reason.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks, undivided, with three named sub-headings. That structure is generous and dangerous at the same time: generous because the examiner has told you three of the sections to write, dangerous because a candidate who writes only those three has not answered the stem, which asks for the causes and mitigation measures, stressing on the following. "Stressing on" means those three carry the weight - not that they are the whole answer. So the shape is: cause first, then the three named sections in the printed order, then the balance and the monitoring that make them work. Roughly a fifth on cause, half across the three named limbs, and the rest on the closing analysis.

The sentence the whole question turns on

The wall temperature came down while the acid dew point went up. Everything in the question is contained in that. Longer stroke and part-load running cool the liner; higher firing pressure raises the pressure of water vapour and sulphur trioxide and so raises the temperature at which the acid condenses. The margin that protected the liner was squeezed from both ends. Write that early and every mitigation afterwards reads as a deliberate response rather than a memorised list.

Get the chemistry right in one line

Sulphur burns to SO2; part oxidises to SO3; SO3 plus water vapour gives H2SO4; it condenses below the acid dew point, which is much higher than the water dew point and rises with both sulphur content and cylinder pressure. Candidates who write "water condenses and causes corrosion" have described a different and far less aggressive mechanism, and lose the foundation of the answer.

The name is misleading - say so

The liner is not cold. It is below the acid dew point at that point on the stroke, which is a much higher threshold than "cold" suggests. A single sentence making that point signals that you understand the mechanism rather than the label.

Recognising it - the three signatures

  • Upper liner, in the ring-swept region near top dead centre.
  • Clover-leafing - lobed heavy wear between the lubricator quills, where the oil is thinnest and least neutralising. This is a neutralisation signature, not a mechanical one.
  • Matt, etched or micro-pitted surface - not the polish of bore polishing and not the scoring of scuffing. In severe cases graphite flakes stand proud as the iron dissolves around them.

And the corroborating measurement: iron up in the scrape-down oil with residual base number near zero.

The three limbs are one control problem

LimbThe strategyThe limit on it
A - jacket water temperatureRaise the surface above the dew pointBoiling and steam pockets in the cooling passages; thermal stress; less forgiving water treatment
B - liner designChange the surface - hold heat locally, keep the oil on itFixed at build or at renewal; not adjustable in service
C - lubricationNeutralise what lands, where and when it landsOver-treatment causes ash deposits, bore polishing and ring sticking

Presenting them as three attacks on one problem - hotter surface, better surface, neutralised surface - is worth more than treating them as three unrelated topics, and it is exactly what "discuss the causes and mitigation measures" invites.

The part most candidates miss: over-treatment

More cylinder oil and higher base number are not free. The calcium detergent that carries the alkalinity forms hard ash deposits if its reserve is not consumed by acid; those deposits are abrasive, they polish the honed surface and destroy oil retention, and they cause ring sticking and blow-by. So an over-alkaline oil can produce the very wear it was chosen to prevent. Under-treatment corrodes, over-treatment polishes, and both show up as high wear - which is why the wear rate alone cannot tell you which mistake you have made. Writing this paragraph is what turns a good answer into a Class I answer.

The one pair of numbers that diagnoses it

Iron and residual base number in the scrape-down oil, trended together. Iron high with residual BN near zero: corrosion, under-dosed - raise the feed rate or the base number. Iron high with residual BN still high: it is not corrosion, and more oil will make it worse - look for abrasive ingress, scuffing or a mechanical cause. That single diagnostic is the practical link between this question and Q9 on the same paper.

Quote no numbers

Jacket water temperatures, liner wall temperatures, acid dew points, feed rates in grammes per kilowatt-hour, base numbers and wear rates in millimetres per thousand hours are all engine, oil, fuel and service specific. The engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation govern them. In an examination, describing the control principle and saying that the value comes from the builder scores better than a remembered figure that may belong to a different engine - and in service, a figure carried between installations is a hazard.

Common mistakes

  • Describing water condensation rather than sulphuric acid condensation. Different mechanism, far less aggressive.
  • Saying the liner is "too cold" without saying below what. The acid dew point is the reference, and it moves.
  • Missing that higher firing pressure raises the dew point, and so treating the problem as purely a cooling issue.
  • Writing only the three lettered sections and never answering "discuss the causes".
  • Treating cold corrosion as a lubrication problem alone, and answering the whole question under limb C.
  • Recommending maximum feed rate and maximum base number as though over-treatment had no cost.
  • Naming engine builders, systems, oils and figures - which invites a follow-up you cannot support.
  • Ignoring monitoring, and so leaving no way to know whether any of the measures worked.

Examiner traps

  • "Why is it called cold corrosion when the liner is at a few hundred degrees?" Because it is below the acid dew point locally, not cold absolutely.
  • "Does the 0.50 per cent sulphur cap solve it?" No. Less sulphur means less acid, but the acid that forms still condenses on a cool surface - and an oil chosen only for low sulphur can leave too little reserve.
  • "What is clover-leafing and why between the quills?" Lobed heavy wear where oil distribution is thinnest, so acid is least neutralised. It is a lubrication-distribution signature.
  • "What does the anti-polishing ring actually do?" Scrapes carbon deposits off the piston crown top land, so they cannot polish the liner and wipe the oil film.
  • "Why raise jacket water temperature as load falls rather than keeping it high always?" Because at full load the limit is thermal loading and boiling in the cooling passages. It is load-dependent control, not a fixed high setting.
  • "You have high wear and high iron in the scrape-down. Increase the feed rate?" Only if the residual base number is low. If it is high, the cause is not corrosion and more oil will make it worse.
  • "Can over-lubrication damage the engine?" Yes - ash deposits, bore polishing, ring sticking and blow-by.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Take me through a scavenge port inspection and tell me what you would look for.
  • What is in your scrape-down oil analysis report and how do you act on it?
  • You are entering an emission control area and changing to low-sulphur fuel. What happens to your cylinder lubrication?
  • Your jacket water outlet temperature control has failed to manual. What are the risks and what do you do?
  • How would you measure liner wear, and what would you compare it against?
  • Where would you expect the wear to be worst, and why there?

Memory framework

The margin closed from both ends - then three ways to reopen it. Cause: wall temperature down (long stroke, part load, better cooling), dew point up (high firing pressure, EGR). Mitigation: HOTTER surface (limb A - jacket water, load-dependent), BETTER surface (limb B - insulation, bore cooling, anti-polishing ring, honing), NEUTRALISED surface (limb C - timed injection, feed rate against sulphur, higher BN). Then the balance: too much treatment polishes what too little corrodes. And the diagnostic: iron and residual BN, together.

Regulation and source map

  • NO IMO OR STATUTORY INSTRUMENT PRESCRIBES A CYLINDER LUBRICATION REGIME. This is engine builder practice and no instrument has been named to give the answer false authority.
  • MARPOL Annex VI - the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil used outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020, and the Tier III NOx requirements that drove EGR. Used only to explain the change in base number selection and in charge composition; no provision is quoted or numbered.
  • Engine builder service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation - the only correct sources for feed rates, base numbers, jacket water temperatures and wear limits. Not held by MIW and not reproduced.
  • ISM Code chapter 10 - maintenance of the ship and equipment, and the identification of critical equipment, under which liner monitoring and scrape-down sampling are planned and recorded.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

This question carries no statutory content and the answer says so rather than borrowing authority from an unrelated instrument. The chemistry is standard combustion and corrosion science; the design responses are published low-speed two-stroke practice, carried as authoritative secondary and described by function.

No numeric value is asserted anywhere as a general rule - no jacket cooling water temperature, no liner wall temperature, no acid dew point, no feed rate, no base number and no wear rate. Every one is engine, oil, fuel and service specific, and a figure carried between installations is a hazard as well as an evidential defect. This is the same discipline MIW applies to condemning limits at Q9 on this paper.

No engine builder, licensee, engine series, proprietary lubricator system, trade name, oil company or cylinder oil product is named. The systems are described by what they do.

The only dated fact used is the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020 - four and a half years before this sitting. No MARPOL Annex VI provision is quoted or numbered; the MIW corpus holds Annex VI as citation-ready only.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What cold corrosion is
  2. __________Why the new generation of engines made it worse
  3. __________How it presents, and how to tell it from other wear
  4. __________A Limb A - increased jacket cooling water temperature
  5. __________B Limb B - new cylinder liner designs
  6. __________C Limb C - new cylinder lubricators
  7. __________The other failure mode - too much treatment
  8. __________Monitoring - what closes the loop
Flashcards 9

1 The mechanism. 2 Why the new generation made it worse. 3 Recognition and differential diagnosis. 4 Limb A - jacket cooling water temperature. 5 Limb B - liner design. 6 Limb C - cylinder lubricators. 7 The over-treatment balance. 8 Monitoring. The three lettered items are weighting instructions, not the whole answer - the stem also asks for the causes.

A candidate who writes only sections 4, 5 and 6 has answered the sub-headings and not the question.

Candidates who describe water condensation have described a different and far less aggressive mechanism.

Because the temperature margin closed from both ends. Long stroke, de-rating and habitual part-load running put less heat into the liner, lowering the wall temperature. Higher maximum combustion pressure raises the partial pressures of water vapour and sulphur trioxide and so raises the acid dew point itself. EGR returns water vapour and acid species to the charge, and more effective cooling removes heat from precisely the region that needs to stay hot.

This is the sentence the whole question turns on, and treating it as purely a cooling problem loses half the causal analysis.

Separate or staged circuits control the upper liner independently of the cover. Limits: reduced margin to boiling and steam pockets in the cooling passages, higher thermal stress, and less forgiving water treatment.

Presenting eight features as one idea is worth more than listing them, and it is what the word "discuss" is asking for.

The two numbers are only diagnostic together.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Sulphur burns to SO2, part oxidises to SO3, which combines with combustion water vapour to form sulphuric acid. It condenses wherever the liner is below the acid dew point - much higher than the water dew point - and attacks the iron unless the cylinder oil's alkaline reserve neutralises it. The new generation made it worse from both directions: long stroke, de-rating and part load put less heat into the liner, while higher firing pressure and EGR raise the dew point. Signature: upper liner, clover-leafing between the quills, matt etched surface, iron up and residual BN down in the scrape-down. Mitigation: A raise jacket water temperature with load-dependent control - limited by boiling in the cooling passages and thermal stress; B liner design - insulating tubes and re-profiled bore cooling, insulating collar, anti-polishing ring, controlled honing for oil retention, higher-alloy irons; C lubrication - electronic timed pulse injection, feed rate against load and the sulphur actually burned, load-change dosage, higher base number oils. But over-treatment forms calcium ash, polishes the bore and sticks the rings - so it is a balance, and iron with residual BN is what tells you which way to move.
Keywords
sulphuric acidacid dew pointSO3cold corrosionclover leafingupper linersuper long strokede-ratingpart loadfiring pressureEGRjacket cooling waterload-dependent temperature controlbore coolinginsulating tubeanti-polishing ringflame ringhoningoil retentionelectronic lubricatortimed pulse injectionfeed ratebase numbercalcium ashbore polishingring stickingscrape-down analysisresidual BN
Critical numbers
  • 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil outside emission control areas, in force 1 January 2020 - the only figure quoted in this answer
  • NO jacket water temperature, liner wall temperature, acid dew point, feed rate, base number or wear rate is quoted as a general value - every one is engine, oil, fuel and service specific
  • The governing sources for those values are the engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation
Critical regulation
None. No IMO or statutory instrument prescribes a cylinder lubrication regime or a liner temperature, and the answer says so rather than borrowing authority from an unrelated instrument. MARPOL Annex VI is named only for the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit that changed base number selection, and for the Tier III requirements that drove exhaust gas recirculation.
Major trap
Describing water condensation instead of sulphuric acid condensation. Different mechanism, far less aggressive, and it removes the foundation of the whole answer. Second trap: treating the problem as purely a cooling issue and missing that higher firing pressure raises the dew point - the margin closes from both ends, not one. Third: recommending maximum feed rate and maximum base number as if over-treatment were free. Unconsumed calcium detergent forms abrasive ash, polishes the honed bore and causes ring sticking - so an over-alkaline oil can cause the wear it was chosen to prevent. Fourth: writing only the three lettered sections and never answering "discuss the causes". Fifth: quoting a remembered temperature, feed rate or base number - they are installation specific, and a figure carried between engines is a hazard as well as an examination risk.

Also on the platform: QP2406 Q9 - the used-oil and scrape-down analysis that measures whether these mitigations are working · QP2406 Q5 - the ISM maintenance and critical-equipment regime under which liner monitoring is planned and recorded

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.
