Reuse tier C · Verification This is a pure marine-engineering question with no statutory content, and it is sourced on that footing. The chemistry - sulphur in the fuel oxidising to sulphur dioxide and trioxide in combustion, combining with water vapour to form sulphuric acid, and condensing on any surface below the acid dew point - is standard combustion and corrosion science and is carried as engineering and analytical-chemistry reference. The design responses are the published two-stroke practice of the low-speed engine designers, carried as authoritative secondary. NO ENGINE BUILDER, LICENSEE, PROPRIETARY SYSTEM, TRADE NAME, OIL COMPANY OR CYLINDER OIL PRODUCT IS NAMED ANYWHERE, and no jacket water temperature, liner wall temperature, feed rate in grammes per kilowatt-hour, base number, sulphur percentage other than the MARPOL limit, or wear rate in millimetres per thousand hours is asserted as a general value. Every one of those is engine, oil, fuel and service specific, and a figure carried from one installation to another is a genuine hazard as well as an evidential defect - the same discipline this paper applies at Q9. The single dated fact used is the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020, four and a half years before this sitting; it is used only to explain why base number selection changed, and no MARPOL Annex VI provision is quoted or numbered, consistent with the corpus holding that Annex as citation-ready only.
Provenance P2_AUTHORITATIVE_SECONDARY 4 · INTERNAL_REUSE_VERIFIED 1 · ENGINEERING_JUDGEMENT 3 · UNRESOLVED 5 · TIME_SENSITIVE_REVERIFY 1
Sources
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - the cold corrosion mechanism: sulphur in the fuel oxidises during combustion to sulphur dioxide and, in part, to sulphur trioxide; sulphur trioxide combines with water vapour from combustion to form sulphuric acid vapour; the acid condenses on any surface whose temperature is below the acid dew point, which is far above the water dew point and rises with both the sulphur content and the cylinder pressure. Standard combustion and corrosion science, stated by mechanism and not attributed to any publication.
  • ENGINEERING REFERENCE - the effect of cylinder pressure on the acid dew point: raising the partial pressure of water vapour and sulphur trioxide raises the temperature at which the acid condenses, so the higher firing pressures of modern de-rated long-stroke engines increase the dew point at the same time as lower specific output reduces liner wall temperature. The two effects act in the same direction and that is the core of the answer.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - published low-speed two-stroke design practice on jacket cooling water temperature control: raising the jacket outlet temperature, load-dependent cylinder liner temperature control, insulating tubes in the cooling bores, and separate or staged liner cooling circuits. Described by principle and function; no builder, series, system name or temperature value is asserted.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - published two-stroke liner design practice: reduced and re-profiled bore cooling in the upper liner, insulating inserts, the anti-polishing ring or flame ring at the top of the liner to remove carbon deposits from the piston crown land, controlled surface finish and honing patterns for oil retention, and higher-alloy grey cast irons and surface treatments. Described by function; no proprietary name is used.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - published cylinder lubrication practice: electronically controlled, timed, pulse injection of cylinder oil synchronised to the position of the piston ring pack; feed rate proportional to engine load and to the sulphur content of the fuel actually burned; load-change dependent dosage; and higher base number cylinder oils for cold-corrosion-prone service. Described by principle and function; no lubricator system, oil, base number or feed rate is named or numbered.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil used outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020, under MARPOL Annex VI. Used solely to explain why cylinder oil base number is now selected against the fuel actually burned rather than against a worst case. No Annex VI provision is quoted or numbered; the corpus holds Annex VI as citation-ready only.
  • ENGINEERING JUDGEMENT - the analysis that the three mitigation routes are one control problem approached from three directions (raise the surface temperature, change the surface, neutralise what lands on it), that over-treatment is a failure mode symmetrical with under-treatment, and the sequencing and monitoring advice in the closing section. Reasoned from the mechanisms rather than taken from a recommendation.
  • INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED - the MIW discipline established at QP2411-Q9 and applied on this paper at Q9: no condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, base number figure or wear rate is quoted as a general rule, because every such value is engine, oil and application specific.
Unresolved
  • NO NUMERIC VALUE IS ASSERTED AS A GENERAL RULE ANYWHERE IN THIS ANSWER - no jacket cooling water temperature, no liner wall temperature, no acid dew point temperature, no cylinder oil feed rate in grammes per kilowatt-hour, no base number, and no liner wear rate. Every one of them is specific to the engine, the oil, the fuel and the service, and a value carried from one installation to another is a hazard as well as an evidential defect. The engine builder's own service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation are the only correct sources for them.
  • NO ENGINE BUILDER, LICENSEE, ENGINE SERIES, PROPRIETARY LUBRICATOR SYSTEM, TRADE NAME, OIL COMPANY OR CYLINDER OIL PRODUCT IS NAMED. The design responses are described by function.
  • The relative contribution of cold corrosion as against abrasive and adhesive wear in any given case of high liner wear cannot be settled from the wear rate alone; the answer states the diagnostic signatures rather than asserting a proportion.
  • The acid dew point is described as rising with sulphur content and with cylinder pressure. The functional relationship is stated; no correlation, chart or equation is reproduced, because MIW holds none.
  • The sequencing and management advice in the closing section is engineering judgement reasoned from the mechanisms. It is not a manufacturer's recommendation and is not presented as one.
Re-verify before publication
  • That the acid dew point rises with cylinder pressure as well as with fuel sulphur content, and that this contributes materially to the problem on high-firing-pressure engines — Stated as a functional relationship reasoned from the partial pressures of water vapour and sulphur trioxide. No correlation, chart or equation is held by MIW and none is reproduced. The direction of the relationship is not in doubt; the magnitude is not asserted. Confirm the account against an engine builder's technical publication before publication. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • The set of liner design features described in limb B, including the anti-polishing ring, insulating tubes in cooling bores and profiled surface finishes — Published low-speed two-stroke design practice, carried as authoritative secondary and described by function with no builder or proprietary name used. MIW holds no engine builder documentation. Confirm before publication. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • That the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit outside emission control areas was in force at this sitting — In force since 1 January 2020, four and a half years before the sitting, and used only to explain base number selection. Date-driven by construction and re-checked before publication. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
Understand this first

The ISM Code is short, and it is short on purpose. It does not tell you how to run a ship - it tells you that you must write down how you run your ship, do what you wrote, record that you did it, and check yourself. That is the whole architecture, and the six functional requirements in limb (A) are just that architecture itemised. Once that is clear, limb (B) stops being a separate topic: the internal audit is the check yourself step, and it is written into the definition of a safety management system rather than bolted on to it. And the crucial thing about an audit is what it is not - it is not an inspection. An inspection asks whether the pump works. An audit asks whether there was a procedure that would have found out, whether anyone followed it, whether anyone wrote it down, and whether anyone read what was written. Limb (C) then supplies the vocabulary the system uses to talk about its own failures, and the three words are genuinely different: a non-conformity is a state of affairs, an accident is an event where harm happened, and a hazardous occurrence is an event where it nearly did. The last is the most valuable, because it carries the same information at none of the cost - which is why a ship reporting no near misses is not a safe ship, it is a quiet one.

Memory cue. Six, then four, then three. SIX at 1.4: Policy, Procedures, Authority, Reporting, Emergency, Audit (1). FOUR questions hidden in limb (B): objectives (2), helps external how (3), salient issues (4), how often - twelve months, so five over the cycle (5). THREE in limb (C): a state, an event with harm, an event without (6-7) - and the forgotten half of major: systemic failure to implement is itself major (6).

Model written answer

1. The functional requirements - ISM Code paragraph 1.4

The Code does not describe a safety management system; it states what one must be able to do. Paragraph 1.4 requires every Company to develop, implement and maintain an SMS that includes six functional requirements. Four marks, six items - so they are listed, correctly and completely, and not discussed:

  1. A safety and environmental protection policy.
  2. Instructions and procedures to ensure safe operation of ships and protection of the environment, in compliance with relevant international and flag State legislation.
  3. Defined levels of authority and lines of communication between, and among, shore and shipboard personnel.
  4. Procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformities with the provisions of the Code.
  5. Procedures to prepare for and respond to emergency situations.
  6. Procedures for internal audits and management reviews.

Two observations are worth one line each. The list is closed and numbered in the Code, so a candidate who offers five items or eight has demonstrably not read paragraph 1.4. And requirement six is the one this question then examines at length in limb (B) - the Code builds its own audit obligation into its definition of a safety management system, so a system without internal audit is not a deficient SMS but not an SMS at all.

For context in a single sentence: the ISM Code was adopted by resolution A.741(18) and made mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX, and its objectives under paragraph 1.2 are to ensure safety at sea, prevention of human injury or loss of life, and avoidance of damage to the environment and to property. The functional requirements are the machinery by which those objectives are pursued.

2. The objectives of an ISM internal audit

An internal audit is the Company auditing itself - its own ships and its own shore organisation - against its own SMS and against the Code. Its objectives are:

  • To verify that safety and pollution-prevention activities comply with the SMS - that what is written down is what is being done.
  • To verify the effectiveness of the SMS, which is a separate and harder question: a procedure can be followed exactly and still not be achieving anything.
  • To confirm continued compliance with the ISM Code and with applicable statutory and flag State requirements.
  • To identify non-conformities, and the conditions that could give rise to them, before they become accidents - the audit looks for latent weakness, not only for breaches.
  • To confirm that corrective actions from previous audits, incidents and inspections have been implemented and are effective, rather than merely closed on paper.
  • To provide the Company's management with objective evidence on which the management review can be based.
  • To drive continual improvement of the system, and to reinforce safety awareness among the crew by involving them in it.

The distinction that separates a good answer here: an internal audit audits the system, it does not inspect the ship. A superintendent's inspection asks whether the pump works. An audit asks whether there is a procedure that would have found out, whether it was followed, whether it was recorded, and whether the record was reviewed. Both are necessary and they are not the same activity.

3. How the internal audit helps the external audit

The external audit is carried out by the flag Administration or by a Recognized Organization acting for it, and leads to the Document of Compliance for the Company and the Safety Management Certificate for the ship. The internal audit supports it in five distinct ways, and listing them as five is what earns the marks:

ContributionEffect on the external audit
Finds and closes non-conformities firstWhat would have been an external finding is already a closed internal one with evidence of corrective action - the strongest possible demonstration that the system works
Produces the objective evidence the external auditor asks forAudit reports, non-conformity records, corrective actions and their verification are exactly the documents an external auditor will call for, and they exist because the internal audit created them
Familiarises the crew with being auditedCrew who have been asked to explain their responsibilities under the SMS by an internal auditor answer the external auditor confidently. Hesitation, not the answer, is what an auditor records
Verifies that the documentation is controlled and currentSuperseded manuals, uncontrolled copies and missing records are among the commonest external findings and are trivially avoidable internally
Feeds the management reviewWhich is itself a functional requirement, and demonstrates to the external auditor that the loop from finding to management decision actually closes

The negative case makes the point sharply and is worth stating: a Company whose internal audits never raise a non-conformity has not proved its ships are perfect. It has proved that its internal audits are not real - and an external auditor who sees a run of clean internal reports will look harder, not less hard.

4. The salient issues addressed in an internal audit

  • The safety and environmental protection policy - is it implemented and understood at all levels, ashore and on board?
  • Company responsibility and authority, and the identity, role and accessibility of the Designated Person Ashore.
  • The master's responsibility and authority, including his overriding authority to take whatever action he considers necessary, and the conduct and content of the master's review of the SMS.
  • Resources and personnel - manning, qualification, familiarisation of newly joined seafarers, and training identified and delivered.
  • Shipboard operations - that plans and instructions for key operations exist, are used, and reflect what the ship actually does.
  • Emergency preparedness - drills and exercises carried out, recorded, and reviewed for lessons rather than merely ticked.
  • Reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences - are they raised, investigated, analysed for cause and closed out?
  • Maintenance of the ship and equipment, and in particular the identification and testing of critical equipment - the point at which many audits actually bite.
  • Documentation - control, currency, availability at the point of use, and the removal of superseded documents.
  • Certification - DOC and SMC valid, endorsed and matching the ship and ship type.

5. Frequency, and the arithmetic over the SMC cycle

Paragraph 12.1 requires the Company to carry out internal safety audits on board and ashore at intervals not exceeding twelve months. The Code allows that interval to be exceeded in exceptional circumstances, and then by not more than three months.

So over the five-year validity of the Safety Management Certificate the minimum is at least five internal audits - one in each twelve-month period. It is worth putting the internal cycle beside the external one, because the question invites the comparison:

Internal auditExternal verification
WhoThe Company, using auditors independent of the area auditedThe flag Administration or a Recognized Organization
IntervalNot exceeding 12 months; in exceptional circumstances not more than 15Initial; at least one intermediate verification between the second and third anniversary date; renewal within five years
Number over the SMC's five yearsAt least fiveInitial, one intermediate, renewal
OutputInternal audit report and non-conformities, feeding the management reviewThe Safety Management Certificate; and for the Company, the Document of Compliance, subject to annual verification

Two further requirements of paragraph 12 belong here because examiners ask for them: personnel carrying out the audit should be independent of the areas being audited unless the size and nature of the Company make that impracticable; and the results must be brought to the attention of all personnel having responsibility in the area involved, whose management must take timely corrective action.

6. Non-conformity and major non-conformity

These two are defined in the Code and the definitions should be given as such.

TermThe Code's definition, in effectConsequence
Non-conformity (1.1.9)An observed situation where objective evidence indicates the non-fulfilment of a specified requirementCorrective action within an agreed time; the certificate stands
Major non-conformity (1.1.10)An identifiable deviation posing a serious threat to the safety of personnel or the ship, or a serious risk to the environment, that requires immediate corrective action; and it includes the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the CodeImmediate action required; may lead to suspension or withdrawal of the SMC or DOC, and is a detainable finding for a port State

Three points about these definitions repay attention. Objective evidence is what distinguishes a non-conformity from an opinion - an auditor must be able to point at something. A specified requirement means the SMS, the Code, or a statutory requirement, so a Company that writes a demanding procedure creates the possibility of a non-conformity against itself, which is why procedures should say what the ship will actually do. And the second half of the major non-conformity definition is the one most often forgotten: systemic failure to implement the Code is itself major, even where no single deviation is dangerous. A ship on which nothing in particular is wrong, but on which the SMS is plainly not being used, is a major non-conformity.

7. Accidents and hazardous occurrences

First, a point of accuracy the examiner will not penalise but will notice: the printed stem says "non-conformities, Accident and Hazardous occurrences", while paragraph 9.1 of the Code requires the SMS to include procedures ensuring that non-conformities, accidents and hazardous situations are reported to the Company, investigated and analysed with the object of improving safety and pollution prevention. The terms are used interchangeably in practice; the answer treats them together and cites the Code's own wording.

The Code does not define "accident", and it is worth saying so rather than inventing a definition. In practice:

  • An accident is an unintended event that has caused harm - injury or death, damage to the ship, cargo or equipment, or damage to the environment.
  • A hazardous occurrence - or hazardous situation - is an event or condition that could have caused such harm but did not. It is the near miss, and it is the more valuable of the two, because it carries the same information about the system at none of the cost.
  • A non-conformity is different in kind from both: it is not an event at all, but a state of non-compliance with a specified requirement, and it may exist for months without anything happening.

Worked examples make the distinction visible, and an examiner asking for "examples" is asking for exactly this:

SituationClassificationWhy
The fire drill record shows the last drill was eight weeks ago; the SMS requires monthlyNon-conformityObjective evidence of non-fulfilment of a specified requirement. Nothing has happened
A fitter suffers a lost-time hand injury while replacing a fuel pumpAccidentAn unintended event that has caused harm
A high-pressure fuel line sprays onto a hot surface; there is no fire because the shielding heldHazardous occurrenceCould have caused serious harm and did not. Report and investigate exactly as if it had
A spanner is dropped from a grating into an empty walkwayHazardous occurrenceNo harm, but the same failure with someone below is a fatality
The oily-water separator's 15 ppm alarm is found disconnectedMajor non-conformitySerious risk to the environment requiring immediate corrective action - and it implies deliberate defeat of the system
Bunkering overflows and oil reaches the waterAccident, and a reportable pollution incidentHarm has occurred; separate statutory reporting is engaged beyond the SMS
The SMS is on board but no one uses it; records are written up retrospectivelyMajor non-conformityLack of effective and systematic implementation of the Code, which the definition expressly includes

The reporting duty under paragraph 9.1 runs to all three, and the object stated in the Code is not punishment but improvement. That is the closing point worth making: a reporting culture that produces few hazardous occurrence reports is not a safe ship - it is a ship where near misses are not being reported, and the system has been denied the information it exists to process.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

4 + 6 + 6, and the mark split tells you the writing style for each limb. Limb (A) is four marks for six listed items - so list them, numbered, and move on. Any candidate who writes three paragraphs of discussion on the functional requirements has spent six marks' worth of time earning four. Limb (B) contains four separate questions - objectives, help to external audit, salient issues, frequency - and each needs a visible answer. Limb (C) asks for three terms with examples, and the examples are not optional decoration; they are how the distinctions are demonstrated.

Paragraph 1.4 - learn it as a closed list of six

Policy - Procedures - Authority - Reporting - Emergency - Audit. Six items, in the Code's own order. The list is closed, so offering five or eight shows you have not read it. A useful mnemonic is that the first two say what the system contains, the third says who does what, and the last three are the three loops that keep it alive: reporting things that went wrong, preparing for things that might, and checking the system itself.

The self-referential point worth one sentence

The sixth functional requirement is internal audits and management reviews - so the Code builds its own audit obligation into its definition of a safety management system. A system without internal audit is not a deficient SMS; it is not an SMS at all. Saying that links limb (A) directly into limb (B) and shows the two are one subject.

The distinction that carries limb (B)

An audit audits the system; an inspection inspects the ship. A superintendent's inspection asks whether the pump works. An audit asks whether there is a procedure that would have found out, whether it was followed, whether it was recorded, and whether anyone reviewed the record. Candidates who describe an internal audit as "a thorough inspection by the Company" have missed the whole point of the Code.

The negative case - use it

A Company whose internal audits never raise a non-conformity has not proved that its ships are perfect; it has proved that its audits are not real. An external auditor who sees a run of clean internal reports looks harder. This is also, from the other side, the ISM-related clear ground a PSCO uses - see Q3 on this paper.

The numbers in limb (B)

  • Internal audits on board and ashore, at intervals not exceeding twelve months (paragraph 12.1).
  • In exceptional circumstances that interval may be exceeded by not more than three months - so fifteen months absolute maximum.
  • Over the SMC's five-year validity: at least five internal audits.
  • Auditors should be independent of the area audited, unless the size and nature of the Company make that impracticable.
  • External: initial, at least one intermediate verification between the second and third anniversary date, and renewal within five years. The DOC is subject to annual verification.

The question expressly asks for the minimum frequency "during the five-year validity of the ship's Safety Management Certificate", so give the twelve-month rule and do the arithmetic - five audits. Candidates give the rule and never answer the question actually asked.

The half of the major non-conformity definition everyone forgets

Most candidates give the first half - a serious threat to personnel or the ship, or a serious risk to the environment, requiring immediate corrective action. The definition also expressly includes the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code. That is how a ship with nothing in particular wrong, but on which the SMS is plainly not used, is a major non-conformity. Write both halves.

Non-conformity, accident, hazardous occurrence - the clean test

Is it an event?Did harm occur?Test
Non-conformityNo - a stateNot necessarilyObjective evidence of non-fulfilment of a specified requirement
AccidentYesYesUnintended event that has caused injury, damage or pollution
Hazardous occurrenceYesNo - but could haveThe near miss; same information, none of the cost

The one-line version: a non-conformity is a state, an accident is an event with harm, a hazardous occurrence is an event without harm.

A wording point the examiner will notice

The stem says "Hazardous occurrences"; the Code at paragraph 9.1 says hazardous situations. They are used interchangeably in practice. Noting the variation in half a sentence and then citing paragraph 9.1 correctly shows you have read the Code rather than a summary of it - and it costs nothing.

Common mistakes

  • Discussing the functional requirements at length for four marks, and then rushing the twelve-mark half of the paper.
  • Giving five functional requirements, or inventing a seventh. The list at 1.4 is closed and numbered.
  • Describing an internal audit as an inspection.
  • Giving the twelve-month rule but never answering "how many during the five-year SMC validity".
  • Omitting the three-month exceptional-circumstances allowance.
  • Giving only the first half of the major non-conformity definition.
  • Attributing a definition of "accident" to the ISM Code. It does not define one - say so.
  • Answering limb (C) with definitions and no examples, when the stem says "with example".

Examiner traps

  • "How many functional requirements?" Six, at paragraph 1.4.
  • "What is the maximum internal audit interval?" Twelve months - fifteen in exceptional circumstances, and the exception must be exceptional.
  • "Who can carry out an internal audit?" Personnel independent of the area audited, unless the Company's size makes that impracticable. The Chief Engineer cannot audit his own department.
  • "Give a major non-conformity with nothing broken." An SMS that is on board but not used - systemic failure to implement, which the definition expressly covers.
  • "Does the ISM Code define an accident?" No.
  • "Which is more useful, an accident report or a near-miss report?" The near miss - same information about the system, none of the cost.
  • "What happens to the certificate on a major non-conformity?" Immediate corrective action; the SMC or DOC may be suspended or withdrawn, and a port State can detain on it.
  • "How often is the DOC verified?" Annually. The SMC has an intermediate verification between the second and third anniversary.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Who is your DPA and how would you contact him tonight?
  • Show me the last internal audit report and the non-conformities raised on your department.
  • What is the master's overriding authority and where is it written?
  • Which equipment on your ship is identified as critical, and how do you know?
  • You find a hazardous occurrence that would embarrass a junior engineer. What do you do?
  • Your internal audit is due next month and you are on a long voyage. What are your options?

Memory framework

Six, then four, then three. SIX functional requirements at 1.4 - Policy, Procedures, Authority, Reporting, Emergency, Audit. FOUR questions inside limb (B) - objectives, helps external how, salient issues, how often (twelve months, five over the cycle). THREE terms in limb (C) - a state, an event with harm, an event without harm - plus the forgotten half of "major": systemic failure to implement is itself major.

Regulation and source map

  • ISM Code paragraph 1.2 - objectives of the Code.
  • Paragraph 1.4 - the six functional requirements of a safety management system.
  • Paragraphs 1.1.9 and 1.1.10 - non-conformity and major non-conformity.
  • Paragraph 5 - master's responsibility and authority, including 5.1.5, the master's review.
  • Paragraph 9.1 - reporting and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous situations.
  • Paragraph 10 - maintenance of the ship and equipment; identification of critical equipment.
  • Paragraph 12 - internal audits, the twelve-month interval, auditor independence, and management review.
  • SOLAS chapter IX - the mandating hook; the DOC and SMC and their verification cycle.
  • A.741(18) - the adopting resolution; MSC.353(92), in force 1 January 2015 - the most recent amendment to the Code.
  • A.1184(33) - Revised guidelines on implementation of the ISM Code by Administrations, December 2023. Guidance, not part of the Code.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

This is the most securely sourced question on the paper. The MIW corpus holds the official IMO resolution chain for the ISM Code and records its storage classification as full official text legally available. The corpus also fixes the amendment cutoff: MSC.353(92), in force 1 January 2015, is the most recent amendment to the Code itself. That places this June 2024 sitting nine and a half years inside a stable period - so, uniquely on this paper, there is no amendment boundary to manage at all.

Notwithstanding that verbatim use is legally permitted for this instrument, no paragraph of the Code is reproduced word for word here. Paragraphs are cited by number and their effect stated, as a matter of MIW house discipline.

One open corpus verification is carried forward: that no ISM amendment was adopted after MSC.353(92). Nothing in this answer depends on a later amendment. The intermediate amendment entry-into-force dates for MSC.179(79), MSC.195(80) and MSC.273(85) are flagged unverified in the corpus and are not asserted anywhere - only the adopting resolution, the mandatory application through SOLAS chapter IX, and MSC.353(92) are dated.

SOLAS chapter IX is held in the corpus base but is not among the provisions verified against primary text, so the DOC and SMC validity and verification periods are authoritative secondary. The domestic statute at this sitting is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, under which the Directorate General of Shipping issues DOCs and SMCs or authorises Recognized Organizations to do so; the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 is not law at this sitting and appears nowhere in this answer.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(A) The functional requirements - ISM Code paragraph 1.4
  2. __________(B) The objectives of an ISM internal audit
  3. __________(B) How the internal audit helps the external audit
  4. __________(B) The salient issues addressed in an internal audit
  5. __________(B) Frequency, and the arithmetic over the SMC cycle
  6. __________(C) Non-conformity and major non-conformity
  7. __________(C) Accidents and hazardous occurrences
Flashcards 8

1 The six functional requirements (limb A). 2 Objectives of the internal audit. 3 How it helps the external audit. 4 Salient issues addressed. 5 Frequency and the five-year arithmetic (2-5 are limb B). 6 Non-conformity and major non-conformity. 7 Accidents and hazardous occurrences with examples (6-7 are limb C).

Limb (B) hides four separate questions inside one printed paragraph. One section each is the only way to make all four visible.

Paragraph 1.4: (1) a safety and environmental protection policy; (2) instructions and procedures to ensure safe operation and protection of the environment in compliance with international and flag State legislation; (3) defined levels of authority and lines of communication between and among shore and shipboard personnel; (4) procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformities; (5) procedures to prepare for and respond to emergency situations; (6) procedures for internal audits and management reviews.

A closed, numbered list of six. Offering five or eight demonstrates you have not read paragraph 1.4.

An inspection inspects the ship - it asks whether the pump works. An audit audits the system - it asks whether there was a procedure that would have found out, whether it was followed, whether it was recorded, and whether the record was reviewed. Both are necessary and they are not the same activity.

Candidates who describe an internal audit as 'a thorough Company inspection' have missed the architecture of the whole Code.

Paragraph 12.1: internal safety audits on board and ashore at intervals not exceeding twelve months. In exceptional circumstances the interval may be exceeded by not more than three months. Over the five-year validity of the Safety Management Certificate that means at least five internal audits. Auditors should be independent of the area audited unless the size and nature of the Company make that impracticable.

The stem asks for the number over the five-year cycle, not just the interval rule. Candidates give the rule and never do the arithmetic.

Non-conformity (1.1.9): an observed situation where objective evidence indicates the non-fulfilment of a specified requirement. Major non-conformity (1.1.10): an identifiable deviation posing a serious threat to the safety of personnel or the ship, or a serious risk to the environment, requiring immediate corrective action - and it includes the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code.

The second half of the major definition is the half candidates omit, and it is how a ship with nothing broken carries a major non-conformity.

A non-conformity is a state, not an event - the fire drill record shows eight weeks where the SMS requires monthly. An accident is an event where harm occurred - a fitter suffers a lost-time hand injury. A hazardous occurrence is an event where harm nearly occurred - a high-pressure fuel line sprays onto a hot surface and the shielding prevents a fire. Note the ISM Code does not define 'accident', and paragraph 9.1 uses hazardous situations where the stem says occurrences.

The stem says 'with example'. The examples are how the distinctions are demonstrated rather than asserted.

An SMS that is on board but is not being used - records written up retrospectively, procedures unknown to the crew, non-conformities never raised. That is the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code, which paragraph 1.1.10 expressly includes in the definition of a major non-conformity.

It proves you know the second half of the definition, and it is the classic oral question on the topic.

No - it is evidence that the audits are not real. An external auditor who sees a run of clean internal reports will look harder, not less hard. The purpose of the internal audit is to find non-conformities and the conditions that could give rise to them; an audit that finds nothing has not performed its function.

The negative case is the sharpest way to state what an internal audit is for, and it links directly to the ISM-related clear grounds a PSCO uses.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Paragraph 1.4 - six functional requirements: safety and environmental protection policy; instructions and procedures for safe operation and environmental protection; defined levels of authority and lines of communication; procedures for reporting accidents and non-conformities; procedures to prepare for and respond to emergencies; procedures for internal audits and management reviews. Internal audit verifies the SMS is complied with AND effective, finds non-conformities and the conditions that breed them, checks previous corrective actions, and feeds the management review - it audits the system, it does not inspect the ship. It helps the external audit by closing findings first, producing the objective evidence, familiarising the crew and controlling the documentation. Paragraph 12.1: intervals not exceeding twelve months, exceeded in exceptional circumstances by not more than three - so at least five over the SMC's five years, by auditors independent of the area audited. 1.1.9 non-conformity = objective evidence of non-fulfilment of a specified requirement; 1.1.10 major = serious threat to personnel, ship or environment requiring immediate action, and includes the lack of effective and systematic implementation of the Code. An accident has caused harm; a hazardous occurrence could have; a non-conformity is a state, not an event. Reporting duty at 9.1.

Critical numbers
  • SIX functional requirements at paragraph 1.4 - a closed, numbered list
  • Internal audits at intervals not exceeding TWELVE months (paragraph 12.1); exceptional circumstances allow not more than THREE months more
  • At least FIVE internal audits over the five-year validity of the SMC
  • External: initial, at least one intermediate verification between the SECOND and THIRD anniversary date, renewal within five years; the DOC is verified ANNUALLY
  • A.741(18) adopted 4 November 1993; mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX from 1 July 1998
  • MSC.353(92) in force 1 January 2015 - the most recent amendment, so the Code is unchanged at this sitting
Critical regulation
ISM Code paragraph 1.4 for the six functional requirements and paragraph 12.1 for the twelve-month internal audit interval - those two carry ten of the sixteen marks between them. Read with paragraphs 1.1.9 and 1.1.10 for the definitions and 9.1 for the reporting duty.
Major trap
Giving only the first half of the major non-conformity definition. It is a serious threat requiring immediate corrective action and it expressly includes the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code - which is how a ship with nothing broken can carry a major non-conformity. Second trap: giving the twelve-month interval but never answering the question actually asked, which is how many over the five-year SMC validity - the answer is at least five. Third: describing an internal audit as an inspection; it audits the system, not the ship. Fourth: attributing a definition of "accident" to the ISM Code - it does not define one. Fifth: writing three paragraphs on the functional requirements for four marks and then rushing the twelve marks that follow.

Understand this first

The MLC is easiest to hold in your head if you stop thinking of it as a convention and start thinking of it as the life cycle of one seafarer's employment. Before he can join, is he old enough, medically fit, trained, and was he recruited without paying for the privilege? That is Title 1 and it is limb (a). Once he joins, what has he actually been promised, and what does the Convention insist is in that promise - a written agreement he had a chance to read, wages monthly, hours limited, leave accruing, and a ticket home? That is Title 2 and it is limb (b). Then: who makes any of this true? The flag State certifies, the port State checks, the State the manning agent sits in regulates the agent - and no flag can escape by not ratifying. That is Title 5 and it is limb (c). And limb (d) is what happens when the whole arrangement collapses - when the shipowner stops answering, the wages stop, and the crew are sitting on a ship in a foreign port with no food and no ticket. The Convention's answer, and this is the part worth understanding rather than memorising, is not another right. The right to go home already existed and was worth nothing against an insolvent owner. What the abandonment amendments added was a solvent payer and a telephone number on the bulkhead that the seafarer can call without asking the shipowner's permission.

Knowledge map
MLC 2006 - Minimum Requirements, Employment, Enforcement and Abandonment
  1. 1What the Convention is, and which version applies here
    • An ILO instrument, not IMO - International Labour Conference 2006, in force 20 August 2013; the 'fourth pillar'
    • Articles; Regulations; Code Part A Standards (mandatory); Code Part B Guidelines (not mandatory)
    • Five Titles: 1 minimum requirements, 2 conditions of employment, 3 accommodation and food, 4 health and welfare, 5 compliance and enforcement
  2. (a)
    1. 2Limb (a) - Title 1: minimum requirements for seafarers to work on a ship
      • Reg 1.1 minimum age - 16; night work under 18 restricted; hazardous work under 18 prohibited
      • Reg 1.2 medical certificate - max 2 years, 1 year under 18, colour vision 6 years; STCW certificate accepted; expiry at sea runs to the next port, up to 3 months
      • Reg 1.3 training and qualifications - STCW certification, plus completed training in personal safety on board
  3. (b)
    1. 3Limb (b) - Title 2: conditions of employment
      • Reg 2.1 the Seafarers' Employment Agreement - written, signed by seafarer and shipowner, opportunity to examine and seek advice, original held by each, copy and English summary available on board for inspection; any collective agreement on board too
      • Reg 2.2 wages - at intervals no greater than monthly, monthly account, right of allotment to family
      • Reg 2.3 hours - EITHER max work 14 in 24 / 72 in 7 OR min rest 10 in 24 / 77 in 7; rest in no more than two periods, one of at least 6 hours, interval not exceeding 14 hours
  4. (c)
    1. 4Limb (c) - Title 5: flag State responsibilities
      • 5.1.1 an effective system of inspection and certification; 5.1.2 Recognized Organizations may be authorised
      • 5.1.3 the Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC at 500 GT on international voyages, 5 years with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary
      • DMLC Part I is the flag State's; Part II is the SHIPOWNER'S - the classic trap
    2. 5Limb (c) - port State and labour-supplying responsibilities
      • 5.2.1 the certificate and DMLC are prima facie evidence, so the ordinary inspection checks documents
      • Clear grounds - documents not produced or invalid, grounds to believe conditions do not conform, or a complaint received - open a more detailed inspection
      • Deficiencies must be rectified; serious, repeated or complaint-based deficiencies may bring detention
    3. 6Limb (c) - the two provisions that make the system work
      • Article V(7) no more favourable treatment - a non-ratifying flag gets no advantage, so a ship cannot escape the Convention by choosing a flag
      • Article VI substantial equivalence - Part A may be implemented by substantially equivalent measures; what made near-universal ratification possible, and the seam through which divergence enters
      • Close honestly: enforcement is strong on documents and weaker on conditions, which is why the complaint procedures matter most in practice
  5. (d)
    1. 7Limb (d) - abandonment, and when it arises
      • Regulation 2.5 and Standard A2.5.2, introduced by the 2014 amendments - IN FORCE at this sitting
      • Before them the right to repatriation existed with no guarantee that anyone solvent would pay
      • Three limbs: fails to cover repatriation; leaves the seafarer without necessary maintenance and support; or otherwise unilaterally severs ties, including failure to pay contractual wages for at least two months
    2. 8Limb (d) - the financial security system
      • Financial security, usually from the P&I club, carried by every ship to which the Convention applies
      • A certificate posted in a conspicuous place on board with the provider's contact details
      • Direct access by the seafarer or a nominated representative - the claim does not go through the shipowner, who is by definition the party who failed
    3. 9Limb (d) - why the numbers rise, and what the institutions do
      • Causes: single-ship companies and opaque ownership; ageing tonnage in marginal trades; arrest and detention stopping earnings while costs continue; sanctions and trade disruption; weak registries and unlicensed agents; seafarers' weak bargaining position; and better reporting inflating the trend
      • ILO owns the Convention and its amendment machinery, and co-hosts the abandonment database
      • IMO co-hosts the database and supplies the port State control machinery that detects abandonment

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. What the Convention is, and which version applies here
(a)

(b)
  1. Limb (b) - Title 2: conditions of employment

  1. Limb (c) - Title 5: flag State responsibilities

  1. Limb (d) - abandonment, and when it arises
