Memory cue. Four limbs, four Titles, four numbers. Title 1 - AGE, MEDICAL, TRAINING, AGENCY (2). Title 2 - the AGREEMENT and everything hanging off it (3). Title 5 - FLAG certifies (4), PORT inspects and the SUPPLY State regulates the agent (5), over NO MORE FAVOURABLE TREATMENT (6). Abandonment: a CERTIFICATE, a PHONE NUMBER, a PAYER (7-9). Numbers: 16 / 2 years / 10 in 24 / 2 months. And the date that governs everything: the 2022 amendments are NOT in force here (1).

Remember9 route headings
Cover47 core points beneath them

Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 47 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What the Convention is, and which version applies here
  • An ILO instrument, not IMO - International Labour Conference 2006, in force 20 August 2013; the 'fourth pillar'
  • Articles; Regulations; Code Part A Standards (mandatory); Code Part B Guidelines (not mandatory)
  • Five Titles: 1 minimum requirements, 2 conditions of employment, 3 accommodation and food, 4 health and welfare, 5 compliance and enforcement
  • Amended by the Special Tripartite Committee under Article XIII - not by IMO tacit acceptance
  • At this sitting the Convention stands as amended through the 2018 set; the 2022 amendments come into force 23 December 2024
2. Limb (a) - Title 1: minimum requirements for seafarers to work on a ship
  • Reg 1.1 minimum age - 16; night work under 18 restricted; hazardous work under 18 prohibited
  • Reg 1.2 medical certificate - max 2 years, 1 year under 18, colour vision 6 years; STCW certificate accepted; expiry at sea runs to the next port, up to 3 months
  • Reg 1.3 training and qualifications - STCW certification, plus completed training in personal safety on board
  • Reg 1.4 recruitment and placement - no fees charged to the seafarer except the statutory medical, seafarer's book and passport; private services licensed and regulated, keeping a register, verifying agreements, and operating a system of protection to compensate monetary loss
  • Emphasise the no-fees rule - it is the Convention's answer to debt bondage and is routinely evaded
3. Limb (b) - Title 2: conditions of employment
  • Reg 2.1 the Seafarers' Employment Agreement - written, signed by seafarer and shipowner, opportunity to examine and seek advice, original held by each, copy and English summary available on board for inspection; any collective agreement on board too
  • Reg 2.2 wages - at intervals no greater than monthly, monthly account, right of allotment to family
  • Reg 2.3 hours - EITHER max work 14 in 24 / 72 in 7 OR min rest 10 in 24 / 77 in 7; rest in no more than two periods, one of at least 6 hours, interval not exceeding 14 hours
  • Reg 2.4 leave - minimum 2.5 calendar days per month; forgoing it is prohibited save as the competent authority provides
  • Reg 2.5 repatriation - at no cost to the seafarer; maximum service before repatriation less than 12 months
  • Reg 2.6 compensation for the ship's loss; 2.7 manning levels; 2.8 career and skill development
  • Name Titles 3 and 4 in one sentence to show the whole Convention has been read
4. Limb (c) - Title 5: flag State responsibilities
  • 5.1.1 an effective system of inspection and certification; 5.1.2 Recognized Organizations may be authorised
  • 5.1.3 the Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC at 500 GT on international voyages, 5 years with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary
  • DMLC Part I is the flag State's; Part II is the SHIPOWNER'S - the classic trap
  • 5.1.4 inspection and enforcement, including the power to prohibit departure
  • 5.1.5 on-board complaint procedures with safeguards against victimisation; 5.1.6 casualty inquiry
5. Limb (c) - port State and labour-supplying responsibilities
  • 5.2.1 the certificate and DMLC are prima facie evidence, so the ordinary inspection checks documents
  • Clear grounds - documents not produced or invalid, grounds to believe conditions do not conform, or a complaint received - open a more detailed inspection
  • Deficiencies must be rectified; serious, repeated or complaint-based deficiencies may bring detention
  • 5.2.2 onshore seafarer complaint-handling, seeking resolution at ship level first and reporting to the flag State
  • 5.3 labour-supplying - responsibility for recruitment services in the territory and social security for resident seafarers; this is what makes the Reg 1.4 no-fees rule enforceable at source
6. Limb (c) - the two provisions that make the system work
  • Article V(7) no more favourable treatment - a non-ratifying flag gets no advantage, so a ship cannot escape the Convention by choosing a flag
  • Article VI substantial equivalence - Part A may be implemented by substantially equivalent measures; what made near-universal ratification possible, and the seam through which divergence enters
  • Close honestly: enforcement is strong on documents and weaker on conditions, which is why the complaint procedures matter most in practice
7. Limb (d) - abandonment, and when it arises
  • Regulation 2.5 and Standard A2.5.2, introduced by the 2014 amendments - IN FORCE at this sitting
  • Before them the right to repatriation existed with no guarantee that anyone solvent would pay
  • Three limbs: fails to cover repatriation; leaves the seafarer without necessary maintenance and support; or otherwise unilaterally severs ties, including failure to pay contractual wages for at least two months
  • Subtlety one: the shipowner need not have disappeared
  • Subtlety two: two months of unpaid wages is sufficient on its own
8. Limb (d) - the financial security system
  • Financial security, usually from the P&I club, carried by every ship to which the Convention applies
  • A certificate posted in a conspicuous place on board with the provider's contact details
  • Direct access by the seafarer or a nominated representative - the claim does not go through the shipowner, who is by definition the party who failed
  • Covers outstanding wages to a defined limit, repatriation, and essential needs - food, clothing, accommodation, drinking water, essential fuel, medical care
  • Provided promptly; the security may not be cancelled without prior notice to the flag State
9. Limb (d) - why the numbers rise, and what the institutions do
  • Causes: single-ship companies and opaque ownership; ageing tonnage in marginal trades; arrest and detention stopping earnings while costs continue; sanctions and trade disruption; weak registries and unlicensed agents; seafarers' weak bargaining position; and better reporting inflating the trend
  • ILO owns the Convention and its amendment machinery, and co-hosts the abandonment database
  • IMO co-hosts the database and supplies the port State control machinery that detects abandonment
  • ITF - not a regulator but the most effective practical actor: its inspectorate boards ships, pursues wage claims and funds food and repatriation
  • Flag State notified before security is cancelled and ultimately responsible; port State detects, detains and handles the onshore complaint
  • The security provider pays
  • Close: the 2014 amendments did not create a right - they created a payer
Model written answer

1. What the Convention is, and which version applies here

The Maritime Labour Convention, 2006 is an International Labour Organization instrument - not an IMO one - adopted by the International Labour Conference at its 94th (Maritime) Session in 2006 and in force since 20 August 2013. It consolidated some sixty-eight earlier maritime labour instruments into one, and is widely called the fourth pillar of international maritime regulation alongside SOLAS, MARPOL and STCW.

Its structure must be understood before any limb can be answered properly, because the four limbs of this question are simply four parts of it:

  • Articles - the general obligations and the machinery of the Convention.
  • Regulations - the substantive requirements, grouped into five Titles.
  • The Code, Part A - Standards, which are mandatory.
  • The Code, Part B - Guidelines, which are not mandatory, but which a Member must give due consideration to.

The five Titles are: Title 1 minimum requirements for seafarers to work on a ship; Title 2 conditions of employment; Title 3 accommodation, recreational facilities, food and catering; Title 4 health protection, medical care, welfare and social security protection; Title 5 compliance and enforcement. Limbs (a), (b) and (c) of this question are Titles 1, 2 and 5; limb (d) sits inside Title 2 at Regulation 2.5.

One further point of machinery, because it distinguishes this Convention from every IMO instrument on the paper: the Code is amended not by MSC or MEPC tacit acceptance but by the Special Tripartite Committee under Article XIII, using the Convention's own simplified amendment procedure. The amendment set operative at this examination is the 2018 set; the 2022 amendments enter into force on 23 December 2024, six months after this sitting, and are not law here.

2. Limb (a) - Title 1: minimum requirements for seafarers to work on a ship

Title 1 asks a single question in four parts: is this person allowed to be on this ship at all?

RegulationRequirementThe examinable detail
1.1 Minimum ageNo person below the minimum age may be employed or work on a shipThe minimum age is 16. Night work by seafarers under 18 is prohibited, subject to a limited exception where it would impair training. Work likely to jeopardise the health or safety of a seafarer under 18 is prohibited outright, and the types of such work are determined by national law after consultation
1.2 Medical certificateNo seafarer may work without a valid medical certificate attesting fitness for the duties to be performedValid for a maximum of two years, or one year if the seafarer is under 18. A colour vision certificate is valid for a maximum of six years. A certificate issued under STCW is accepted. Where a certificate expires at sea it continues in force until the next port where a qualified practitioner is available, not exceeding three months. In urgent cases a seafarer may be permitted to work without a valid certificate until the next port, for a limited period
1.3 Training and qualificationsSeafarers must be trained, certificated or otherwise qualified for their dutiesTraining and certification in accordance with STCW; and no seafarer may work unless successfully completed training in personal safety on board
1.4 Recruitment and placementSeafarers must have access to an efficient and well-regulated recruitment and placement system, free of chargeNo fees or other charges for recruitment or placement may be borne directly or indirectly by the seafarer, other than the cost of the statutory medical certificate, the national seafarer's book and a passport. Private services must be licensed or regulated, must maintain a register, must verify that employment agreements meet the Convention, and must operate a system of protection - insurance or equivalent - to compensate seafarers for monetary loss from the service's or the shipowner's failure to meet its obligations. Where a shipowner uses a service in a State that has not ratified, he must satisfy himself that it meets the same requirements

The no-fees rule in Regulation 1.4 is the one to write with emphasis. It is the Convention's answer to debt bondage in the manning market, it is routinely evaded, and it is the provision under which a port State inspection most often finds something a candidate would not have predicted.

3. Limb (b) - Title 2: conditions of employment

If Title 1 is about admission, Title 2 is about the bargain - and everything in it hangs off one document.

  • Regulation 2.1 - the Seafarers' Employment Agreement. Every seafarer must have a written agreement signed by both the seafarer and the shipowner, giving decent working and living conditions. The seafarer must be given an opportunity to examine and seek advice on it before signing. An original signed copy is held by each, and a copy and, where the agreement is not in English, an English-language summary of its terms must be available on board for inspection. Where a collective bargaining agreement forms part of it, that too must be on board. Standard A2.1 lists the required particulars, and the seafarer must be given a document containing a record of employment that contains no statement as to the quality of his work or his wages.
  • Regulation 2.2 - Wages. Paid at no greater than monthly intervals and in full, in accordance with the agreement and any applicable collective agreement. Seafarers must be given a monthly account showing payments and the exchange rate used. Seafarers must be able to allot all or part of their earnings to their families, and any charge for the service must be reasonable and the exchange rate fair.
  • Regulation 2.3 - Hours of work and hours of rest. This is the provision candidates get wrong, so state it in the Convention's own alternative form: a Member must fix either a maximum number of hours of work - not exceeding 14 in any 24-hour period and 72 in any seven-day period - or a minimum number of hours of rest - not less than 10 in any 24-hour period and 77 in any seven-day period. Where the rest option is used, rest may be divided into no more than two periods, one of which must be at least six hours, and the interval between consecutive rest periods must not exceed fourteen hours. Records must be kept and the schedule posted in an accessible place. Musters, drills and safety work may interrupt rest, with compensatory rest given.
  • Regulation 2.4 - Entitlement to leave. Paid annual leave calculated on the basis of a minimum of 2.5 calendar days per month of employment. Any agreement to forgo the minimum leave is prohibited, except in cases provided for by the competent authority.
  • Regulation 2.5 - Repatriation, with the financial security system dealt with at limb (d) below. Seafarers are entitled to repatriation at no cost to themselves in the defined circumstances, and the maximum duration of service on board before repatriation is less than twelve months.
  • Regulation 2.6 - Compensation for the ship's loss or foundering, including an indemnity against unemployment resulting from it.
  • Regulation 2.7 - Manning levels - sufficient, safe and efficient manning, having regard to the safe manning document and to fatigue.
  • Regulation 2.8 - Career and skill development and opportunities for employment.

It is worth adding, because the examiner is testing breadth, that Titles 3 and 4 complete the picture of conditions on board - accommodation, recreational facilities, food and catering under Title 3, and health protection, on-board and onshore medical care, shipowner liability, occupational safety and health, welfare facilities and social security under Title 4. Naming them in a sentence shows the whole Convention has been read even though limb (b) does not require them in detail.

4. Limb (c) - Title 5: flag State responsibilities

Title 5 has three parts, one for each actor, and the strength of the Convention is that it does not rely on any one of them.

  • The flag State must establish an effective system of inspection and certification of maritime labour conditions (5.1.1), and may authorise Recognized Organizations to carry it out (5.1.2).
  • 5.1.3 - the Maritime Labour Certificate and the Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance. Required for ships of 500 gross tonnage and above engaged on international voyages or operating from a port, or between ports, in another country. The certificate is valid for a maximum of five years, subject to an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary date. The DMLC Part I is drawn up by the flag State and identifies the national requirements; Part II is drawn up by the shipowner and sets out the measures adopted to ensure ongoing compliance. Both are attached to the certificate and carried on board.
  • 5.1.4 - Inspection and enforcement, with a sufficient number of qualified inspectors, powers to require deficiencies to be remedied, and power to prohibit the ship from leaving port until they are.
  • 5.1.5 - On-board complaint procedures. Every ship must have procedures allowing seafarers to complain about breaches of the Convention, with safeguards against victimisation. The seafarer has a right to complain directly to the master, and to the flag State and other authorities.
  • 5.1.6 - Marine casualties, requiring official inquiry into serious casualties involving loss of life or injury.

5. Limb (c) - port State and labour-supplying responsibilities

  • 5.2.1 - Inspections in port. A valid Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC are prima facie evidence of compliance, so the ordinary inspection is limited to checking them. Where there are clear grounds - the documents are not produced or are not valid, there are grounds to believe conditions do not conform, or a complaint has been received - a more detailed inspection may be carried out. Deficiencies must be rectified; where they are serious, or repeated, or the subject of a valid complaint, the ship may be detained until they are.
  • 5.2.2 - Onshore seafarer complaint-handling procedures. A seafarer may complain ashore in any port, and the port State must have a procedure to handle it, seeking resolution at ship level first and reporting to the flag State where it cannot be resolved.

A Member is also responsible for the seafarer recruitment and placement services operating in its territory, and for the social security protection of seafarers ordinarily resident there. This is the limb of Title 5 that reaches the manning agent, and it is what makes the no-fees rule in Regulation 1.4 enforceable at source.

6. Limb (c) - the two provisions that make the system work

  • "No more favourable treatment" - Article V(7). A Member must ensure that ships flying the flag of a State that has not ratified the Convention receive no more favourable treatment than ships of a State that has. Without this, non-ratification would be a commercial advantage; with it, a ship cannot escape the Convention by choosing a flag.
  • Substantial equivalence - Article VI. A Member may implement Part A of the Code by measures substantially equivalent to it, provided it is satisfied they give full effect to the general purpose and objective of the provision. This is what made near-universal ratification achievable, and it is also the seam through which national divergence enters.

The honest closing observation on limb (c): the Convention's enforcement is strong on documents and weaker on conditions. A certificate and a DMLC are prima facie evidence, and an inspection that finds the papers in order will usually go no further. That is why the complaint procedures at 5.1.5 and 5.2.2, and the port State's power to act on a complaint, matter more in practice than the certificate does.

7. Limb (d) - abandonment, and when it arises

Abandonment is dealt with under Regulation 2.5 and Standard A2.5.2, introduced by the 2014 amendments, which are in force at this sitting. Before them the Convention gave a right to repatriation with no guarantee that anyone solvent would pay for it - which is precisely the position that produced the problem.

A seafarer is regarded as abandoned where, in breach of the Convention or of the employment agreement, the shipowner does any of three things:

  1. Fails to cover the cost of the seafarer's repatriation;
  2. Leaves the seafarer without the necessary maintenance and support - which in practice means food, water, fuel, power, medical care and accommodation;
  3. Otherwise unilaterally severs ties with the seafarer, including failure to pay contractual wages for a period of at least two months.

Two features of that definition are worth pointing out. It does not require the shipowner to have disappeared - a shipowner who is present, contactable and simply not paying can abandon a crew. And the third limb makes two months of unpaid wages a sufficient trigger on its own, which converts a slow financial failure into a defined event with defined remedies.

8. Limb (d) - the financial security system

  • Every ship to which the Convention applies must carry financial security - typically provided by the P&I club - to assist seafarers in the event of abandonment.
  • A certificate or other documentary evidence of that security, issued by the provider, must be posted in a conspicuous place on board where seafarers can see it, and must state the provider's contact details.
  • The seafarer, or a nominated representative, has direct access to the provider - the claim does not go through the shipowner, which is the point, since the shipowner is by definition the party who has failed.
  • The security must cover outstanding wages and other entitlements up to a defined limit, the cost of repatriation, and the seafarer's essential needs - which the Convention itself enumerates as including adequate food, clothing where necessary, accommodation, drinking water, essential fuel for survival on board, necessary medical care, and any other reasonable costs.
  • Assistance must be provided promptly, and the security may not be cancelled without prior notice to the flag State.

9. Limb (d) - why the numbers rise, and what the institutions do

  • Single-ship companies and opaque ownership. The corporate owner of one ageing vessel can be allowed to fail at no cost to the beneficial owner, leaving nothing to pursue.
  • Ageing tonnage in marginal trades, where a modest fall in freight rates makes the ship uneconomic and the cheapest option is to stop paying.
  • Arrest and detention. A ship arrested for debt or detained for deficiencies stops earning immediately while her costs continue, and crews are frequently abandoned on board a vessel they may not lawfully leave.
  • Sanctions, war risk and trade disruption, which can strand a ship and her crew with no lawful means of trading or of being paid.
  • Registries and manning chains with weak oversight, together with recruitment through unlicensed agents in States with limited enforcement capacity.
  • Seafarers' weak bargaining position - fear of blacklisting, debts incurred to obtain the job, and reluctance to leave the ship because leaving may forfeit the wage claim.
  • And a reporting effect that should be acknowledged rather than ignored: part of the increase reflects better reporting through the joint database, not only more incidents.
BodyRole
ILOOwns the Convention and its amendment machinery through the Special Tripartite Committee; maintains, jointly with IMO, the database of reported abandonment incidents; provides the standards against which flag and port States act
IMOJoint custodian of the database with ILO, and the forum through which the port State control machinery that detects abandonment operates
ITFNot a regulator but the most effective practical actor - its inspectorate boards ships, identifies unpaid wages, negotiates with owners and charterers, funds food and repatriation in emergencies, and pursues wage claims, backed by industrial action and by ITF-approved collective agreements
Flag StateMust be notified before security is cancelled; must facilitate repatriation and, ultimately, bears responsibility where the shipowner does not act
Port StateDetects the situation, may detain, handles the onshore complaint under 5.2.2, and in practice provides the humanitarian response first
P&I club / financial security providerPays - wages up to the limit, repatriation and essential needs - on direct application by the seafarer

The structural point to close on: the 2014 amendments did not create a right; they created a payer. The right to repatriation existed long before them. What was missing was certainty that someone solvent stood behind it, and a certificate on the bulkhead with a telephone number the seafarer can use without the shipowner's permission is what supplies it.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks, four limbs, no printed sub-marks - so the examiner has left the allocation open and will mark on balance. Four roughly equal parts is the safe reading, and the discipline that matters is not overrunning limb (a). Title 1 is the easiest material in the question and candidates write half the paper on it, then run out of time at limb (d), which is the limb that actually distinguishes answers. Budget the time before you start writing.

Get the architecture down in three lines

Articles, Regulations, Code Part A Standards (mandatory), Code Part B Guidelines (not mandatory). Five Titles: 1 minimum requirements, 2 conditions of employment, 3 accommodation and food, 4 health and welfare, 5 compliance and enforcement. This question is Titles 1, 2, 5 plus Regulation 2.5. Writing that opening paragraph makes every subsequent limb look like a part of a system rather than a separate recollection - and it is three lines.

It is an ILO instrument

Not IMO. Adopted by the International Labour Conference in 2006, in force 20 August 2013, and amended by the Special Tripartite Committee under Article XIII - not by MSC or MEPC tacit acceptance. Candidates who describe MLC amendments arriving by tacit acceptance have confused it with SOLAS. One sentence on this is cheap and it is the kind of thing that is asked orally.

The temporal point that governs this whole question

At a June 2024 sitting the Convention stands as amended through the 2018 set. The 2022 amendments entered into force on 23 December 2024 - six months after this examination. So do not write, as current obligations, the things the 2022 set introduced: appropriately sized personal protective equipment, free drinking water, the strengthened food and catering provisions, social connectivity and internet access, the repatriation of the remains of a deceased seafarer, or the reporting duties added around the abandonment database. If you have revised from a later syllabus or read a later MIW answer, those will be sitting in your memory as law. Here they are not.

The good news, and the thing to be sure of: the 2014 amendments are in force and have been for years. Limb (d) - the whole abandonment financial security system - is operative law at this sitting, not future law. So limb (d) can be written in full and with confidence.

Hours of rest - state it as an alternative

This is where candidates lose marks by giving half the rule. The Member fixes either maximum hours of work - 14 in 24 and 72 in 7 - or minimum hours of rest - 10 in 24 and 77 in 7. Under the rest option: no more than two periods, one of at least six hours, and no more than fourteen hours between consecutive rest periods. Learn all six numbers as one block, because the examiner will ask for the one you left out.

The other numbers in Title 1 and 2

  • Minimum age 16; night work under 18 restricted; hazardous work under 18 prohibited.
  • Medical certificate 2 years, or 1 year under 18; colour vision 6 years; expiry at sea continues to the next port, up to 3 months.
  • Wages at intervals no greater than monthly.
  • Leave at 2.5 calendar days per month of employment.
  • Maximum service on board before repatriation: less than 12 months.
  • MLC certification at 500 GT and above on international voyages; certificate 5 years with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary.
  • Abandonment trigger: wages unpaid for at least 2 months.

The definition of abandonment - three limbs, and the two subtleties

Fails to pay for repatriation; leaves the seafarer without necessary maintenance and support; or otherwise unilaterally severs ties, including failure to pay contractual wages for at least two months. Then the two points that show understanding: the shipowner does not have to have disappeared - a present, contactable owner who simply stops paying has abandoned the crew; and two months of unpaid wages is a sufficient trigger on its own, which turns a slow financial failure into a defined event with defined remedies.

The sentence to end limb (d) with

The 2014 amendments did not create a right - they created a payer. The right to repatriation was always there. What was missing was a solvent party standing behind it and a way for the seafarer to reach that party without the shipowner's cooperation. A certificate posted on the bulkhead with a number the seafarer can call is the whole reform in one object.

Do not quote a ratification figure

The temptation is to write "over 100 States and more than 96 per cent of world tonnage". Those figures are from a later date than this sitting and cannot be carried back honestly. Say that ratification is very wide, covering the great majority of world tonnage, and move on. It costs nothing and it cannot be wrong. The same applies to abandonment statistics - name the joint ILO/IMO database as the mechanism and give no number.

Common mistakes

  • Spending half the answer on Title 1 and leaving limb (d) unwritten.
  • Calling the MLC an IMO convention.
  • Giving the hours-of-rest limits without the hours-of-work alternative, or giving four of the six numbers.
  • Treating Part B Guidelines as mandatory. Part A Standards are mandatory; Part B is not.
  • Saying the DMLC is one document. Part I is the flag State's; Part II is the shipowner's.
  • Omitting "no more favourable treatment", which is what stops flag-shopping out of the Convention.
  • Defining abandonment as the shipowner disappearing. He need not disappear.
  • Writing 2022-amendment content - PPE sizing, drinking water, connectivity - as law at a June 2024 sitting.
  • Quoting a ratification count or an abandonment statistic.

Examiner traps

  • "Who signs the seafarers' employment agreement?" The seafarer and the shipowner - not the master, and not the manning agent.
  • "Who draws up DMLC Part II?" The shipowner. Part I is the flag State's.
  • "Can a seafarer be charged a placement fee?" No - other than the statutory medical certificate, the national seafarer's book and a passport.
  • "What is the maximum period of service before repatriation?" Less than twelve months.
  • "Is a valid Maritime Labour Certificate conclusive?" No - it is prima facie evidence. Clear grounds or a complaint reopen the inspection.
  • "What does 'no more favourable treatment' mean?" A non-ratifying flag gets no advantage - Article V(7).
  • "Who pays when a crew is abandoned?" The financial security provider, usually the P&I club, on direct application by the seafarer.
  • "Where is the abandonment certificate kept?" Posted in a conspicuous place on board, with the provider's contact details - go and look at yours.
  • "Is two months' unpaid wages abandonment?" Yes, on its own, under the third limb of the definition.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Show me your seafarers' employment agreement and the record of hours of rest for your department.
  • Where is the financial security certificate posted, and who would you telephone?
  • Your ship has not been paid for ten weeks and the owner is not answering. What are your options?
  • What is in DMLC Part II and who wrote it?
  • How does a port State inspector deal with an MLC complaint from a rating?
  • Which Title covers accommodation and food, and what would an inspector look at?

Memory framework

Four limbs, four Titles, four numbers. Limb (a) is Title 1 - AGE, MEDICAL, TRAINING, AGENCY. Limb (b) is Title 2 - the AGREEMENT and everything hanging off it (wages, hours, leave, repatriation). Limb (c) is Title 5 - FLAG certifies, PORT inspects, SUPPLY State regulates the agent. Limb (d) is Reg 2.5 / Standard A2.5.2 - a CERTIFICATE, a PHONE NUMBER, and a PAYER. The four numbers to carry in: 16 (age), 2 years (medical), 10 in 24 / 77 in 7 (rest), 2 months (abandonment).

Regulation and source map

  • Articles - V(7) no more favourable treatment; VI substantial equivalence; XIII the Special Tripartite Committee.
  • Title 1 - Reg 1.1 minimum age; 1.2 medical certificate; 1.3 training and qualifications; 1.4 recruitment and placement.
  • Title 2 - Reg 2.1 seafarers' employment agreement and Standard A2.1; 2.2 wages; 2.3 hours of work and rest; 2.4 leave; 2.5 repatriation; 2.6 compensation for ship's loss; 2.7 manning; 2.8 career development.
  • Regulation 2.5 and Standard A2.5.2 - abandonment and financial security, introduced by the 2014 amendments, IN FORCE at this sitting.
  • Titles 3 and 4 - accommodation, food and catering; health protection, medical care, welfare and social security.
  • Title 5 - 5.1.1 to 5.1.6 flag State; 5.2.1 and 5.2.2 port State; 5.3 labour-supplying responsibilities.
  • Joint ILO/IMO database of reported abandonment incidents; the ITF inspectorate - industry actors, not law.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

This question rests on the strongest single source in the MIW corpus. The official ILO Compendium of maritime labour instruments, Fourth revised edition, 2026 is held, and the corpus records the instrument text as public domain on the source's own statement - a materially more permissive position than the IMO publications. Even so, no provision is reproduced verbatim here; provisions are cited by number and their effect stated, as a matter of MIW house discipline.

The amendment position is the decisive fact and it is verified rather than assumed. The corpus amendment table, checked against the Compendium, records the 2014 amendments in force, the 2016 set in force 8 January 2019, the 2018 set in force 26 December 2020, the 2022 set in force 23 December 2024 and the 2025 set not yet in force. This examination sat in June 2024, so the operative Convention is the MLC as amended through the 2018 set, and nothing the 2022 amendments introduced appears here as law. That is the exact inversion of what the corpus register says for current law - the register is maintained for the present and records the 2022 set as the latest in force - and getting the direction right is the single most important temporal judgement on this paper.

No ratification count and no tonnage percentage is asserted. The corpus figure - 110 ratifications, more than 96 per cent of world gross tonnage - is stated 'as of September 2025', fifteen months after this sitting, and cannot honestly be carried backwards. No abandonment statistic is asserted either; the joint ILO/IMO database is named as the mechanism and MIW holds no extract from it.

The domestic statute at this sitting is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, under which India gives effect to the MLC and the Directorate General of Shipping issues Maritime Labour Certificates or authorises Recognized Organizations to do so. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced 15 March 2026 and appears nowhere in this answer.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What the Convention is, and which version applies here
  2. __________(a) Limb (a) - Title 1: minimum requirements for seafarers to work on a ship
  3. __________(b) Limb (b) - Title 2: conditions of employment
  4. __________(c) Limb (c) - Title 5: flag State responsibilities
  5. __________(c) Limb (c) - port State and labour-supplying responsibilities
  6. __________(c) Limb (c) - the two provisions that make the system work
  7. __________(d) Limb (d) - abandonment, and when it arises
  8. __________(d) Limb (d) - the financial security system
  9. __________(d) Limb (d) - why the numbers rise, and what the institutions do
Flashcards 9

1 Architecture and which amendment set applies. 2 Limb (a) Title 1 - age, medical, training, recruitment. 3 Limb (b) Title 2 - the employment agreement and everything hanging off it. 4 Limb (c) Title 5 - flag State, port State, labour-supplying, plus no more favourable treatment. 5-6 Limb (d) - the three-limb definition of abandonment, then financial security, causes and institutions.

Four limbs with no printed sub-marks means equal weighting and strict time discipline. Title 1 is the easiest material and is what overruns, leaving limb (d) - the discriminating limb - unwritten.

1.1 minimum age - 16; night work and hazardous work restricted under 18. 1.2 medical certificate - maximum 2 years, 1 year under 18, colour vision 6 years; STCW certificates accepted. 1.3 training - STCW certification plus completed training in personal safety on board. 1.4 recruitment and placement - no fees charged to the seafarer except the statutory medical, seafarer's book and passport; services licensed and operating a system of protection against monetary loss.

Four Regulations, four numbers. The no-fees rule is the Convention's answer to debt bondage and is the one most often found breached.

A Member fixes either maximum hours of work - not exceeding 14 in any 24-hour period and 72 in any seven-day period - or minimum hours of rest - not less than 10 in any 24-hour period and 77 in any seven-day period. Under the rest option, rest may be divided into no more than two periods, one of at least six hours, and the interval between consecutive rest periods must not exceed fourteen hours. Records are kept and the schedule posted; drills interrupting rest attract compensatory rest.

It is an alternative, not a single rule, and candidates give half of it. Learn all six numbers as one block.

Flag State (5.1) - inspection and certification, may authorise ROs; issues the Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC at 500 GT, five years with an intermediate inspection; on-board complaint procedures. Port State (5.2) - the certificate is prima facie evidence, so the ordinary inspection checks documents; clear grounds or a complaint open a more detailed inspection, and detention may follow. Labour-supplying State (5.3) - regulates the agents. Over all of it, Article V(7) no more favourable treatment.

Three actors, and the Convention deliberately does not rely on any one. Missing the labour-supplying limb loses a third of the limb.

Part I is drawn up by the flag State and identifies the national requirements implementing the Convention. Part II is drawn up by the shipowner and sets out the measures adopted to ensure ongoing compliance on that ship. Both are attached to the Maritime Labour Certificate and carried on board.

The classic trap. Candidates describe the DMLC as a single flag State document and lose the mark.

Under Standard A2.5.2 (2014 amendments), where in breach of the Convention or the employment agreement the shipowner: fails to cover the cost of repatriation; leaves the seafarer without the necessary maintenance and support; or otherwise unilaterally severs ties, including failure to pay contractual wages for at least two months. Note that the shipowner need not have disappeared, and that two months of unpaid wages is a sufficient trigger on its own.

The two subtleties are what turn a memorised definition into an understood one, and the second converts a slow financial failure into a defined event with defined remedies.

Every ship carries financial security, usually from the P&I club. A certificate is posted in a conspicuous place on board with contact details. The seafarer has direct access to the provider - the claim does not go through the shipowner, who is by definition the party that failed. It covers outstanding wages to a defined limit, repatriation and essential needs - food, accommodation, drinking water, essential fuel, medical care - and must be provided promptly.

The reform is procedural rather than substantive: the right to go home already existed, and what was added was a solvent payer the seafarer can reach unaided.

The 2022 amendments. They entered into force on 23 December 2024, six months after this examination, and introduced appropriately sized PPE, free drinking water, strengthened food and catering, social connectivity, and repatriation of a deceased seafarer's remains. The 2025 amendments are excluded absolutely. What is in force: the 2014 set (abandonment financial security), the 2016 set and the 2018 set.

This is the paper's most consequential temporal boundary, and it runs the wrong way for anyone revising from a later syllabus.

They did not create a right - they created a payer. The right to repatriation existed long before them and was worth nothing against an insolvent single-ship owner. The 2014 amendments added compulsory financial security, a certificate posted on board, and direct access to the provider without the shipowner's cooperation. They also added financial security for death or long-term disability from occupational injury.

One sentence that reframes the whole limb, and the best possible close to the answer.

Quick revision
15-second recall
ILO convention, in force 20 August 2013, amended by the Special Tripartite Committee under Article XIII. Structure: Articles, Regulations, Part A Standards mandatory, Part B Guidelines not; five Titles. (a) Title 1 - age 16, night work and hazardous work under 18 restricted; medical certificate 2 years (1 under 18, colour vision 6); STCW training plus personal safety training; recruitment free of charge to the seafarer, agencies licensed with a system of protection. (b) Title 2 - written SEA signed by seafarer and shipowner, copy on board; wages monthly with an account and allotments; hours EITHER 14 in 24 / 72 in 7 work OR 10 in 24 / 77 in 7 rest, rest in no more than two periods one of at least six hours; leave 2.5 days per month; repatriation, service under 12 months. (c) Title 5 - flag State certifies with the Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC (Part I flag, Part II shipowner) at 500 GT, five years with an intermediate inspection; port State treats the certificate as prima facie evidence and may inspect further on clear grounds or a complaint, and detain; labour-supplying States regulate the agents; Article V(7) no more favourable treatment. (d) Abandonment under Standard A2.5.2 (2014 amendments, in force): the shipowner fails to pay repatriation, leaves the seafarer without maintenance and support, or unilaterally severs ties including two months' unpaid wages - answered by financial security, a certificate posted on board, direct access, and cover for wages, repatriation and essential needs.

Critical numbers
  • Convention in force 20 August 2013; operative amendment set at this sitting is the 2018 one
  • 2022 amendments in force 23 DECEMBER 2024 - six months AFTER this sitting, so not law here

  • Medical certificate maximum 2 years; 1 year under 18; colour vision 6 years; up to 3 months' continuation where it expires at sea
  • Hours: EITHER 14 in 24 and 72 in 7 of work, OR 10 in 24 and 77 in 7 of rest; rest in no more than two periods, one of at least 6 hours, interval not exceeding 14 hours
  • Leave 2.5 calendar days per month of employment; maximum service before repatriation less than 12 months
  • Maritime Labour Certificate at 500 GT and above on international voyages; 5 years, intermediate inspection between the 2nd and 3rd anniversary

Critical regulation
Standard A2.5.2, introduced by the 2014 amendments and in force at this sitting, is the provision that makes limb (d) answerable - it supplies the three-limb definition of abandonment and the financial security system behind it. Read with Regulation 2.5 on repatriation, and with Title 5 for the certification and inspection machinery that detects the failure.
Major trap
Writing 2022-amendment content as law at a June 2024 sitting - appropriately sized PPE, free drinking water, the strengthened food and catering provisions, social connectivity and internet access, repatriation of a deceased seafarer's remains. Those entered into force on 23 December 2024, six months after this examination, and a candidate revising from a later syllabus will have them in mind as current. Second trap: giving only one half of the hours rule - it is an alternative between maximum work and minimum rest, and the examiner will ask for the half you omitted. Third: saying DMLC is one document - Part I is the flag State's, Part II is the shipowner's. Fourth: defining abandonment as the shipowner disappearing - he need not; two months of unpaid wages is enough on its own. Fifth: quoting a ratification figure - the available number is dated fifteen months after this sitting and cannot be carried back. Sixth: calling the MLC an IMO convention.

Understand this first

Everything in this question falls out of one fact: a P&I club has no customers. An ordinary insurer sells you a policy and keeps the profit; if the year goes badly, that is the insurer's problem. A club is an association of shipowners who have agreed to carry each other's liabilities, so there is nobody outside the group to absorb a bad year. That single structural difference explains all of limb (a). It is why the money is called a call and not a premium - a call is what a member of an association contributes, and it can be adjusted after the event, which a premium cannot. It is why there is a supplementary call at all: if the year cost more than expected, the members pay the difference, because there is nobody else. It is why a release call exists when you leave: liability claims take years to mature, so the years you were a member are still open and still capable of costing money. And it is why the cover has no fixed sum insured - the members decided to cover each other's liabilities, whatever they turn out to be, rather than to buy a stated amount of protection. Limb (b) is then just a division of labour: your hull policy looks after your ship, and the club looks after what you owe to everyone else - people and the world under protection, cargo under indemnity. And limb (c) is the part worth understanding rather than memorising: what the club actually sells is not money, it is control and credit - a lawyer and surveyor on the quay within hours, and a piece of paper that gets your ship out of arrest without you finding cash in a foreign port.

Knowledge map
P&I Clubs - Funding, Cover and Claims Handling
  1. (a)
    1. 1What a P&I club is
      • A mutual association of shipowners, charterers and operators covering third-party liabilities from the operation of their ships
      • The members are the club - no external shareholders, no profit extracted
      • Surplus retained for the members; deficit met by them - hence calls, not premiums
    2. 2Why it is not ordinary marine insurance
      • Premium against call; property loss against legal liability; fixed sum insured against none
      • Narrow defined perils against very wide cover, extensible by the board's omnibus discretion
      • Profit to shareholders against surplus retained for members
    3. 3How the club collects funds - the tiers
      • Advance call - on entry for the policy year, in instalments; the estimated cost collected up front
      • Supplementary call - where claims and expenses exceed advance calls plus investment income. Not a penalty
      • Release call - on leaving, buying out exposure to future supplementary calls on open policy years
    4. 4Above the club - the International Group
      • The club's own retention
      • The International Group Pooling Agreement - claims above retention shared among the participating clubs
      • The Group's collective reinsurance, and above it an overspill layer met by members through an overspill call
  2. (b)
    1. 5"Protection" - liabilities to people and to the world
      • Crew - injury, illness, death, medical, hospital and funeral, repatriation, personal effects, wages in defined circumstances
      • Other persons - stevedores, pilots, visitors, passengers, third parties
      • Collision - the proportion not covered by hull, which classically covers only three-fourths
    2. 6"Indemnity" - liability to cargo
      • Loss, damage, shortage and contamination of cargo carried, from breach of the contract of carriage
      • Cargo's proportion of general average unrecoverable because of the owner's breach - the link to Q8
      • Liabilities under approved bills of lading and charterparty terms
    3. 7The boundary - what P&I does not cover, and why
      • NOT damage to the member's own ship - that is hull and machinery
      • NOT the member's own trading loss - delay and loss of hire is a separate cover. P&I covers liabilities, not your own losses
      • NOT war risks; NOT liabilities under unapproved contracts; NOT where trading in breach of class or terms of entry
  3. (c)
    1. 8How the club handles a claim
      • Takes control of the defence - appoints or approves lawyers, surveyors and experts; no admission or settlement without the club
      • Works through correspondents - attendance on board, statements while the crew are aboard, surveys and joint surveys
      • Provides security - the letter of undertaking that prevents or lifts arrest, in place of cash or a bank guarantee
    2. 9The procedure for filing a claim
      • Notify immediately - club and local correspondent; rules impose time limits and late notice can defeat the claim. Notify on suspicion
      • Preserve and gather evidence at once - logs, engine and bridge data, Oil Record Book, crew statements, photographs, damaged items, cargo and stowage records, soundings, SMS records
      • Arrange survey, and a joint survey where cargo or a third party is involved

FOUR CALLS: advance, supplementary, release, deductible (3); above them POOL, REINSURANCE, OVERSPILL (4). TWO WORDS: protection is people and the world (5), indemnity is cargo (6).
