Cover55 core points beneath them

Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 55 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What a P&I club is
  • A mutual association of shipowners, charterers and operators covering third-party liabilities from the operation of their ships
  • The members are the club - no external shareholders, no profit extracted
  • Surplus retained for the members; deficit met by them - hence calls, not premiums
  • Liability cover, with no fixed sum insured
  • Governed by a board drawn from the membership; run by professional managers
  • Twelve clubs participate in the International Group - phrase it as a current figure, not a permanent one
2. Why it is not ordinary marine insurance
  • Premium against call; property loss against legal liability; fixed sum insured against none
  • Narrow defined perils against very wide cover, extensible by the board's omnibus discretion
  • Profit to shareholders against surplus retained for members
  • The three the corpus itself names: mutuality, breadth of cover, absence of a fixed sum insured
3. How the club collects funds - the tiers
  • Advance call - on entry for the policy year, in instalments; the estimated cost collected up front
  • Supplementary call - where claims and expenses exceed advance calls plus investment income. Not a penalty
  • Release call - on leaving, buying out exposure to future supplementary calls on open policy years
  • Say why years stay open: liability claims take years to mature
  • Deductible - the member's first amount on every claim; suppresses small-claim frequency
  • Rated on entered tonnage, then ship type, trade and area, crew nationality and terms, management standard - and above all the claims record
  • One Indian line: Admiralty Act 2017 s.4(1)(s) makes an unpaid mutual insurance call a maritime claim supporting arrest
4. Above the club - the International Group
  • The club's own retention
  • The International Group Pooling Agreement - claims above retention shared among the participating clubs
  • The Group's collective reinsurance, and above it an overspill layer met by members through an overspill call
  • Why it matters: cover of a magnitude no single insurer would write, and a solvent target for a coastal State after a major casualty
  • Give no figures - retentions and layers are reset annually
5. "Protection" - liabilities to people and to the world
  • Crew - injury, illness, death, medical, hospital and funeral, repatriation, personal effects, wages in defined circumstances
  • Other persons - stevedores, pilots, visitors, passengers, third parties
  • Collision - the proportion not covered by hull, which classically covers only three-fourths
  • Fixed and floating objects - piers, jetties, berths, buoys, cables, pipelines, navigational aids
  • Pollution - liability, clean-up and preventive measures beyond the hull cover
  • Wreck removal where compulsory by law
  • Stowaways, refugees, persons rescued at sea; quarantine and disinfection
  • Fines - pollution, cargo shortage, immigration and customs, safety breaches, so far as the rules permit
  • Towage and salvage liabilities under approved contracts; legal costs throughout
6. "Indemnity" - liability to cargo
  • Loss, damage, shortage and contamination of cargo carried, from breach of the contract of carriage
  • Cargo's proportion of general average unrecoverable because of the owner's breach - the link to Q8
  • Liabilities under approved bills of lading and charterparty terms
  • Disposal of damaged or unclaimed cargo; through and transhipment liabilities
7. The boundary - what P&I does not cover, and why
  • NOT damage to the member's own ship - that is hull and machinery
  • NOT the member's own trading loss - delay and loss of hire is a separate cover. P&I covers liabilities, not your own losses
  • NOT war risks; NOT liabilities under unapproved contracts; NOT where trading in breach of class or terms of entry
  • The corollary: the club issues the blue cards behind the CLC and Bunkers certificates, the MLC financial security, and the Nairobi wreck-removal certificate
  • No entry, no certificates, no trading - link to Q1 and Q6
8. How the club handles a claim
  • Takes control of the defence - appoints or approves lawyers, surveyors and experts; no admission or settlement without the club
  • Works through correspondents - attendance on board, statements while the crew are aboard, surveys and joint surveys
  • Provides security - the letter of undertaking that prevents or lifts arrest, in place of cash or a bank guarantee
  • 'Pay to be paid' - the member discharges first and is indemnified; in practice the club pays claimants directly, especially seafarers and coastal States
  • Utmost good faith - full disclosure, and nothing done to prejudice the club's position
9. The procedure for filing a claim
  • Notify immediately - club and local correspondent; rules impose time limits and late notice can defeat the claim. Notify on suspicion
  • Preserve and gather evidence at once - logs, engine and bridge data, Oil Record Book, crew statements, photographs, damaged items, cargo and stowage records, soundings, SMS records
  • Arrange survey, and a joint survey where cargo or a third party is involved
  • Do not admit liability, do not settle; sign nothing beyond a factual receipt or protest
  • Note protest and issue letters of protest or holding replies
  • Submit the formal claim with incident report, surveys, invoices, wage and medical records, B/L and charterparty, legal correspondence
  • Request security where arrest threatens, so the LOU can issue and the ship can sail
  • Cooperate through the defence - witnesses, documents, the club's instructions
  • Settlement and reimbursement, less the deductible, with the club pursuing recovery by subrogation under the Marine Insurance Act, 1963
  • Close on the Chief Engineer's own part: report at once, preserve the evidence, write it down while fresh, admit nothing
Model written answer

1. What a P&I club is

A Protection and Indemnity club is a mutual association of shipowners, charterers and operators, formed to cover the third-party liabilities arising from the operation of their ships. The word that governs everything else in the answer is mutual: the members are the club. There are no external shareholders, no profit is taken out, an underwriting surplus is retained for the members and a deficit is met by them.

So a club is not a company selling a product to a customer. It is a group of shipowners sharing each other's liabilities, governed by a board of directors drawn from the membership and administered day to day by professional managers. Twelve clubs participate in the International Group, through which the largest claims are shared - and that figure moves with mergers, so it is worth writing as "the twelve clubs of the International Group" rather than as a permanent fact.

2. Why it is not ordinary marine insurance

Hull and machinery / ordinary marine insuranceP&I club
NatureA commercial insurer selling cover for profitA mutual - the assureds own the association
What is paidA premium, fixed in advanceA call - a member's contribution, which can be adjusted after the event
What is coveredLoss of or damage to the insured propertyThe member's legal liabilities to third parties
LimitA fixed sum insuredNo fixed sum insured - the liability incurred, subject to the club's rules and to any applicable limitation
BreadthDefined perils, narrowly drawnVery wide, and extensible by the board's omnibus discretion
SurplusProfit to shareholdersRetained for the members

The three differences the corpus itself identifies are mutuality, breadth of cover, and the absence of a fixed sum insured, and those are the three to write if only one line is available.

3. How the club collects funds - the tiers

This is the second half of limb (a), and it is answered in tiers because that is what the funding structure is.

  1. Advance call. Levied on entry for the policy year and usually paid in instalments. This is the estimated cost of the year, collected up front.
  2. Supplementary call. Levied if the year's claims and expenses exceed the advance calls plus investment income. It is not a penalty - it is the mutual mechanism operating exactly as designed, the members carrying the year between them.
  3. Release call. Payable by a member leaving the club, buying out his exposure to future supplementary calls on the policy years still open. Policy years are kept open for several years because liability claims take years to mature - which is the whole reason a release call exists.
  4. Deductible. The member bears an agreed first amount of every claim. Its purpose is not revenue but behaviour: it suppresses small-claim frequency and keeps the member's own claims-handling discipline engaged.

The advance call is assessed on the member's entered tonnage, and then rated for the ship type, the trade and area of operation, the crew's nationality and terms of employment, the standard of management - and, dominating all of them, the member's own claims record. A club is a mutual, but it is not a subsidy: a member with a poor record pays for it.

Indian law recognises this machinery expressly, and it is worth one line because it makes the answer an Indian one. The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 lists at section 4(1)(s) "insurance premium (including mutual insurance calls)" among the maritime claims for which a vessel may be arrested. An unpaid call is therefore not merely a debt - it is a claim that can put the ship under arrest.

4. Above the club - the International Group

No single mutual could carry a very large casualty, so above the club sit three further layers:

  • The club's own retention - the amount each club keeps for its own account.
  • The International Group Pooling Agreement - claims above that retention are shared among the participating clubs, so a single catastrophic claim is met by the whole group rather than by one club.
  • The Group's collective reinsurance, placed in the market for the whole Group, and above that a further overspill layer met by the members themselves through an overspill call.

This structure is the reason a shipowner can obtain liability cover of a magnitude no ordinary insurer would write, and the reason a coastal State facing a major pollution casualty has somebody solvent to claim against. No figures are given here for the retention, the pool layers, the reinsurance attachment or the overspill limit, because they are reset every policy year; quoting one is the surest way to be wrong in an oral.

5. "Protection" - liabilities to people and to the world

The two halves of the club's name are two halves of the cover, and the division is genuinely useful: protection is everything except cargo, indemnity is cargo.

  • Crew - personal injury, illness and death; medical, hospital and funeral expenses; repatriation; loss of personal effects; and wages in defined circumstances such as after a casualty.
  • Other persons - injury to or death of stevedores, pilots, visitors, passengers and other third parties.
  • Collision liability - the proportion of collision liability not covered by the hull and machinery policy, which classically covers only three-fourths of running-down liability, together with liabilities excluded from the hull cover.
  • Fixed and floating objects - damage to piers, jetties, berths, buoys, cables, pipelines and navigational aids.
  • Pollution - liability for oil and other pollution, clean-up costs and the cost of preventive measures, beyond what the hull policy reaches.
  • Wreck removal - the cost of raising, removing, destroying, lighting or marking the wreck where that is compulsory by law.
  • Stowaways, refugees and persons rescued at sea - maintenance, deviation and repatriation costs.
  • Quarantine and disinfection expenses arising from infectious disease on board.
  • Fines - for pollution, for cargo shortage or overlanding, for immigration, customs and smuggling breaches, and for breaches of safety or navigation rules, so far as the rules permit and subject to the board's discretion.
  • Towage and salvage liabilities under approved contracts, and liabilities under approved indemnities.
  • Legal costs of investigating, defending or settling any of the above.

6. "Indemnity" - liability to cargo

  • Loss of, damage to, shortage of and contamination of cargo carried, arising from breach of the contract of carriage.
  • Cargo's proportion of general average where it cannot be recovered from cargo interests because of a breach of the contract of carriage by the owner - which is the direct link to Q8 on this paper.
  • Liabilities under approved bills of lading and charterparty terms, and under contracts approved by the club.
  • The cost of disposing of damaged or unclaimed cargo, and liabilities arising from through and transhipment carriage.

7. The boundary - what P&I does not cover, and why

This paragraph is what raises an answer above a list, because it shows the logic of the cover rather than its contents.

  • Damage to the member's own ship - that is the hull and machinery policy's subject, not the club's.
  • The member's own trading loss. Delay and loss of hire after a casualty are the owner's own economic loss, not a liability to anyone, and fall to a separate loss-of-hire cover if it has been bought. This is the commonest misconception on the topic: P&I covers liabilities, not the owner's own losses.
  • War risks, which are separately insured.
  • Liabilities arising from unapproved contracts, or from terms more onerous than the club permits - the club underwrites the member's liability, not the member's willingness to accept anyone's terms.
  • Liabilities arising from the ship trading in breach of her class, certification or the terms of entry.

And the corollary that is the club's real commercial function alongside paying claims: because the club stands behind the member's liabilities, it also underwrites the member's compulsory insurance certificates - the blue cards that produce the CLC 1992 and Bunkers Convention certificates, the MLC 2006 financial security for repatriation, abandonment and contractual claims, and the wreck-removal certificate under the Nairobi Convention. Without a club entry a ship cannot obtain those certificates, and without them she cannot trade. See Q1 and Q6 on this paper.

8. How the club handles a claim

A club is not a passive payer. Its handling has three characteristics that distinguish it from an ordinary indemnity insurer:

  • It takes control of the defence. The club appoints or approves lawyers, surveyors and experts, and the member must not admit liability or settle without the club's agreement. Doing so can prejudice the cover.
  • It works through a worldwide network of correspondents. The correspondent in the port of the incident attends the ship, takes statements while the crew are still on board and their memory is fresh, arranges surveys and joint surveys with cargo interests, and advises the master immediately - which is why the correspondent's contact details are on board and why the master should reach them before anyone else.
  • It provides security to prevent or lift an arrest. This is the club's single most valuable practical service. Rather than a cash deposit or a bank guarantee, the club issues a letter of undertaking to the claimant, promising to satisfy any judgment or award up to a stated sum. That releases the ship, or prevents her being detained at all - and under the Admiralty Act 2017, arrest in rem is a real and immediate risk in an Indian port.

Two governing principles sit behind all of it. Pay to be paid: the classic mutual principle is that the member must first discharge the liability and is then indemnified, although clubs exercise discretion and pay directly in practice, particularly where a seafarer or a coastal State is the claimant. And utmost good faith: the member must disclose fully and must not prejudice the club's position - which is what makes prompt and complete notification a condition of the cover rather than a courtesy.

9. The procedure for filing a claim

  1. Notify immediately. The master or the Company notifies the club - directly and through the local correspondent - as soon as an incident occurs or a claim is threatened. Club rules impose notification time limits and late notification can prejudice or defeat the claim. Notify on suspicion; do not wait for certainty.
  2. Preserve and gather the evidence, at once. Log entries, engine and bridge data, the Oil Record Book, statements from the crew involved, photographs, damaged items, cargo condition and stowage records, tank soundings, and the relevant SMS records. Evidence that is not taken while the ship is in port and the crew are on board is usually lost for good.
  3. Arrange survey. The correspondent instructs a surveyor on the club's behalf; where cargo or a third party is involved, a joint survey with the other side's surveyor is arranged so the facts are agreed before the argument starts.
  4. Do not admit liability and do not settle. Sign nothing beyond a factual receipt or protest; refer every demand to the club.
  5. Note protest where the circumstances call for it, and issue the appropriate letters of protest or holding replies to third parties.
  6. Submit the formal claim to the club with the supporting documentation - the incident report, survey reports, invoices, wage and medical records, the bill of lading and charterparty, and legal correspondence.
  7. Request security where arrest is threatened or has occurred, so the club can issue its letter of undertaking and the ship can sail.
  8. Cooperate through the defence. The club conducts negotiation, mediation, arbitration or litigation; the member provides witnesses and documents and follows the club's instructions.
  9. Settlement and reimbursement. The club settles the third-party claim or reimburses the member, less the deductible; and where the loss was another party's fault the club pursues recovery by subrogation, stepping into the member's rights - a principle of the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 in Indian law.

The Chief Engineer's part in that sequence is narrow and decisive: report immediately, preserve the machinery evidence and the records before anything is disturbed, write down what happened while it is fresh, and admit nothing. Most claims that fail on the facts fail because the evidence was not taken in the first twenty-four hours.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

5 + 5 + 6, and the marks tell you where the writing goes. Limb (a) is two questions - what is a club, and how does it collect money - so the funding tiers must be visible and not folded into a general description of mutuality. Limb (b) is a classification question: the marks are in placing risks correctly on either side of the protection / indemnity line, not in the length of the list. Limb (c) carries the most marks and is the limb most often written thinnest, because candidates know what a club is and have never thought about what happens after an incident. Give limb (c) the most space.

One word governs limb (a)

Mutual. The members are the club - no shareholders, no profit extracted, surplus retained and deficit met by the members. Every other feature follows: calls not premiums, because a call is what a member of an association contributes and can be adjusted after the event; no fixed sum insured, because the club covers the liability incurred rather than a stated value; and very wide cover, extensible by the board's omnibus discretion, because the members decide what the association covers.

The four tiers - and what they are each for

TierWhenPurpose
Advance callOn entry, for the policy year, in instalmentsThe estimated cost of the year, collected up front
Supplementary callIf claims and expenses exceed advance calls plus investment incomeThe mutual mechanism working as designed - not a penalty
Release callOn leaving the clubBuys out exposure to future supplementary calls on open policy years
DeductibleOn every claimSuppresses small-claim frequency and keeps the member's own discipline engaged

Say why policy years stay open - liability claims take years to mature - because that single sentence explains the release call, which is otherwise the tier candidates cannot account for.

What the call is rated on

Entered tonnage first, then ship type, trade and area of operation, crew nationality and terms, standard of management - and above all the member's own claims record. A mutual is not a subsidy.

The Indian point that costs one line and earns more

The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 lists at section 4(1)(s) "insurance premium (including mutual insurance calls)" as a maritime claim. So an unpaid call can support the arrest of the vessel. In an Indian examination, one sentence naming an Indian statute that recognises the exact mechanism you have just described is worth more than another paragraph on mutuality.

Limb (b) - the clean dividing line

Protection is people and the world; indemnity is cargo. Learn it that way and every risk sorts itself. Crew, passengers, stevedores, pilots, collision liability beyond the hull cover, fixed and floating objects, pollution, wreck removal, stowaways, quarantine, fines and legal costs are all protection. Loss, damage, shortage and contamination of cargo, cargo's proportion of general average unrecoverable through the owner's breach, and liabilities under approved bills of lading are indemnity.

The exclusion that is nearly always got wrong

P&I covers liabilities, not the owner's own losses. Damage to your own ship is hull and machinery. Delay and loss of hire after a casualty is your own trading loss, not a liability to anybody, and falls to a separate loss-of-hire cover if it was bought. War risks are separately insured. Writing this paragraph converts a memorised list into a demonstrated understanding of what the cover is for.

The three-fourths point

Hull and machinery classically covers only three-fourths of running-down (collision) liability. The remaining one-fourth, and liabilities the hull policy excludes, fall to the club. It is the standard follow-up question on limb (b) and it takes half a sentence.

Limb (c) - the three things a club does that an insurer does not

  1. Takes control of the defence - appoints or approves lawyers and surveyors; the member must not admit liability or settle without the club.
  2. Works through correspondents - the local correspondent attends the ship, takes statements while the crew are aboard, and arranges joint surveys.
  3. Provides security - the letter of undertaking that prevents or lifts an arrest, instead of a cash deposit or bank guarantee. This is the club's most valuable practical service, and under the Admiralty Act 2017 arrest is a live risk in an Indian port.

"Pay to be paid" - name it

The classic mutual principle is that the member must first discharge the liability and is then indemnified. In practice clubs exercise discretion and pay claimants directly, especially seafarers and coastal States. Naming the principle and then noting the practice is exactly the kind of two-layer answer that scores in an oral.

The Chief Engineer's own part

Report immediately, preserve the machinery evidence and records before anything is disturbed, write down what happened while it is fresh, admit nothing. Most claims that fail on the facts fail because nobody took the evidence in the first twenty-four hours. Ending limb (c) on the candidate's own responsibility rather than on the club's procedure is a strong close.

Quote no figures

Club retentions, pool layers, reinsurance attachment points, overspill limits, oil-pollution sub-limits and deductibles are reset every policy year. Describe the structure - retention, pool, collective reinsurance, overspill - and give no numbers. The one count worth using is that the International Group comprises twelve clubs, and even that moves with mergers, so phrase it as "the twelve clubs of the International Group" rather than as a permanent truth.

Common mistakes

  • Describing a club as an insurance company. It is a mutual association owned by its members.
  • Saying "premium" instead of "call", which signals the mutuality has not been understood.
  • Answering limb (a) on what a club is and never explaining how it collects money - half the limb.
  • Omitting the release call, or being unable to say why open policy years require one.
  • Putting cargo claims under "protection".
  • Claiming P&I covers loss of hire or damage to the member's own ship.
  • Writing limb (c) as "you inform the club and they pay" - six marks, the largest limb.
  • Quoting pool limits or reinsurance figures.

Examiner traps

  • "Premium or call - and why does it matter?" A call. Because the members are the association, so the contribution can be adjusted after the event.
  • "What is a release call for?" Buying out future supplementary calls on policy years that are still open, because liability claims take years to mature.
  • "How much of the collision liability does the hull policy cover?" Classically three-fourths; the balance falls to the club.
  • "Does the club cover your loss of hire?" No - that is the owner's own trading loss, not a liability.
  • "What is a letter of undertaking and why does it matter to you?" The club's promise to satisfy a judgment, which releases the ship instead of a cash deposit - and it is what gets you out of arrest.
  • "What is a blue card?" The club's evidence of cover that produces the CLC, Bunkers and MLC financial security certificates. No entry, no certificates, no trading.
  • "What is 'pay to be paid'?" The member must discharge the liability first and is then indemnified - subject to the club's discretion in practice.
  • "An unpaid call - can the ship be arrested in India?" Yes - Admiralty Act 2017, section 4(1)(s).

Likely oral follow-up

  • Who is your club and where are the correspondent's details kept?
  • A stevedore is injured in your engine room. What do you do in the first hour?
  • Your ship is arrested for an unpaid bunker bill. How does the club get you out?
  • Which of your certificates depend on the club, and what happens if the entry lapses?
  • What is subrogation, and give an example from your own experience.
  • Why does the club care whether you signed the bill of lading as presented?

Memory framework

MUTUAL - four calls - two words - three services. MUTUAL: members are the club, so calls not premiums and no fixed sum insured. FOUR CALLS: advance, supplementary, release, deductible - then pool, reinsurance, overspill above. TWO WORDS: protection is people and the world; indemnity is cargo. THREE SERVICES in limb (c): controls the defence, sends the correspondent, issues the letter of undertaking.

Regulation and source map

  • Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 - section 4(1) maritime claims, including (s) insurance premium and mutual insurance calls; section 5 arrest in rem; section 11 wrongful arrest.
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 (India) - the governing marine insurance statute at this sitting; utmost good faith, insurable interest, subrogation.
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 (India) - the governing merchant shipping statute at this sitting.
  • CLC 1992 and the Bunkers Convention 2001 - compulsory insurance and the blue card issued by the club.
  • MLC 2006 Standard A2.5.2 and Standard A4.2.1 - financial security for repatriation and abandonment, and for contractual claims.
  • Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention 2007 - compulsory wreck-removal insurance at 300 GT and above.
  • International Group of P&I Clubs - pooling agreement and collective reinsurance. Industry structure, not law.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

This is a question about market practice rather than about a convention, and it is sourced accordingly. The MIW corpus classifies P&I material as industry reference from igpandi.org, the International Group's own official site, and that supports the mutual structure, the calls, the pooling agreement and the collective reinsurance. Two propositions used here come from the corpus directly rather than from recall: that P&I differs from ordinary insurance by mutuality, breadth of cover and the absence of a fixed sum insured; and that delay and loss of hire fall to specific covers and are generally not P&I.

No monetary figure is asserted anywhere - no retention, pool layer, reinsurance attachment, overspill limit or deductible. These are reset every policy year and are club-specific, and they are the only genuinely decaying element of the topic. The single count used, the number of clubs in the International Group, should be checked against the Group's current membership before you rely on it, because it moves with mergers. No club is named and no club's rules are cited or quoted; club rules differ and are revised annually.

The Indian frame was verified for this sitting rather than assumed. The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 and the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 were all in force in June 2024, and remained so at later sittings, so the frame transfers - but it transfers because it was checked. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced 15 March 2026 and appears nowhere in this answer. The subrogation section number in the Marine Insurance Act carries the corpus's own VERIFY flag and is stated with that qualification.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) What a P&I club is
  2. __________(a) Why it is not ordinary marine insurance
  3. __________(a) How the club collects funds - the tiers
  4. __________(a) Above the club - the International Group
  5. __________(b) "Protection" - liabilities to people and to the world
  6. __________(b) "Indemnity" - liability to cargo
  7. __________(b) The boundary - what P&I does not cover, and why
  8. __________(c) How the club handles a claim
  9. __________(c) The procedure for filing a claim
Flashcards 9

1 What a club is - the mutual. 2 Contrast with ordinary insurance. 3 The four funding tiers and what they are rated on. 4 The International Group layers above (1-4 are limb a). 5 Protection. 6 Indemnity. 7 The boundary and the blue cards (5-7 are limb b). 8 How the club handles a claim. 9 The filing procedure (8-9 are limb c).

Limb (c) carries the most marks at six and is the limb candidates write thinnest. Two sections for it is a deliberate corrective.

A mutual association of shipowners, charterers and operators covering the third-party liabilities arising from operating their ships. The members are the club - no external shareholders and no profit extracted, so a surplus is retained for them and a deficit is met by them. That single fact produces everything else: calls not premiums (a call can be adjusted after the event), no fixed sum insured (the liability incurred is what is covered), and very wide cover extensible by the board's omnibus discretion.

Every feature of the cover is derivable from mutuality. Answering from that one word makes the rest of the limb reconstructable rather than memorised.

Advance call - on entry for the policy year, assessed on entered tonnage and rated for ship type, trade, crew terms and above all the member's claims record. Supplementary call - if claims and expenses exceed advance calls plus investment income; the mutual mechanism working as designed, not a penalty. Release call - on leaving, buying out future supplementary calls on policy years still open because liability claims take years to mature. Deductible - the member's first amount on every claim.

Half of limb (a) is 'how they collect fund', and the release call is the tier candidates cannot account for unless they know why years stay open.

The club's own retention; then the International Group Pooling Agreement, under which claims above that retention are shared among the participating clubs; then the Group's collective reinsurance placed in the market; and above that an overspill layer met by the members through an overspill call. This is why liability cover of a magnitude no ordinary insurer would write is available at all. No figures - retentions and layers are reset every policy year.

It answers the obvious oral follow-up - how can a mutual of shipowners possibly pay for a major pollution casualty?

Protection = people and the world. Crew injury, illness and death; stevedores, pilots and passengers; the collision liability the hull policy's three-fourths leaves; fixed and floating objects; pollution and clean-up; wreck removal; stowaways; quarantine; fines so far as the rules permit; legal costs. Indemnity = cargo. Loss, damage, shortage and contamination of cargo; cargo's proportion of general average unrecoverable through the owner's breach; liabilities under approved bills of lading.

The marks in limb (b) are for putting risks on the correct side of the line, not for the length of the list.

Neither. Damage to the member's own ship is the hull and machinery policy's subject. Delay and loss of hire after a casualty is the owner's own trading loss - not a liability to anyone - and falls to a separate loss-of-hire cover if it was bought. War risks are separately insured too. The governing principle: P&I covers liabilities, not the owner's own losses.

This is the commonest misconception on the topic, and the MIW corpus flags it by name.

Three things an ordinary insurer does not. It takes control of the defence - appointing lawyers and surveyors, with the member forbidden to admit liability or settle. It works through worldwide correspondents, who attend the ship, take statements while the crew are aboard, and arrange joint surveys. And it provides security - a letter of undertaking promising to satisfy any judgment, which prevents or lifts an arrest without a cash deposit.

What the club really sells is control and credit, not money. That reframing is what makes the six-mark limb worth six marks.

Notify immediately - club and correspondent, on suspicion rather than certainty, because late notice can defeat the claim. Preserve evidence at once - logs, engine data, Oil Record Book, crew statements, photographs, cargo records. Arrange survey, jointly where a third party is involved. Admit nothing and settle nothing. Submit the formal claim with documents. Request security if arrest threatens. Settlement follows, less the deductible, with recovery by subrogation.

Nine steps in the order they actually happen. Most claims that fail on the facts fail because nobody took the evidence in the first twenty-four hours.

The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 lists at section 4(1)(s) "insurance premium (including mutual insurance calls)" among the maritime claims for which a vessel may be arrested in rem under section 5 - so an unpaid call can put the ship under arrest. Section 11 protects against wrongful arrest. The Marine Insurance Act, 1963 supplies utmost good faith and subrogation. Both were in force at this sitting; the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced 15 March 2026 and has no application here.

One sentence naming an Indian statute that recognises the exact mechanism just described is worth more in an Indian examination than another paragraph on mutuality.

Quick revision
15-second recall
A P&I club is a mutual association of shipowners covering third-party liabilities; the members are the club, so there are calls, not premiums, no fixed sum insured, and surplus is retained for the members. It collects in tiers: advance call on entry, assessed on entered tonnage and rated above all on the claims record; supplementary call if the year overruns; release call on leaving, because policy years stay open while liability claims mature; and a deductible on every claim. Above the club sit the International Group pooling agreement, the Group's collective reinsurance and an overspill layer. Protection covers people and the world - crew injury and death, other persons, the collision liability the hull policy's three-fourths leaves, fixed and floating objects, pollution, wreck removal, stowaways, quarantine, fines and legal costs. Indemnity covers cargo - loss, damage, shortage, contamination, and cargo's GA contribution unrecoverable through the owner's breach. It does not cover your own ship or your own loss of hire. On a claim: notify at once, preserve evidence, correspondent attends, joint survey, admit nothing, submit with documents, take a letter of undertaking to lift arrest, settle less deductible, and the club recovers by subrogation. In India an unpaid call is a maritime claim supporting arrest - Admiralty Act 2017, s.4(1)(s).

Critical numbers
  • Hull and machinery classically covers three-fourths of collision (running-down) liability; the balance falls to the club
  • Twelve clubs participate in the International Group - a current figure that moves with mergers, not a permanent fact
  • NO retention, pool layer, reinsurance attachment, overspill limit or deductible figure is quoted - all are reset every policy year
  • Admiralty Act 2017, section 4(1)(s) - insurance premium including mutual insurance calls, as a maritime claim
Critical regulation
There is no convention governing P&I - it is market practice, and saying so is correct rather than evasive. The Indian statutory hooks are what anchor the answer: the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, whose section 4(1)(s) makes an unpaid mutual insurance call a maritime claim supporting arrest, and the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 for utmost good faith and subrogation.
Major trap
Saying premium instead of call, which signals that the mutuality has not been understood - and then being unable to explain the supplementary or release call, both of which exist only because the members carry the risk themselves. Second trap: claiming P&I covers loss of hire or damage to the member's own ship. It does not - P&I covers liabilities, not the owner's own losses. Third: writing limb (c) as "tell the club and they pay". It is the largest limb at six marks, and what the club actually supplies is control and credit - a lawyer and surveyor on the quay, and a letter of undertaking that lifts an arrest without cash. Fourth: quoting pool or reinsurance figures, which are reset every policy year.

  • Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 section 4(1)(s), and sections 5 and 11 — The Act is held in the corpus India folder but the sub-clause lettering is carried as authoritative secondary rather than read from the text. It is the point that makes this an Indian answer, so it should be confirmed against the official text before publication. No wording is quoted. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • That the scope of cover described is the general market position — Club rules differ and are revised annually, and the terms of entry vary by ship. The answer states the general structure and says so expressly, but any candidate-facing publication should carry that qualification prominently. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
Understand this first

A ship goes aground with cargo on board. Two entirely separate money questions arise from that one event, and the paper is asking about both. Question one: somebody outside the adventure - a salvage tug - came and pulled her off. What is he owed? That is salvage, and the answer is governed by an international convention with one dominant rule: if he achieves nothing, he is paid nothing. That rule is brutal and deliberate, because it makes the salvor carry the risk instead of the shipowner - and it produced one perverse consequence that had to be fixed, which is that a salvor who spent everything preventing a spill from a ship that sank anyway got nothing at all. The Convention fixes that with a separate payment for environmental effort, owed even where the property itself is lost. Question two: the ship's owner and the twelve cargo owners were all in this adventure together. Getting off the ground cost money and destroyed some property. Who bears it? That is general average, and the answer is that everyone who was saved pays a share, in proportion to what they saved. The principle is ancient and it exists to stop the master hesitating: if he must jettison one shipper's cargo to save the rest, that shipper should not bear the whole loss of a decision taken for everybody. Then limb C is simply the four practical stages of making that sharing happen: who pays (entitlement), what counts (artificial), who works out the numbers (adjustment), and who argues about them (contestation). And the two halves of the question meet at the end - what you paid the salvor is itself a general average expenditure, shared by everyone he saved.

Model written answer

1. Two doctrines, two questions

It is worth opening with the distinction, because the paper puts salvage and general average in one question and candidates blur them. Both arise from the same casualty and they answer different questions. Salvage asks: what does the outsider who saved the property get paid? General average asks: how do the owners of the property that was saved share the cost between themselves? One is a reward to a stranger; the other is an apportionment among the adventure. They also differ in source - salvage is convention and law, general average is contract.

2. Limb A - the principles of modern salvage law

Modern salvage law is governed by the International Convention on Salvage, 1989, in force 14 July 1996, which replaced the 1910 Brussels Convention. Its principles are:

  1. Voluntariness. The service must be given voluntarily - there must be no pre-existing duty to render it. A tug under a towage contract, a crew member doing his job, or a State body under a public duty is not a salvor for these purposes.
  2. Real danger. The property must be in danger. The danger need not be immediate or absolute, but it must be real and not fanciful.
  3. Maritime property. The subject must be a vessel or other property in navigable waters, and the salvage must be of property rather than of life alone. Life salvage attracts no reward of itself, but a life salvor shares in a reward payable for the property saved in the same operation.
  4. No cure, no pay - Article 12. A salvage operation that has had a useful result gives a right to a reward; one that has not gives no payment. This is the foundation of the whole regime and it is what makes salvage a commercial risk rather than a service contract.
  5. The reward is fixed on criteria, and is capped - Article 13. The reward is assessed on ten listed criteria in sub-paragraphs (a) to (j): the salved value of the vessel and property; the skill and efforts of the salvors in preventing or minimising damage to the environment; the measure of success; the nature and degree of the danger; the skill and efforts in salving the vessel, other property and life; the time used and expenses and losses incurred; the risk of liability and other risks run; the promptness of the services; the availability and use of vessels or other equipment intended for salvage; and the state of readiness and efficiency of the salvor's equipment and its value. The reward may not exceed the salved value of the vessel and other property.
  6. Encouragement of environmental protection - Article 14. Where a salvor has carried out operations on a vessel which by herself or her cargo threatened damage to the environment, and has failed to earn a reward at least equivalent to the special compensation, he is entitled to special compensation from the shipowner equivalent to his expenses. If he has actually prevented or minimised environmental damage, that may be increased by up to 30 per cent of his expenses, and a tribunal may go to a maximum of 100 per cent. This is the deliberate qualification of no cure no pay: a salvor who protects the environment and saves nothing is not left unpaid.
  7. Duties run both ways - Article 8. The salvor must carry out the operation with due care, exercise due care to prevent or minimise damage to the environment, seek assistance from other salvors where reasonably required, and accept the intervention of other salvors when reasonably requested. The owner and master must co-operate fully with the salvor, exercise the same environmental due care, and accept redelivery when the property is brought to a place of safety.
  8. Freedom of contract, subject to control. Parties are free to agree salvage terms, but an agreement may be annulled or modified where it was entered into under undue influence or the influence of danger and its terms are inequitable, or where the payment is excessively large or small for the services actually rendered.

In practice these principles are delivered through Lloyd's Open Form, the standard no cure no pay salvage agreement, which allows a master to engage a salvor in minutes without negotiating terms and refers the reward to arbitration afterwards. Attached to it, the SCOPIC clause supplies a contractual alternative to Article 14: instead of arguing about actual expenses after the event, the salvor may invoke SCOPIC and be remunerated on an agreed tariff with an uplift, backed by security from the owner's P&I club. Article 14 remains the legal fallback; SCOPIC is how the market avoids litigating it.

3. Limb B - what general average is

General average is the ancient principle that where a sacrifice or expenditure is made intentionally and reasonably for the common safety of the maritime adventure, the loss is shared rateably by all the interests that were saved by it. It is older than insurance and it survives because it removes a perverse incentive: without it, the master would hesitate to jettison one shipper's cargo to save the rest, and the shipper whose cargo happened to be nearest the hatch would bear the whole loss of a decision taken for everyone's benefit.

Rule A of the York-Antwerp Rules gives the definition, and it is best written as five elements every one of which must be present:

ElementWhat it meansWhat fails it
ExtraordinaryThe sacrifice or expenditure is outside the ordinary course of the voyageBunkers burned on a normal passage; ordinary wear; anything the ship was going to do anyway
IntentionalIt is deliberately made, not merely sufferedCargo washed overboard by a sea. Cargo jettisoned by decision is general average
ReasonableIt is a reasonable response in the circumstances as they appearedA grossly disproportionate or panicked act
For the common safetyMade to preserve the whole adventure from peril, not one interestAn act benefiting only the ship, or only one parcel of cargo
Property preservedSomething must in fact be saved - there must be a fund to contributeA total loss. If nothing is saved, there is nothing to apportion

Classic examples: jettison of cargo to refloat a grounded ship; water damage to cargo from fighting a fire in the hold; deliberate beaching to prevent foundering; the cost of engaging salvors; damage to machinery from working engines hard in a refloating attempt; and the expenses of putting into a port of refuge.

One point of law that must be stated because it explains everything in limb C: the York-Antwerp Rules are not law. They are a set of rules produced by the Comite Maritime International and they apply only because the bill of lading or charterparty incorporates them. Which edition applies is therefore a question of what the contract says, and editions differ in substance. In Indian law, general average is recognised by section 66 of the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, which is what makes it enforceable domestically rather than merely contractual.

4. Limb C(i) - Entitlement

Entitlement is the question of who may claim and who must contribute.

  • The party who made the sacrifice or incurred the expenditure is entitled to a contribution from the other interests saved.
  • The contributing interests are ship, cargo and freight at risk - and, where applicable, bunkers and containers owned separately. Each contributes in proportion to the contributory value of its property at the termination of the adventure, not at the time of the act - so a cargo that has deteriorated for other reasons contributes on its arrived value.
  • The party who sacrificed also contributes on the value of what it saved, so it does not recover the whole of its loss - it recovers all but its own share.
  • The loss must be the direct consequence of the general average act. Indirect losses - demurrage, market loss, loss of the voyage - are excluded.
  • Actionable fault defeats the claim. A party whose fault caused the peril cannot recover a general average contribution from those who could sue him for that fault. This is the most important defence in the subject, and it is why a general average declaration after an engine-room fire is so often disputed: if the fire arose from unseaworthiness for which the owner is answerable, the owner's claim can fail entirely.
  • Security: the shipowner has a lien on the cargo for its general average contribution and will not release it without security. In practice cargo interests provide an average bond from the receiver together with an average guarantee from their cargo underwriter, or a cash deposit where there is no insurance. That is the mechanism by which the cargo actually leaves the terminal.

5. Limb C(ii) - Artificial general average

State the honest position first: "artificial general average" is practitioner terminology and is not a term defined in the York-Antwerp Rules. Saying so is worth a mark; presenting it as a defined category is a small but real error.

What it describes is this. Rule A sets a strict test - the act must be for the common safety. But the numbered Rules that follow expressly allow certain further items into general average which do not satisfy that strict test, because they are incurred for the common benefit of completing the adventure rather than to escape an immediate peril. Practitioners call those allowances artificial general average, or general average by convention, because they are general average by the agreement of the Rules rather than by the principle.

The standard examples all arise at a port of refuge. Once the ship is safely in the port, the immediate peril has passed - so the cost of discharging, storing and reloading cargo to permit repairs, the port charges, and the wages and maintenance of the crew during the detention are not common-safety expenditure at all. The adventure is no longer in danger; it is merely interrupted. The numbered Rules nonetheless allow them, because the alternative is to abandon the voyage and leave everyone worse off. The same reasoning supports temporary repairs made to enable the voyage to be completed, and the cost of substituting a cheaper expense for an allowable one.

The practical significance, and the reason candidates should understand rather than memorise this, is that artificial general average is where most of the money usually is. The jettison is dramatic and cheap; the four months in a port of refuge, with crew wages, storage and reloading, is undramatic and expensive. It is also the part most often argued about, and the part where the edition of the Rules incorporated by the contract matters most, because successive editions have moved these allowances in and out.

6. Limb C(iii) - Adjustment

Adjustment is the process of turning a casualty into a set of numbers, and it is done by an average adjuster.

  • The adjuster is appointed by the shipowner but is required to act impartially between all the interests. That combination - paid by one party, neutral between all - is the single most misunderstood feature of the role, and stating it correctly is worth a mark.
  • He does not declare general average; the shipowner declares it. He does not decide liability either; questions of fault and of the contract of carriage are for the courts or arbitrators.
  • He determines which losses and expenses are allowable under the applicable edition of the Rules, and on what basis they are valued.
  • He ascertains the contributory values of ship, cargo and freight at the termination of the adventure.
  • He calculates the rate of contribution and apportions the general average fund between the interests.
  • He collects the securities - average bonds, guarantees and deposits - and issues the general average statement, which sets out the allowances, the values, the apportionment and each party's balance.
  • Adjustment is slow. A statement can take years on a major casualty, because contributory values cannot be fixed until the cargo is delivered and its arrived value known.

7. Limb C(iv) - Contestation

Contestation is the challenging of the adjustment, and the first point is the one that makes it possible at all: the general average statement is evidence, not a judgment. The adjuster has no judicial authority. A party who disputes the statement is not appealing against a decision; it is declining to accept a document. Grounds of challenge fall into five groups:

  1. There was no general average act. The sacrifice was not extraordinary, or not intentional, or not reasonable, or not made for the common safety - one of Rule A's elements fails.
  2. The allowance is not permitted under the applicable Rules, or the wrong edition has been applied - which is a live argument precisely because the Rules are contractual and editions differ.
  3. The values or the apportionment are wrong - contributory values misstated, the wrong basis of valuation used, or arithmetical error.
  4. Actionable fault. The most powerful ground: the peril was caused by the fault of the party claiming - typically unseaworthiness for which the shipowner is answerable and which is not excepted under the contract of carriage. If it succeeds, the claim can fail entirely, not merely in part.
  5. The claim is time-barred, or the security was given under protest and its terms are not met.

Resolution follows the contract: negotiation between the adjuster and the underwriters first, which settles the great majority; then arbitration where the bill of lading or charterparty so provides; and litigation in the agreed forum where it does not. Cargo underwriters are the real parties in interest on the cargo side, and it is usually they, rather than the receivers, who contest.

8. Where the two doctrines meet

The connection worth making at the end, because it shows the question was read as one question rather than two: the salvage award is itself a general average expenditure. Money paid to salvors to save the whole adventure is extraordinary, intentional, reasonable and for the common safety, so it goes into general average and is shared between ship, cargo and freight - which is why the same casualty produces a salvage arbitration and a general average adjustment running in parallel, and why the P&I club and the cargo underwriters end up providing security in both. In Indian law the general average side of that is anchored by section 66 of the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, and the domestic shipping statute at this examination is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

4 + 4 + 8. The eight-mark limb is half the question and it has four named sub-items, so it needs four visible answers of roughly two marks each. That is the whole time-management problem here: candidates write beautifully on salvage, which they enjoy, and then give one paragraph to entitlement, artificial, adjustment and contestation combined. Budget half the answer for limb C before you start, and give each roman numeral its own heading.

Open with the distinction

Salvage asks what the outsider who saved the property gets paid. General average asks how the owners of the saved property share the cost between themselves. One is a reward to a stranger, the other an apportionment among the adventure. One is convention and law, the other is contract. Two sentences, and the whole question is framed.

Limb A - the four Articles that carry it

ArticleWhat it doesThe number to remember
8Duties - of the salvor, and of the owner and masterDuties run both ways
12No useful result, no paymentNo cure, no pay
13Criteria for fixing the rewardTen criteria, (a) to (j), capped at the salved value
14Special compensation where the environment was threatenedExpenses, +30%, and up to 100% at a tribunal's discretion

Salvage Convention 1989, in force 14 July 1996, replacing the 1910 Brussels Convention. Those two dates and those four Articles are four marks' worth on their own.

Why Article 14 exists - say it in one line

Pure no cure no pay produced a perverse result: a salvor who spent a fortune preventing a spill from a ship that was ultimately lost got nothing, so salvors declined environmental casualties - which are exactly the ones society most wants attended. Article 14 pays his expenses, with an uplift for success in protecting the environment. SCOPIC is then the market's way of avoiding an argument about what those expenses were: an agreed tariff with an uplift, invoked by the salvor and secured by the owner's club. Article 14 is the law; SCOPIC is the practice.

Rule A - five elements, and lose one and it fails

EXTRAORDINARY - INTENTIONAL - REASONABLE - COMMON SAFETY - SOMETHING SAVED. Every one must be present. Learn the failure case for each: bunkers burned on a normal passage is not extraordinary; cargo washed overboard is not intentional (cargo jettisoned is); a panicked disproportionate act is not reasonable; an act benefiting only the ship is not for the common safety; and a total loss leaves no fund to apportion.

The honesty point that earns a mark

"Artificial general average" is not a term defined in the York-Antwerp Rules. It is practitioner terminology for allowances the numbered Rules permit on a common-benefit basis although they fail Rule A's strict common-safety test - port of refuge expenses, crew wages and maintenance during detention, discharging, storing and reloading to permit repairs, temporary repairs, substituted expenses. Say it is not a defined term, then explain what it describes. Presenting it as a defined category of the Rules is a small error that a good examiner will notice.

And the practical point behind it

Artificial general average is where most of the money usually is. The jettison is dramatic and cheap; four months in a port of refuge with crew wages, storage and reloading is undramatic and expensive. It is also where the edition of the Rules matters most, because successive editions have moved these allowances in and out.

The adjuster - what he is and is not

  • Appointed by the shipowner, but acts impartially between all interests. State both halves.
  • He does not declare general average - the shipowner does.
  • He does not decide liability - fault and contract questions are for courts or arbitrators.
  • He decides allowances under the applicable edition, ascertains contributory values at the termination of the adventure, apportions, collects securities and issues the statement.

Actionable fault - the answer to two sub-items at once

A party whose fault caused the peril cannot recover a general average contribution from those who could sue him for it. It appears under entitlement as the thing that defeats the claim, and again under contestation as the most powerful ground of challenge - and if it succeeds the claim can fail entirely, not merely in part. Using it in both places, and saying it is the same point, is efficient and shows the four sub-items are understood as one subject.

The security chain - know the three words

The shipowner has a lien on the cargo for its contribution. Cargo interests provide an average bond (from the receiver) plus an average guarantee (from the cargo underwriter), or a cash deposit where the cargo is uninsured. That is the mechanism by which cargo actually leaves the terminal, and it is the direct link to the P&I question on this same paper.

The York-Antwerp Rules are not law

They are Comite Maritime International rules that apply only because the bill of lading or charterparty incorporates them, and which edition applies is a question of what the contract says. In Indian law general average is recognised by section 66 of the Marine Insurance Act, 1963. Do not say "under the York-Antwerp Rules it is the law that..." - it is not.

Common mistakes

  • Confusing salvage with general average, or treating them as one doctrine.
  • Giving no cure no pay without Article 14 - and so missing why the environmental exception exists at all.
  • Saying salvage requires a contract. It does not; it requires voluntariness, danger and a useful result.
  • Losing an element of Rule A, usually 'extraordinary' or 'something must be saved'.
  • Saying cargo washed overboard is general average. Jettisoned is; washed is not.
  • Presenting 'artificial general average' as a defined term of the Rules.
  • Saying the adjuster declares general average, or that he decides liability.
  • Contributing on values at the time of the act rather than at the termination of the adventure.
  • Spending the answer on limb A and giving limb C - half the marks - a single paragraph.

Examiner traps

  • "Can the reward exceed the salved value?" No - Article 13 caps it there.
  • "Is life salvage rewarded?" Not of itself; a life salvor shares in the reward payable for the property saved in the same operation.
  • "Your ship is under tow and gets into difficulty. Is the tug a salvor?" Only if the service goes beyond the towage contract - otherwise there is a pre-existing duty and no voluntariness.
  • "What is SCOPIC and why was it needed?" A contractual tariff-based alternative to arguing Article 14 expenses after the event, invoked by the salvor and secured by the owner's club.
  • "Who declares general average?" The shipowner. The adjuster adjusts it.
  • "Is the general average statement binding?" No - it is evidence, not a judgment.
  • "Give a general average act in your own engine room." Damage to main engine or auxiliaries from working them hard in a deliberate refloating attempt; or water damage from flooding a hold to fight a fire.
  • "What defeats a general average claim entirely?" Actionable fault - the peril was caused by the claimant's own fault, typically unseaworthiness for which the owner is answerable.
  • "Which edition of the York-Antwerp Rules applies?" The one the contract incorporates. There is no universal answer.

Likely oral follow-up

  • You are aground and the master signs Lloyd's Open Form. What happens next, and what do you do in the engine room?
  • General average has been declared. What does that mean for the cargo on your ship?
  • What is an average bond and who gives it?
  • Your engines are damaged refloating the ship. Is that general average, and who pays?
  • Why would the cargo underwriter contest the adjustment?
  • What is the difference between salvage and towage?

Memory framework

Two doctrines - four Articles - five elements - four sub-items. TWO DOCTRINES: salvage pays the stranger; GA shares among the adventure. FOUR ARTICLES: 8 duties, 12 no cure no pay, 13 ten criteria capped at salved value, 14 special compensation +30% up to 100%. FIVE ELEMENTS of Rule A: EXTRAORDINARY, INTENTIONAL, REASONABLE, COMMON SAFETY, SOMETHING SAVED. FOUR SUB-ITEMS: who pays (entitlement) - what counts (artificial) - who works it out (adjustment) - who argues (contestation).

Regulation and source map

  • International Convention on Salvage, 1989, in force 14 July 1996 - Article 8 duties; Article 12 no useful result, no payment; Article 13 the ten criteria and the salved-value ceiling; Article 14 special compensation, 30 per cent and up to 100 per cent.
  • York-Antwerp Rules, Rule A - the general average act. Contractual, not law; the edition is whichever the bill of lading or charterparty incorporates.
  • The numbered Rules of the York-Antwerp Rules - the source of the allowances called artificial general average.
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 (India), section 66 - general average loss.
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 (India) - the domestic shipping statute in force at this sitting.
  • Lloyd's Open Form and the SCOPIC clause - industry practice, not law.
  • LLMC 1976 / 1996 Protocol - the limitation regime that can cap what is ultimately recoverable.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

Neither the Salvage Convention text nor the York-Antwerp Rules is held by the MIW corpus. The corpus liability-and-compensation family log covers HNS, the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention, LLMC and the CLC/FUND/Bunkers group, and does not extend to salvage or general average. Both are therefore carried as authoritative secondary: Articles and Rules are cited by number, their effect is stated, and no wording is quoted anywhere.

Two points of interpretation matter more than anything else here, and both are stated openly rather than glossed. First, 'artificial general average' is practitioner terminology, not a term defined in the Rules - the answer says so rather than presenting it as a defined category. Second, which edition of the York-Antwerp Rules governs depends on the incorporating contract, so no assertion is made that the 2016 edition applies universally.

No case is named anywhere. MIW holds no law reports, and both salvage and general average are subjects where a misattributed authority would be worse than none. No monetary figure is asserted - no award, no percentage of salved value, no SCOPIC tariff rate and no security figure. The Article 14 percentages are structural features of the Article itself rather than market values.

India's party status to the Salvage Convention 1989 is not asserted; the corpus does not record it, so the Convention's international effect and the Indian statutory position are stated separately rather than joined. The domestic statutes at this sitting are the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 and the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced 15 March 2026 and appears nowhere in this answer - a point of some importance here, because this same question has also been set at sittings in January 2026 and April 2026, after that Act had commenced; an answer written for one of those sittings would not be correct for this one.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Two doctrines, two questions
  2. __________A Limb A - the principles of modern salvage law
  3. __________B Limb B - what general average is
  4. __________C(i) Limb C(i) - Entitlement
  5. __________C(ii) Limb C(ii) - Artificial general average
  6. __________C(iii) Limb C(iii) - Adjustment
  7. __________C(iv) Limb C(iv) - Contestation
  8. __________Where the two doctrines meet
Flashcards 9

1 Frame the two doctrines. 2 Limb A - principles of salvage. 3 Limb B - what general average is, with Rule A. 4 C(i) Entitlement. 5 C(ii) Artificial. 6 C(iii) Adjustment. 7 C(iv) Contestation. 8 Where the two meet - the salvage award is itself a GA expenditure.

Limb C is half the marks and has four named sub-items. One headed section each is the only way to make all four visible.

Salvage asks what the outsider who saved the property is paid - a reward to a stranger, governed by the Salvage Convention 1989. General average asks how the owners of the property that was saved share the cost between themselves - an apportionment among the adventure, governed by the York-Antwerp Rules, which are contract, not law. Both arise from the same casualty and they answer different questions.

Two sentences that frame the whole answer and stop the two doctrines being blurred, which is the commonest structural failure here.

Salvage Convention 1989, in force 14 July 1996. Voluntariness - no pre-existing duty. Real danger to maritime property. Article 12 no cure, no pay - a useful result earns a reward, no result earns nothing. Article 13 - ten criteria, capped at the salved value. Article 14 - special compensation where the environment was threatened: expenses, plus up to 30 per cent, and 100 per cent at a tribunal's discretion. Article 8 - duties both ways. In practice, Lloyd's Open Form with SCOPIC.

Four Articles carry four marks. Naming them with their effects is more efficient than any narrative.

Because pure no cure no pay produced a perverse result: a salvor who spent a fortune preventing a spill from a ship that was ultimately lost recovered nothing, so salvors declined the environmental casualties society most wanted attended. Article 14 pays his expenses, increased by up to 30 per cent - and 100 per cent at a tribunal's discretion - where he prevented or minimised damage. SCOPIC is the market's contractual alternative: an agreed tariff with an uplift, secured by the owner's club.

Explaining why a rule exists is worth more than stating it, and it sets up the SCOPIC point which is the standard oral follow-up.

EXTRAORDINARY - outside the ordinary course of the voyage. INTENTIONAL - deliberately made, not merely suffered. REASONABLE - a proportionate response in the circumstances as they appeared. FOR THE COMMON SAFETY - to preserve the whole adventure from peril, not one interest. PROPERTY PRESERVED - something must in fact be saved, or there is no fund to apportion. All five must be present.

Learn the failure case for each: bunkers on a normal passage is not extraordinary; cargo washed overboard is not intentional, cargo jettisoned is; a total loss leaves nothing to share.

Ship, cargo and freight at risk, each in proportion to its contributory value at the termination of the adventure - not at the time of the act. The party who sacrificed also contributes, so it recovers all but its own share. The loss must be the direct consequence; demurrage and market loss are excluded. Actionable fault defeats the claim. The owner has a lien on the cargo, released against an average bond plus an average guarantee.

The timing of the valuation and the security chain are both routinely asked, and the security chain is the direct link to the P&I question.

It is practitioner terminology and is NOT a term defined in the York-Antwerp Rules - say that first. It describes allowances the numbered Rules permit on a common-benefit basis although they fail Rule A's strict common-safety test. The examples arise at a port of refuge: discharging, storing and reloading cargo, port charges, crew wages and maintenance during detention, temporary repairs. In practice this is where most of the money is.

The sub-item most candidates cannot place at all, and the honesty about it being undefined is itself worth a mark.

Appointed by the shipowner but required to act impartially between all interests. He determines which losses and expenses are allowable under the applicable edition and how they are valued, ascertains the contributory values at the termination of the adventure, calculates the rate and apportions, collects bonds, guarantees and deposits, and issues the general average statement. He does NOT declare general average - the shipowner does - and he does NOT decide liability; fault and contract questions are for courts or arbitrators.

'Appointed by one party, neutral between all' is the single most misunderstood feature of the role, and the two things he does not do are standard traps.

Starting from the fact that the statement is evidence, not a judgment: (1) there was no general average act - one of Rule A's elements fails; (2) the allowance is not permitted, or the wrong edition was applied; (3) the values or apportionment are wrong; (4) actionable fault, which can defeat the claim entirely; (5) time bar. Resolved by negotiation with the underwriters, then arbitration or litigation.

Five grounds for two marks, and actionable fault is the same point that appears under entitlement - using it twice shows the four sub-items are one subject.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Salvage - International Convention on Salvage, 1989, in force 14 July 1996. Principles: the service must be voluntary (no pre-existing duty), the property in real danger, and under Article 12 there is no cure, no pay - a useful result earns a reward, no result earns nothing. Article 13 fixes the reward on ten criteria and caps it at the salved value. Article 14 gives special compensation where the environment was threatened - expenses, plus up to 30 per cent, and up to 100 per cent at a tribunal's discretion. Article 8 imposes duties both ways. In practice: Lloyd's Open Form with SCOPIC as the tariff alternative to Article 14. General average - Rule A of the York-Antwerp Rules: an extraordinary, intentional, reasonable sacrifice or expenditure for the common safety, with property preserved, shared rateably. The Rules are contractual, not law; India recognises GA at section 66, Marine Insurance Act, 1963. Entitlement: ship, cargo and freight contribute on contributory values at the termination of the adventure; actionable fault defeats the claim; security by average bond and guarantee against the owner's lien. Artificial GA: not a defined term - practitioner shorthand for common-benefit allowances the numbered Rules permit though they fail Rule A's common-safety test, chiefly port of refuge expenses and crew wages. Adjustment: the average adjuster, appointed by the owner but impartial, does not declare GA and does not decide liability; he fixes allowances and values and issues the statement. Contestation: the statement is evidence, not a judgment - challenge on no GA act, allowance not permitted or wrong edition, wrong values, actionable fault, or time bar.

Critical numbers
  • Salvage Convention 1989, in force 14 July 1996, replacing the Brussels Convention 1910
  • Article 13 - TEN criteria, sub-paragraphs (a) to (j); the reward may not exceed the SALVED VALUE
  • Article 14 - special compensation: expenses, increased by up to 30 per cent, and to a maximum of 100 per cent
  • Rule A - FIVE elements, all of which must be present
  • Contributory values are taken at the TERMINATION OF THE ADVENTURE, not at the time of the act
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 section 66 - general average loss in Indian law
  • NO salvage award, percentage of salved value, SCOPIC tariff rate or security figure is quoted
Critical regulation
Salvage Convention 1989 Articles 12, 13 and 14 carry the whole of limb A - no cure no pay, the ten criteria capped at salved value, and the environmental exception that qualifies the first. York-Antwerp Rule A carries limb B, and it must be introduced as CONTRACTUAL rather than legal, because that single fact is what makes limb C(ii) and the wrong-edition ground in limb C(iv) intelligible.
Major trap
Spending the answer on salvage, which candidates enjoy, and compressing limb C - which is half the marks and has four named sub-items needing four separate answers. Second trap: presenting "artificial general average" as a term defined in the York-Antwerp Rules. It is not - it is practitioner shorthand for common-benefit allowances the numbered Rules permit although they fail Rule A's common-safety test. Say so, and gain the mark the error costs. Third: saying the adjuster declares general average - the shipowner declares it, and the adjuster neither declares it nor decides liability. Fourth: taking contributory values at the time of the act rather than at the termination of the adventure. Fifth: treating the York-Antwerp Rules as law, when they apply only because the contract incorporates them - which is also why "which edition?" is a real question. Sixth, specific to this sitting: reaching for the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025, which commenced 15 March 2026, twenty-one months after this examination.
