(B_CURRENCY_CHECK)
Understand this first

Every machine on the ship is sealed, and you cannot see inside it while it runs. But the oil can - it touches every working surface, continuously, and it carries away a sample of everything that happens in there. Oil analysis is simply the business of reading that sample. The reason there are four techniques and not one is that they are answering different questions, and they are blind to each other's subjects. Two of them look at the machine: spectrometry tells you what the debris is made of, and particle count tells you how much of it there is and how big - and you need both, because spectrometry physically cannot see large particles, so a clean spectrometric report on a spalling bearing is a false reassurance rather than good news. The other two look at the oil: FTIR tells you what the oil has chemically become, and Base and Acid Number tell you how much fight it has left in it. Once you see it that way, the interpretation the question demands stops being a memory exercise. You are not reading numbers - you are reading pairs. Iron rising tells you almost nothing on its own; iron rising with the base number gone tells you the oil stopped protecting the metal, while iron rising with the base number healthy tells you something entirely different and means that adding more oil will make it worse. And the last idea, which is what the stem's preamble is driving at: the analysis does not maintain anything. It tells you where to look. A programme that produces reports nobody reads is a cost with no benefit.

Knowledge map
Lubricating Oil Condition Analysis Techniques
  1. 1Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for
    • Used oil is the only sample in continuous contact with every working surface
    • The four techniques are not four ways of doing one thing
    • Is the machine wearing? - spectrometry and particle count
  2. A
    1. 2A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis
      • Sample excited in a plasma or across a rotating-disc electrode arc
      • Each element emits at characteristic wavelengths; intensity gives concentration in ppm
      • Wear metals - iron from liners, rings, gears, shafts; copper, lead, tin from bearings; chromium from rings and plating; aluminium from pistons
    2. 3What spectrometry cannot see - and why that governs its use
      • The method needs complete atomisation of the particle in the excitation source
      • Large particles are not fully atomised, so response falls away with increasing size
      • It therefore reports essentially the fine fraction only
  3. B
    1. 4B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy
      • Infrared through a thin film; molecular bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers
      • All wavelengths gathered at once as an interferogram, Fourier transformed to a spectrum - hence fast
      • A DIFFERENTIAL measurement - subtracted from the spectrum of the same oil unused
  4. C
    1. 5C) Particle count
      • Solid particles counted and sized, usually by optical light obscuration
      • Pore blockage where the oil is too dark; microscopy is the referee method
      • Reported as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands, condensed to a cleanliness code
  5. D
    1. 6D) Base Number against Acid Number
      • Both titrations, both in mg KOH per gramme - which is why they are confused
      • BN = alkaline reserve from overbased detergent additives; it FALLS in service
      • AN = acidic constituents from combustion and oxidation; it RISES
  6. 7Correct interpretation - reading the four together
    • No single result diagnoses anything; pairs and trends do
    • Iron up + residual BN near zero = corrosive wear (in a cylinder, cold corrosion)
    • Iron up + BN healthy = NOT corrosion; more oil will make it worse
  7. 8Corrective measures
    • The sequence: verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil
    • Re-sample before acting - many alarming results are sampling artefacts
    • Confirm physically - magnetic plugs, filters cut open, centrifuge sludge, ferrography for debris shape, direct inspection
  8. 9Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything
    • Same point, same way, same interval; machine at temperature and oil circulating
    • From a live line upstream of the filter, not the drain or tank bottom
    • Flush the point; use clean dedicated bottles - a dirty bottle condemns a healthy machine

Memory cue. Three questions, four techniques, two pairs, one sequence. QUESTIONS: is it wearing, is the oil fit, is anything getting in (1). TECHNIQUES: spectrometry says WHAT (2, limited by step 3), particle count says HOW MUCH and HOW BIG (5), FTIR says what the OIL has become (4), BN/AN says how much FIGHT is left (6). PAIRS: iron with residual BN; particle count with spectrometry (7). SEQUENCE: verify, confirm physically, correct the cause, then correct the oil (8).

Remember9 route headings
Cover75 core points beneath them

Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 75 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for
  • Used oil is the only sample in continuous contact with every working surface
  • The four techniques are not four ways of doing one thing
  • Is the machine wearing? - spectrometry and particle count
  • Is the oil still fit? - FTIR, and BN and AN
  • Is anything getting in? - all four, from different directions
  • The governing rule: trend against the machine's own history, not the absolute value
2. A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis
  • Sample excited in a plasma or across a rotating-disc electrode arc
  • Each element emits at characteristic wavelengths; intensity gives concentration in ppm
  • Wear metals - iron from liners, rings, gears, shafts; copper, lead, tin from bearings; chromium from rings and plating; aluminium from pistons
  • Contaminants - silicon from dirt (abrasive); sodium and boron from coolant; vanadium and nickel from residual fuel
  • Additive elements - calcium and magnesium (detergent), zinc and phosphorus (anti-wear)
  • Additives falling = depletion; rising = the wrong oil was added
  • Silicon reads two ways - dirt ingress in service, or sealant and antifoam residue right after an overhaul
3. What spectrometry cannot see - and why that governs its use
  • The method needs complete atomisation of the particle in the excitation source
  • Large particles are not fully atomised, so response falls away with increasing size
  • It therefore reports essentially the fine fraction only
  • Normal rubbing wear is fine and is seen well
  • Bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting are coarse and may not be seen
  • So a clean spectrometric report is NOT evidence of a healthy machine
  • Which is exactly why particle count, ferrography and plug inspection sit alongside it
4. B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy
  • Infrared through a thin film; molecular bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers
  • All wavelengths gathered at once as an interferogram, Fourier transformed to a spectrum - hence fast
  • A DIFFERENTIAL measurement - subtracted from the spectrum of the same oil unused
  • Practical consequence: keep a retained sample of every new oil delivery
  • Detects oxidation (carbonyl), nitration, sulphation, water (hydroxyl)
  • Soot as a broad baseline rise, not a discrete peak
  • Also fuel dilution, glycol ingress, additive depletion
  • Fast, small sample, no reagents - but semi-quantitative and blind to wear metals and particles
5. C) Particle count
  • Solid particles counted and sized, usually by optical light obscuration
  • Pore blockage where the oil is too dark; microscopy is the referee method
  • Reported as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands, condensed to a cleanliness code
  • Decisive for hydraulic and servo systems where clearances are microns
  • The direct measure of filtration and centrifuge performance and of ingress
  • It sees the large particles spectrometry misses - the two are complementary
  • Limits: counts water droplets and air bubbles as particles; cannot say what the particles are
6. D) Base Number against Acid Number
  • Both titrations, both in mg KOH per gramme - which is why they are confused
  • BN = alkaline reserve from overbased detergent additives; it FALLS in service
  • AN = acidic constituents from combustion and oxidation; it RISES
  • An oil can show both at once - separate titrations of separate species
  • Two standard BN methods give systematically different values - never trend them together
  • BN governs for cylinder and trunk-piston oils, matched to the sulphur actually burned
  • AN governs for turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils, which have no reserve
  • Since the 0.50 per cent limit of 1 January 2020, BN is matched to the fuel in use - and over-alkalinity deposits abrasive calcium
7. Correct interpretation - reading the four together
  • No single result diagnoses anything; pairs and trends do
  • Iron up + residual BN near zero = corrosive wear (in a cylinder, cold corrosion)
  • Iron up + BN healthy = NOT corrosion; more oil will make it worse
  • Particle count up + wear metals normal = filtration or centrifuge failure, or ingress
  • Particle count up + spectrometry clean = coarse debris - the most dangerous case
  • Silicon up + iron and chromium up = abrasive dirt ingress
  • Sodium and boron up + FTIR glycol and water = coolant leak
  • Vanadium and nickel + FTIR fuel + viscosity down = fuel dilution
  • Oxidation up + AN up + no wear metals = the oil is degrading, the machine is fine
  • Additive elements up = the wrong oil was added
  • Two disciplines: trend do not spot-read; and rate of change beats level
8. Corrective measures
  • The sequence: verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil
  • Re-sample before acting - many alarming results are sampling artefacts
  • Confirm physically - magnetic plugs, filters cut open, centrifuge sludge, ferrography for debris shape, direct inspection
  • Abrasive ingress - find the entry path (filters, seals, breathers), not just more filtration
  • Coolant leak - pressure-test coolers, jackets, covers; glycol destroys bearings
  • Fuel dilution - injection equipment, timing, combustion; then renew, because diluted oil cannot be recovered
  • Water - find the source and run the purifier correctly (gravity disc, throughput, temperature)
  • Rising particle count - filters, differential pressure, bypass, purifier operation; then the ingress path
  • Low BN in cylinder oil - raise feed rate or move to higher BN, matched to fuel sulphur, and re-check by scrape-down
  • High AN and oxidation - find the cause, then renew, and clean the system before recharging
  • Additive depletion - renew; additives cannot be topped up
  • Record and close the loop in the PMS - unrecorded action destroys the trend
  • Standing caution: every limit comes from the builder and the oil supplier for THAT installation
9. Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything
  • Same point, same way, same interval; machine at temperature and oil circulating
  • From a live line upstream of the filter, not the drain or tank bottom
  • Flush the point; use clean dedicated bottles - a dirty bottle condemns a healthy machine
  • Retain a sample of every new oil - FTIR needs the reference
  • Label fully: ship, machine, point, hours since change and since overhaul, grade, top-ups
  • Trend, and read the rate of change
  • Plan it in the PMS under the ISM maintenance regime
  • Close: analysis does not maintain machinery - it tells you where to look
Model written answer

1. Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for

Used lubricating oil is the only sample on the ship that has been in continuous contact with every working surface in the machine. Everything that wears, everything that leaks in, and every change in the oil itself is carried in it. That is why oil analysis is the foundation of condition-based maintenance rather than an adjunct to it.

The four techniques in this question are not four ways of doing the same thing. They answer three separate questions, and seeing that before describing any of them is what makes the rest of the answer coherent:

The questionWhich techniques answer it
Is the machine wearing?Elemental spectrometry and particle count
Is the oil still fit for service?FTIR, and Base Number and Acid Number
Is anything getting in?All four, from different directions

And one rule governs the use of every one of them: the trend against the machine's own history matters more than any single value. An absolute number means very little without knowing where that machine normally sits.

2. A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis

Principle. A measured sample is excited - in a plasma, or across a rotating-disc electrode arc. Excited atoms of each element emit light at wavelengths characteristic of that element, and the intensity at each wavelength is proportional to the concentration of that element present. The instrument reads many elements simultaneously and reports each in parts per million.

What the elements mean. They sort into three families, and reading them as families rather than as a list is what turns a report into a diagnosis:

FamilyElementsWhat their presence indicates
Wear metalsIron; copper, lead and tin; chromium; aluminium; silver; nickelIron from liners, rings, gears and shafts. Copper, lead and tin from bearings and bushes. Chromium from rings and plated surfaces. Aluminium from pistons and some bearings. Each points at a different part of the machine
ContaminantsSilicon; sodium and boron; vanadium and nickel; water-related indicatorsSilicon from airborne dirt through a defective filter or seal - and it is abrasive. Sodium and boron from coolant leakage. Vanadium and nickel from residual fuel, so their presence indicates fuel dilution
Additive elementsCalcium and magnesium; zinc and phosphorus; bariumCalcium and magnesium from detergent and dispersant additives; zinc and phosphorus from anti-wear additives. A falling trend means additive depletion; a rising one usually means the wrong oil has been added

Silicon deserves a note of its own because it is read two ways: dirt ingress, but also silicone antifoam or sealant residue after an overhaul. A single high silicon result shortly after a major job is usually the second; a rising trend in service is the first, and the first is serious because silicon is abrasive.

3. What spectrometry cannot see - and why that governs its use

This is the most important qualification in the whole question and it is regularly omitted. The method depends on the complete atomisation of the particles in the excitation source. Large particles are not fully atomised, so the response falls away progressively with increasing particle size. In practice the technique reports essentially the fine fraction only.

The consequence is severe and must be stated plainly. Normal rubbing wear produces fine debris and is seen well. Bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting produce coarse debris and may not be seen at all. So a satisfactory spectrometric report is not evidence that the machine is healthy - it is evidence that the fine fraction is normal, which is a much weaker statement. This is precisely why particle count, ferrography and magnetic plug inspection must sit alongside it rather than being treated as alternatives to it.

4. B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy

Principle. An infrared beam is passed through a thin film of the oil. Molecular bonds absorb infrared energy at wavenumbers characteristic of the bond, so the absorption pattern is a fingerprint of what molecules are present. Rather than scanning one wavelength at a time, the instrument gathers all wavelengths at once as an interferogram and applies a Fourier transform to convert it into a spectrum - which is what makes the technique fast and what the name describes.

It is a differential measurement, and this is the point candidates miss. The spectrum of the used oil is compared against the spectrum of the same oil unused, and it is the difference that is reported. Without a new-oil reference the result is very much weaker - which is a practical instruction as well as a technical one: keep a retained sample of every new oil delivered.

What it detects, each at its own characteristic absorption region:

  • Oxidation - at the carbonyl region, the principal measure of the oil's thermal and chemical ageing.
  • Nitration - from combustion products, characteristic of gas and dual-fuel engines in particular.
  • Sulphation - from sulphur oxides, and directly related to fuel sulphur and to the acid attack discussed at Q4 on this paper.
  • Water - at the hydroxyl region.
  • Soot - seen as a broad rise in the baseline rather than as a discrete peak, because soot scatters rather than absorbs selectively.
  • Fuel dilution, glycol ingress from coolant, and additive depletion.

Strengths and limits. It is fast, needs a very small sample, requires no reagents and returns several parameters at once, which makes it ideal for routine screening. But it is semi-quantitative - excellent at trends, weaker at absolute values - and it is blind to wear metals and to particles entirely. It tells you about the oil, not about the machine.

5. C) Particle count

Principle. Solid particles suspended in the oil are counted and sized. The usual method is automatic optical light obscuration - the oil passes through a cell across a light beam and each particle casts a shadow whose magnitude gives its size. Where the oil is too dark or too heavily sooted for light to pass, pore-blockage methods are used instead, and microscopic counting remains the referee method.

Reporting. Results are given as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands - the number of particles larger than each of several sizes - and those bands are condensed into a cleanliness code for trending and for specification. The ISO cleanliness code is the usual convention; no code or band table is reproduced here because MIW does not hold the standard, and in service the target code is set by the equipment manufacturer.

Why it matters. It is decisive for hydraulic, servo and control systems, where clearances are measured in microns and a particle population that is harmless in a crankcase will destroy a servo valve. It is also the direct measure of filtration and centrifuge performance and of ingress through seals and breathers - a rising count with unchanged wear metals usually means the filtration has failed, not that the machine has. And crucially it sees the large particles that spectrometry misses, which is why the two are complementary rather than alternative.

Limits. Optical counters count water droplets and air bubbles as particles, so a wet or aerated sample reads falsely high - the sample must be settled and, where necessary, the water content measured separately. And the technique says how many and how big, never what: it cannot distinguish steel from sand from soot.

6. D) Base Number against Acid Number

These two are confused more often than any other pair in oil analysis, and for a good reason: both are titrations and both are reported in the same units - milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil. They are nonetheless separate measurements of opposite things.

Base Number (BN)Acid Number (AN)
What it measuresThe alkaline reserve conferred by overbased detergent additivesThe quantity of acidic constituents present
Where it comes fromDeliberately formulated into the oilCombustion products (sulphuric and nitric acids) and oxidation of the oil itself
Direction in serviceFALLS as acid is neutralised and additive is consumedRISES as acid accumulates
Unitsmg KOH per grammemg KOH per gramme - the same units, which is why they are confused
What a bad value meansAlkaline reserve exhausted - the oil can no longer protect against acid attackAcid is present and is attacking the metal, or the oil is heavily oxidised

Three points that separate a good answer:

  • An oil can show a significant Base Number and a significant Acid Number at the same time. They are separate titrations of separate species, and weak organic acids can coexist with the alkaline reserve. Candidates who assume the two must be mutually exclusive misread every report of this kind.
  • Two standard titration methods for Base Number are in use, one employing a strongly acidic titrant and one a weaker one, and they give systematically different results on the same oil - the stronger method returning the higher value. Values from the two methods must never be trended together, and a change of laboratory or method can look exactly like a change in the oil.
  • Which number governs depends on the oil. For cylinder oils and trunk-piston engine oils, which carry a deliberate alkaline reserve to neutralise combustion acids, BN is the controlling parameter and is matched to the sulphur of the fuel actually burned. For turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils, which have no alkaline reserve at all, BN is meaningless and AN is the governing parameter - a rising AN is the direct measure of oxidative degradation.

On cylinder oils specifically, the relationship with fuel changed after the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit outside emission control areas took effect on 1 January 2020. Less sulphur means less acid and in principle a lower base number - but over-alkalinity is not a safe error either: unconsumed calcium detergent forms hard deposits which are abrasive and polish the liner. Base number is therefore matched to the fuel actually in use, and adjusted at fuel changeover.

7. Correct interpretation - reading the four together

This is what the stem's opening sentence is asking for, and it is where the four techniques stop being a list. The rule is that no single result diagnoses anything; pairs and trends do.

What the results showInterpretationConfidence
Iron rising, residual Base Number falling toward zeroCorrosive wear - the alkaline reserve is being consumed faster than it is supplied. In a cylinder, this is cold corrosionHigh - this is the clearest signature in the subject
Iron rising, Base Number still healthyNot corrosion. Look for abrasive ingress, scuffing, or a mechanical cause - and note that increasing the feed rate will make it worseHigh
Wear metals normal, particle count rising sharplyFiltration or centrifuge failure, or ingress - the machine is not necessarily wearing more, the oil is simply not being cleanedHigh
Particle count rising with wear metals normal AND spectrometry cleanSuspect coarse debris that spectrometry cannot see - bearing fatigue or spalling. Go physical immediatelyMedium - and the most dangerous case, because the spectrometric report looks reassuring
Silicon rising with iron and chromiumAbrasive dirt ingress through a defective air filter, seal or breather, causing three-body abrasive wearHigh
Silicon high alone, immediately after an overhaulSilicone sealant or antifoam residue, not dirt. Re-sample before actingHigh
Sodium and boron rising, FTIR showing glycol and waterCoolant leak. Find it before the bearings do - glycol is far more damaging to bearing metal than water aloneHigh
Vanadium and nickel present, FTIR showing fuel dilution, viscosity fallingFuel dilution from leaking injection equipment or poor combustionHigh
FTIR oxidation rising, Acid Number rising, no wear metalsThe oil is degrading - overheating, over-extended drain interval, or air entrainment. The machine is fine; the oil is notHigh
Copper, lead or tin rising aloneBearing distress. Copper alone can also mean cooler-tube corrosion, so identify the source before condemning a bearingMedium
Additive elements falling steadilyAdditive depletion - the oil is at the end of its useful lifeHigh
Additive elements risingThe wrong oil has been added. Check the topping-up recordsHigh

Two interpretive disciplines govern all of it. First, trend, do not spot-read: a value is meaningful only against that machine's own history, on samples taken the same way from the same point. Second, rate of change matters more than level: a moderate iron figure that has doubled in a month is far more serious than a higher figure that has been stable for a year.

8. Corrective measures

The stem asks for corrective measures, so the answer must say what is actually done. The general sequence on any adverse result is: verify the result, confirm it physically, correct the cause, and only then correct the oil.

  1. Verify before acting. Re-sample properly - same point, same method, machine at operating temperature and circulating - and re-test. A large proportion of alarming results are sampling artefacts, and shutting a machine down on a bad sample is an expensive mistake.
  2. Confirm physically. Where wear is indicated, go and look: magnetic plugs and chip detectors, filter elements cut open and examined, centrifuge sludge, ferrography for particle morphology, and where accessible a direct inspection - scavenge ports, crankcase, gear teeth, bearing clearances. Ferrography is the technique that tells you the shape of the debris and therefore the wear mechanism, which no elemental figure can.
  3. Treat abrasive ingress by finding the entry path - air filters, seals, breathers, hatch and cover joints - rather than by increasing filtration alone. Filtration removes the consequence; the seal is the cause.
  4. Treat coolant leaks at once. Locate by pressure-testing coolers, jackets and covers. Glycol attacks bearing metals and forms sludge, and a coolant leak left running will destroy bearings long before the wear metals reach an alarm value.
  5. Treat fuel dilution by attending to injection equipment, timing and combustion, and by checking for leakage past pumps and pipes. Then renew the oil - diluted oil cannot be recovered.
  6. Treat water by locating the source - cooler leaks, condensation, steam coils, stern tube ingress - and by running the purifier correctly: right gravity disc, right throughput, right temperature. Water is the one contaminant the ship can usually remove itself.
  7. Treat a rising particle count by checking and renewing filters, verifying filter differential pressure and bypass operation, and reviewing purifier and centrifuge operation - throughput, temperature, discharge interval. Then look for the ingress path.
  8. Treat low Base Number in a cylinder oil by increasing the feed rate or moving to a higher-BN oil, matched to the fuel sulphur actually burned - and re-check by scrape-down analysis rather than assuming the change worked. Do the reverse if residual BN is high, because over-alkalinity deposits abrasive calcium ash.
  9. Treat high Acid Number and high oxidation in a system oil by investigating the cause - overheating, air entrainment, contamination, an over-extended drain interval - then by partial or complete renewal, and by cleaning the system before recharging so the new oil is not immediately contaminated.
  10. Treat additive depletion by renewal. Additives cannot be topped up in service and an attempt to do so usually creates incompatibility.
  11. Record and close the loop. Every action taken, and the analysis result that follows it, goes into the machine's history under the planned maintenance system. Unrecorded corrective action is indistinguishable from no corrective action, and it destroys the trend that the next diagnosis will depend on.

And the standing caution: every condemning limit and target value comes from the engine builder and the oil supplier for that specific installation. None is quoted here, and none should be carried from one ship to another - the same figure on a different engine, oil or duty can be entirely wrong.

9. Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything

  • Sample from the same point, in the same way, at the same interval, with the machine at operating temperature and the oil circulating. A sample from a settled sump is not the same fluid as the oil in service.
  • Take the sample from a live line or a dedicated sampling point, upstream of the filter, not from the drain or the bottom of the tank.
  • Flush the sampling point and use clean, dedicated bottles. A contaminated bottle produces a particle count that condemns a healthy machine.
  • Keep a retained sample of every new oil delivered - FTIR is a differential measurement and is much weaker without the reference.
  • Label fully: ship, machine, sampling point, running hours since the last change and since overhaul, oil grade and any top-up.
  • Trend the results, and read the rate of change, not the absolute value.
  • Plan the sampling in the planned maintenance system under the ISM maintenance regime, so that it happens whether or not anyone remembers.

The closing point is the one the stem's preamble is really driving at: analysis does not maintain machinery - it tells you where to look. Its value is realised entirely in the interpretation and the corrective action that follow, and a programme that produces reports nobody reads and acts on is a cost with no benefit.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks, undivided, four named sub-items - and a preamble that adds two more tasks. The stem's first sentence says that periodical oil analysis, its correct interpretation and corrective measures, are of critical significance, and it prints "discuss" rather than "briefly discuss". That is not scene-setting; it is an instruction. So the shape is roughly: half the answer across the four techniques, and the other half on interpretation, corrective measures and sampling. A candidate who describes the four techniques beautifully and stops has answered a different and easier question.

Open with the three questions

Is the machine wearing? Is the oil still fit? Is anything getting in? Spectrometry and particle count answer the first; FTIR with BN and AN answer the second; all four answer the third from different directions. Two sentences, and the four techniques become a system instead of a list - which is exactly what "discuss" is asking for.

The qualification that most candidates omit

Spectrometry reports the fine fraction only. The method needs the particle to be fully atomised in the excitation source, and large particles are not - so the response falls away with increasing size. Normal rubbing wear is fine and is seen well; bearing fatigue, spalling, scuffing and gear pitting are coarse and may be invisible. Therefore a clean spectrometric report is not evidence of a healthy machine. Write that sentence: it is the single most valuable thing in the question, and it is the reason particle count exists alongside spectrometry rather than instead of it.

FTIR - two words to get right

Interferogram and differential. The instrument gathers all wavelengths at once as an interferogram and Fourier transforms it into a spectrum - that is what the name means and why it is fast. And it works by subtracting the spectrum of the same oil unused, so without a new-oil reference sample the result is much weaker. That last point is also a practical instruction: keep a retained sample of every oil delivery.

The four techniques in one table

Tells you aboutSeesBlind to
SpectrometryThe machineWhat the debris is, elementallyLarge particles; the condition of the oil
FTIRThe oilChemical change - oxidation, nitration, sulphation, water, soot, fuel, glycolWear metals; particles
Particle countThe machine and the filtrationHow many and how bigWhat the particles are; counts water and air as particles
BN / ANThe oilAlkaline reserve and acid loadEverything mechanical

The pairing to remember: spectrometry says WHAT, particle count says HOW MUCH and HOW BIG. Neither is sufficient alone, and that is the practical point of the whole question.

BN and AN - the three things that score

  1. Same units, opposite directions. Both mg KOH per gramme - which is why they are confused. BN falls, AN rises.
  2. An oil can show both at once. They are separate titrations of separate species; they are not two ends of one scale.
  3. Which one governs depends on the oil. Cylinder and trunk-piston engine oils carry an alkaline reserve, so BN governs. Turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils have no reserve, so AN governs and is the measure of oxidative degradation.

And the trap within the trap: two standard BN titration methods are in use and they give systematically different values on the same oil, the stronger titrant returning the higher figure. Never trend them together - a change of laboratory can look exactly like a change in the oil.

Interpretation - learn the pairs, not the values

The examiner asking for "correct interpretation" wants diagnostic pairs:

  • Iron up + residual BN near zero = corrosive wear. In a cylinder, cold corrosion.
  • Iron up + BN still healthy = not corrosion. More oil will make it worse.
  • Particle count up + wear metals normal = filtration or centrifuge failure, or ingress.
  • Particle count up + spectrometry clean = coarse debris spectrometry cannot see. The most dangerous case, because the report looks reassuring.
  • Silicon up + iron and chromium up = abrasive dirt ingress.
  • Sodium and boron up + FTIR glycol and water = coolant leak.
  • Vanadium and nickel + fuel dilution on FTIR + viscosity down = fuel dilution.
  • Oxidation up + AN up + no wear metals = the oil is degrading, the machine is fine.
  • Additive elements up = somebody added the wrong oil.

Corrective measures - the sequence

Verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil. Re-sample before acting, because a large proportion of alarming results are sampling artefacts. Then go and look: magnetic plugs, filters cut open, centrifuge sludge, ferrography - ferrography being the one that tells you the shape of the debris and therefore the wear mechanism. Then attack the cause - the leaking seal, the failing cooler, the injection equipment - and only then renew or adjust the oil. Increasing filtration to deal with dirt ingress treats the consequence and leaves the cause running.

Quote no numbers - and say why

No condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, cleanliness code, base number or wear rate should be given as a general rule. Every one is engine-builder, oil, application and service specific, and a figure carried from one installation to another is a genuine hazard as well as an examination risk. Saying "the limit comes from the engine builder's service letter and the oil supplier's recommendation for this installation" scores better than a remembered figure that may belong to a different machine - and it is the answer a Chief Engineer should give.

Common mistakes

  • Describing the four techniques and never answering the stem's demand for interpretation and corrective measures - roughly half the marks.
  • Omitting the particle-size limitation of spectrometry, and so implying a clean report means a healthy machine.
  • Treating spectrometry and particle count as alternatives rather than complements.
  • Saying FTIR measures wear metals. It is blind to them.
  • Forgetting that FTIR is a differential measurement needing a new-oil reference.
  • Assuming BN and AN cannot both be significant in the same sample.
  • Applying BN to a turbine or hydraulic oil, which has no alkaline reserve.
  • Quoting condemning limits, ppm values or cleanliness codes as general rules.
  • Ignoring sampling technique, without which every result is unreliable.

Examiner traps

  • "Your spectrometric report is clean. Is the engine healthy?" No - it says the fine fraction is normal. Coarse debris from fatigue or spalling may not appear at all.
  • "What does FTIR see that spectrometry cannot, and vice versa?" FTIR sees the chemical condition of the oil; spectrometry sees the elements. Neither sees the other's subject.
  • "Why is water a problem for a particle counter?" Optical counters count droplets and air bubbles as particles, so a wet or aerated sample reads falsely high.
  • "BN is 40 and AN is 3. Is that possible?" Yes - separate titrations of separate species.
  • "Would you trend BN results from two different laboratories?" No - two standard methods exist and give systematically different values.
  • "Iron is up. Do you increase the cylinder oil feed rate?" Only if residual BN is low. If BN is healthy the cause is not corrosion and more oil will make it worse.
  • "What tells you the shape of the wear debris?" Ferrography - and shape identifies the wear mechanism.
  • "Where do your condemning limits come from?" The engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier for this installation. Not from a textbook.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Show me where you take your main engine system oil sample and talk me through doing it.
  • Your last report shows sodium and boron rising. What do you do?
  • What is in a scrape-down analysis and how does it differ from a system oil analysis?
  • How does your purifier remove water, and how would you know it was working?
  • Which of your oils has no base number, and what do you monitor instead?
  • You have an alarming result on a Sunday at sea. What is your first action?

Memory framework

Three questions, four techniques, two pairs, one sequence. THREE QUESTIONS: is it wearing, is the oil fit, is anything getting in. FOUR TECHNIQUES: spectrometry says WHAT, particle count says HOW MUCH and HOW BIG, FTIR says what the OIL has become, BN/AN says how much fight is LEFT. TWO PAIRS for diagnosis: iron with residual BN, and particle count with spectrometry. ONE SEQUENCE for action: verify - confirm physically - correct the cause - then correct the oil.

Regulation and source map

  • NO IMO OR STATUTORY INSTRUMENT PRESCRIBES A LUBRICATING OIL ANALYSIS PROGRAMME. None has been named, because naming one would give the answer false authority.
  • MARPOL Annex VI - the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil used outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020. Used only to explain cylinder oil base number selection; no provision is quoted or numbered.
  • ISO cleanliness code - named as the reporting convention for particle counts. NOT HELD by MIW; no code, band or table reproduced.
  • The two standard titration methods for Base Number - described by chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy. NOT HELD by MIW and no standard number asserted.
  • Engine builder service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation - the only correct sources for condemning limits, alarm levels and target values. Not held; none reproduced.
  • ISM Code chapter 10 - maintenance of the ship and equipment, under which sampling and analysis are planned and recorded.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

This question carries no statutory content and the answer says so expressly rather than borrowing authority from an unrelated instrument. Atomic emission spectrometry, infrared spectroscopy, particle counting and acid-base titration are not dated law and carry no amendment boundary; the answer would be identical at any sitting in this period.

The no-figures rule is absolute here and is the most important evidential feature of the answer. No condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, ISO cleanliness code, base number figure or wear rate is quoted as a general rule. Every one is engine-builder, oil, application and service specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard as well as an evidential defect. The engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation are the governing sources, and MIW holds neither.

The characteristic infrared wavenumbers are approximate - they are properties of the molecular bonds and vary with the chemical environment, and the integration windows are instrument and method specific. The two standard Base Number titration methods are not identified by standard number; they are distinguished by their chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy between them. The ISO cleanliness code is named as a reporting convention only. No oil company, analyser manufacturer, engine builder, laboratory or proprietary condition-monitoring programme is named anywhere.

The interpretation and corrective-measures material is engineering judgement, reasoned from the properties of the techniques. It answers the interpretation and corrective-measures limbs that this sitting's enlarged stem adds, and which the shorter form of the question set in November 2024 did not call for. It is not a manufacturer's recommendation and is not presented as one.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for
  2. __________A A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis
  3. __________A What spectrometry cannot see - and why that governs its use
  4. __________B B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy
  5. __________C C) Particle count
  6. __________D D) Base Number against Acid Number
  7. __________Correct interpretation - reading the four together
  8. __________Corrective measures
  9. __________Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything
Flashcards 10

1 Why analyse, and the three questions. 2 Elemental spectrometry. 3 Its particle-size limitation. 4 FTIR. 5 Particle count. 6 BN against AN (2-6 are the four printed sub-items). 7 Correct interpretation - the diagnostic pairs. 8 Corrective measures. 9 Sampling and trending.

The stem's preamble expressly demands interpretation and corrective measures, and prints 'discuss' not 'briefly discuss'. Sections 7 and 8 are roughly half the marks and are what most candidates never write.

A quiet trap that catches experienced engineers as often as candidates, because the report looks identical either way.

The stem demands 'correct interpretation'. No single result diagnoses anything - pairs and trends do, and rate of change beats absolute level.

And record every action, or the trend is destroyed.

This is the second half of what the stem asks for, and 'correct the cause before the oil' is the sentence that shows it is understood.

From the engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for that specific installation - never from a textbook and never from another ship. Every condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, cleanliness code and base number target is engine, oil, application and service specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard as well as an examination risk.

Saying this scores better than a remembered number, and it is the answer a Chief Engineer is supposed to give.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Four techniques answering three questions. Elemental spectrometry - the sample is excited in a plasma or rotating-disc arc, each element emits at characteristic wavelengths and intensity gives ppm; reads wear metals (iron, copper, lead, tin, chromium, aluminium), contaminants (silicon from dirt, sodium and boron from coolant, vanadium and nickel from residual fuel) and additive elements (calcium, magnesium, zinc, phosphorus). Its critical limitation: large particles are not fully atomised, so it reports the fine fraction only - fatigue, spalling and scuffing debris may be invisible, so a clean report is not a healthy machine. FTIR - bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers, all gathered as an interferogram and Fourier transformed; a differential measurement against the same oil unused; detects oxidation, nitration, sulphation, water, soot (baseline rise), fuel dilution, glycol, additive depletion; fast but semi-quantitative and blind to wear metals. Particle count - light obscuration, or pore blockage on dark oil, microscopy as referee; counts per millilitre in cumulative bands, condensed to a cleanliness code; decisive for hydraulics, the measure of filtration performance, and it sees what spectrometry misses - but counts water and air as particles and cannot say what they are. BN and AN - both mg KOH/g, BN falls, AN rises, both can be present at once; two standard BN methods differ systematically so never trend them together; BN governs cylinder and trunk-piston oils, AN governs turbine, hydraulic, gear and stern tube oils. Interpret by pairs: iron up with BN gone = corrosion; iron up with BN healthy = not corrosion; count up with metals normal = filtration failure. Then verify, confirm physically, correct the cause, correct the oil.
Keywords

Critical numbers
  • 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil outside emission control areas, in force 1 January 2020 - the only figure quoted in this answer
  • NO condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, ISO cleanliness code, base number or wear rate is quoted as a general rule
  • Both BN and AN are expressed in milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil - the same units, opposite meanings
  • The governing sources for all limits: the engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation
Critical regulation
None. No IMO or statutory instrument prescribes a lubricating oil analysis programme, and saying so expressly is correct rather than evasive. MARPOL Annex VI is named only for the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit that changed cylinder oil base number selection. Sampling and analysis are planned and recorded under ISM Code chapter 10 as part of the maintenance regime.
Major trap
Describing the four techniques and never answering the stem's express demand for correct interpretation and corrective measures - which the preamble states and which "discuss" rather than "briefly discuss" reinforces. That is roughly half the marks. Second trap: omitting the particle-size limitation of spectrometry, and so implying that a clean spectrometric report means a healthy machine. It does not - coarse debris from bearing fatigue, spalling and scuffing may not be seen at all. Third: assuming BN and AN cannot both be significant in the same sample, or applying BN to a turbine or hydraulic oil that has no alkaline reserve. Fourth: trending base numbers from two different laboratories or methods - the two standard methods give systematically different values on the same oil. Fifth: quoting condemning limits, ppm values or cleanliness codes as general rules. Every one is engine-builder, oil and application specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard.

Also on the platform: QP2406 Q4 - cold corrosion, where scrape-down iron and residual base number are the controlling measurement · QP2406 Q5 - the ISM maintenance and critical-equipment regime under which sampling is planned and recorded

First in set First set here. The same task returns at November 2024 Q9.

Production metadata (review build only)
Reuse tier D · Verification Authored on the QP2411-Q9 route with the interpretation and corrective-measures material written fresh for this sitting's enlarged stem, and with the donor's evidential discipline inherited whole. This is instrumental analysis and machinery condition monitoring: NO IMO OR STATUTORY INSTRUMENT PRESCRIBES A LUBRICATING OIL ANALYSIS PROGRAMME, and none is named, so the answer does not borrow authority from an unrelated instrument. The techniques are described at mechanism level and carried as engineering and analytical-chemistry reference: atomic emission spectrometry by plasma or rotating-disc electrode; the particle-size limitation arising from incomplete atomisation of large particles in the excitation source; infrared absorption at characteristic wavenumbers with Fourier transformation of the interferogram and differential comparison against the unused oil; particle counting by optical light obscuration, pore blockage or microscopy, reported in cumulative size bands; and Base Number and Acid Number as separate titrations expressed in milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme. THE NO-FIGURES RULE IS ABSOLUTE AND IS THE MOST IMPORTANT EVIDENTIAL FEATURE OF THIS ANSWER: no condemning limit, alarm level, ppm value, ISO cleanliness code, base number figure or wear rate is quoted as a general rule anywhere, because every one is engine-builder, oil, application and service specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard as well as an evidential defect. No standard number is asserted for either Base Number titration method; the two methods are distinguished by their chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy between them. No oil company, analyser manufacturer, engine builder, laboratory or proprietary condition-monitoring programme is named. The single dated fact is the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020, four and a half years before this sitting, used only to explain cylinder oil base number selection; no MARPOL Annex VI provision is quoted or numbered, consistent with the corpus holding that Annex as citation-ready only.
Provenance P2_AUTHORITATIVE_SECONDARY 2 · INTERNAL_REUSE_VERIFIED 1 · ENGINEERING_JUDGEMENT 6 · UNRESOLVED 7 · TIME_SENSITIVE_REVERIFY 1
Sources
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - atomic emission spectrometry: the sample is excited in a plasma or across a rotating-disc electrode arc, each element emits light at characteristic wavelengths, and the intensity at each wavelength gives that element's concentration in parts per million. Stated by mechanism; no instrument, method standard or manufacturer is named.
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - the particle-size limitation of emission spectrometry: large particles are not fully atomised in the excitation source, so the response falls away progressively with increasing particle size and the method effectively reports the fine fraction only. This is the constraint that governs how the whole technique may be used and it is stated as such, not as a footnote.
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - Fourier Transform Infrared spectroscopy: an infrared beam is passed through a thin film of oil, molecular bonds absorb at characteristic wavenumbers, the interferogram is Fourier transformed to a spectrum, and that spectrum is subtracted from the spectrum of the same oil unused. A differential measurement. Wavenumber positions are given as approximate, because they vary with chemical environment and with the instrument and method.
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - particle counting by automatic optical light obscuration, by pore blockage where the oil is too dark, or by microscopic count as the referee method; results reported as counts per millilitre in cumulative size bands and expressed as a cleanliness code. The ISO cleanliness code is named as the reporting convention only - MIW does not hold the standard and no code, band or table is reproduced.
  • ENGINEERING AND ANALYTICAL-CHEMISTRY REFERENCE - Base Number as the alkaline reserve conferred by overbased detergent additives, and Acid Number as the quantity of base required to neutralise the acidic constituents present, both expressed in milligrammes of potassium hydroxide per gramme of oil - which is why they are confused - and moving in opposite directions in service.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - that two distinct standard titration methods for Base Number are in use, one employing a strongly acidic titrant and one a weaker acidic titrant, giving systematically different results on the same oil, the stronger method returning the higher value. Described by chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy; no standard number is asserted, because MIW holds no test-method standard.
  • P2 AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY - the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil used outside emission control areas, in force since 1 January 2020, under MARPOL Annex VI. Used solely to explain why cylinder oil base number is now selected against the fuel actually burned. No Annex VI provision is quoted or numbered; the corpus holds Annex VI as citation-ready only.
  • ENGINEERING JUDGEMENT - the interpretation and corrective-measures analysis in sections 7 and 8, and the sampling and trending discipline in section 9. This is the material the printed stem demands and the donor paper was not asked for. It is reasoned from the mechanisms and from the properties of the techniques; it is not a manufacturer's recommendation and is not presented as one.
  • INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED: QP2411-Q9 (November 2024), the donor - a NEAR stem five months later whose route and evidential discipline are reused and whose stem is enlarged here by an express demand for interpretation and corrective measures. Its own record anticipated this question by name and recorded QP2406-Q9 as unsolved intake; that instruction is discharged.
Unresolved
  • NO CONDEMNING LIMIT, ALARM LEVEL, PPM VALUE, ISO CLEANLINESS CODE, BASE NUMBER FIGURE OR WEAR RATE IS QUOTED AS A GENERAL RULE ANYWHERE. Every such value is specific to the engine builder, the oil, the application and the service, and a single figure presented as general would be both an evidential defect and a genuine hazard if carried to another installation. The engine builder's service letters and the oil supplier's recommendation for the specific installation are the only correct sources.
  • The characteristic infrared wavenumbers are given as APPROXIMATE positions. They are properties of the molecular bonds and vary slightly with the chemical environment; exact positions and the integration windows used are instrument and method specific.
  • The two standard Base Number titration methods are described by their chemistry and by the direction of the discrepancy between them, and are NOT identified by standard number. MIW holds no test-method standard and no procedure is quoted.
  • The ISO cleanliness code is named as the reporting convention for particle counts, but no code, band or table is reproduced, because MIW does not hold the standard.
  • No oil company, analyser manufacturer, engine builder, laboratory or proprietary condition-monitoring programme is named anywhere.
  • The interpretation and corrective-measures material in sections 7 and 8, and the sampling discipline in section 9, are engineering judgement reasoned from the properties of the techniques. They are not manufacturers' recommendations and are not presented as such.
  • Whether a particular fault signature is present in a given case cannot be settled from the analysis alone; the answer gives the diagnostic pairs and directs the reader to physical confirmation - ferrography, magnetic plugs, filter and centrifuge residues - rather than asserting a diagnosis from numbers.
Re-verify before publication
  • That two distinct standard titration methods for Base Number are in use and give systematically different results on the same oil, the stronger titrant returning the higher value — Carried as authoritative secondary. MIW holds no test-method standard, and neither method is identified by standard number here. The chemistry and the direction of the discrepancy are stated. Confirm against the test-method standards before publication. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • The approximate infrared absorption regions attributed to oxidation, nitration, sulphation, water and soot — Given as approximate positions because they are properties of the molecular bonds and vary with chemical environment; exact positions and integration windows are instrument and method specific. No wavenumber value is asserted. Confirm against an analytical reference before publication. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • That the 0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit outside emission control areas was in force at this sitting — In force since 1 January 2020, four and a half years before the sitting, and used only to explain base number selection. Date-driven by construction and re-checked before publication. (B_CURRENCY_CHECK)

June 2024 rapid revision

Every question in one pass. Generated from each question's Quick Revision block — open the full answer from the question number.

QTopicAnswer routeCritical rule / numberMajor trap
Q1 Statutory Certificates on an Oil Tanker and Harmonization under SOLAS
16 marks		 What an oil tanker carries, and under which convention → SOLAS 1974/1988 certificates → MARPOL certificates - where the tanker differs most → Other conventions → Harmonization under SOLAS 74/88 - what it actually changed → A certificate that has just expired, in a port that cannot survey → The actions to take, in the order you would take them SOLAS regulation I/14 for duration, validity and every form of relief - the five-month endorsement, the three-month voyage extension and the one-month period of grace - read with regulation I/12 for issue and endorsement. The harmonized system itself comes from the 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and to the Load Line Convention, in force 3 February 2000.
Harmonized system in force 3 February 2000, under the 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and Load Lines		 Giving every certificate a five-year validity. The International Tonnage Certificate does not expire, the IEEC runs for the ship's life, the AFS certificate for the life of the system, the insurance certificates for the insurance period and the sanitation certificate for six months. Second trap: sailing on the three-month extension past the survey port - the regulation expressly forbids it, and the replacement certificate still runs from the original expiry date, so the extension buys a voyage, not time. Third trap, specific to this sitting: listing an IHM or Ready-for-Recycling certificate. The Hong Kong Convention entered into force on 26 June 2025, a year after this examination.
Q2 Common Law and Civil Law Systems, and Giving Effect to Conventions
16 marks		 The single difference from which the rest follows → The judge → The lawyer → Adversarial and inquisitorial - the models these roles create → How a State becomes bound in the first place → Monism and dualism → India as the worked example → The contrasting mechanism in a monist State → Why the difference matters at sea Article 253 of the Constitution of India is the provision that makes limb (b) an Indian answer rather than a general one: Parliament may legislate for the whole or any part of India to implement a treaty, and may do so even on State List subjects. The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 is what it has produced for the IMO conventions at this sitting.
Article 253 of the Constitution of India - the treaty-implementation power		 Equating dualism with common law and monism with civil law. They are independent axes - the Scandinavian civil law States are dualist and the United States is a common law State with self-executing treaties. Second trap: writing limb (b) with no country named, when the stem expressly says "with examples". Third trap, specific to this sitting: citing the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025. It commenced on 15 March 2026, twenty-one months after this examination - the governing statute here is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. Fourth: naming a case you are not certain of. A misattributed authority is worse than none, and the principle scores without it.
Q3 Port State Control - Clear Grounds, Detainable Deficiencies and PR17
16 marks		 The governing instrument at this sitting → "Clear grounds" - the definition → The circumstances that create clear grounds → ISM-related deficiencies → Five detainable deficiencies, their causes and their prevention → PR17 - what it is, and the scenarios that lead to it A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, supplies the clear-grounds definition, the three levels of inspection and the detention criteria. It is read with the ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92) for the ISM-related limb, because a major non-conformity is defined there and not in the PSC procedures.
A.1185(33) - Procedures for Port State Control, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023, operative at this sitting		 Defining clear grounds and then giving ISM-related deficiencies one line. The stem names two terms in a six-mark limb and both must be defined. Second trap: listing five deficiencies without the causes and preventive actions the stem also asks for - that is three answers per item, not one. Third: treating an OWS bypass as an ordinary maintenance deficiency. It is a deliberate act, a criminal offence in most jurisdictions, it makes the Oil Record Book a falsified document, and the consequences fall personally on the Chief Engineer. Fourth: writing PR17 as though SOLAS required it - it is IACS class practice, not an IMO instrument. Fifth, specific to this sitting: naming a 34th Assembly resolution. That Assembly sat in December 2025, eighteen months after this examination.
Q4 Cold Corrosion of Cylinder Liners in Super-Long-Stroke Engines
16 marks		 What cold corrosion is → Why the new generation of engines made it worse → How it presents, and how to tell it from other wear → Limb A - increased jacket cooling water temperature → Limb B - new cylinder liner designs → Limb C - new cylinder lubricators → The other failure mode - too much treatment → Monitoring - what closes the loop None. No IMO or statutory instrument prescribes a cylinder lubrication regime or a liner temperature, and the answer says so rather than borrowing authority from an unrelated instrument. MARPOL Annex VI is named only for the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit that changed base number selection, and for the Tier III requirements that drove exhaust gas recirculation.
0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil outside emission control areas, in force 1 January 2020 - the only figure quoted in this answer		 Describing water condensation instead of sulphuric acid condensation. Different mechanism, far less aggressive, and it removes the foundation of the whole answer. Second trap: treating the problem as purely a cooling issue and missing that higher firing pressure raises the dew point - the margin closes from both ends, not one. Third: recommending maximum feed rate and maximum base number as if over-treatment were free. Unconsumed calcium detergent forms abrasive ash, polishes the honed bore and causes ring sticking - so an over-alkaline oil can cause the wear it was chosen to prevent. Fourth: writing only the three lettered sections and never answering "discuss the causes". Fifth: quoting a remembered temperature, feed rate or base number - they are installation specific, and a figure carried between engines is a hazard as well as an examination risk.
Q5 ISM Code - Functional Requirements, Internal Audit and Non-Conformities
16 marks		 The functional requirements - ISM Code paragraph 1.4 → The objectives of an ISM internal audit → How the internal audit helps the external audit → The salient issues addressed in an internal audit → Frequency, and the arithmetic over the SMC cycle → Non-conformity and major non-conformity → Accidents and hazardous occurrences ISM Code paragraph 1.4 for the six functional requirements and paragraph 12.1 for the twelve-month internal audit interval - those two carry ten of the sixteen marks between them. Read with paragraphs 1.1.9 and 1.1.10 for the definitions and 9.1 for the reporting duty.
SIX functional requirements at paragraph 1.4 - a closed, numbered list		 Giving only the first half of the major non-conformity definition. It is a serious threat requiring immediate corrective action and it expressly includes the lack of effective and systematic implementation of a requirement of the Code - which is how a ship with nothing broken can carry a major non-conformity. Second trap: giving the twelve-month interval but never answering the question actually asked, which is how many over the five-year SMC validity - the answer is at least five. Third: describing an internal audit as an inspection; it audits the system, not the ship. Fourth: attributing a definition of "accident" to the ISM Code - it does not define one. Fifth: writing three paragraphs on the functional requirements for four marks and then rushing the twelve marks that follow.
Q6 MLC 2006 - Minimum Requirements, Employment, Enforcement and Abandonment
16 marks		 What the Convention is, and which version applies here → Limb (a) - Title 1: minimum requirements for seafarers to work on a ship → Limb (b) - Title 2: conditions of employment → Limb (c) - Title 5: flag State responsibilities → Limb (c) - port State and labour-supplying responsibilities → Limb (c) - the two provisions that make the system work → Limb (d) - abandonment, and when it arises → Limb (d) - the financial security system → Limb (d) - why the numbers rise, and what the institutions do Standard A2.5.2, introduced by the 2014 amendments and in force at this sitting, is the provision that makes limb (d) answerable - it supplies the three-limb definition of abandonment and the financial security system behind it. Read with Regulation 2.5 on repatriation, and with Title 5 for the certification and inspection machinery that detects the failure.
Convention in force 20 August 2013; operative amendment set at this sitting is the 2018 one		 Writing 2022-amendment content as law at a June 2024 sitting - appropriately sized PPE, free drinking water, the strengthened food and catering provisions, social connectivity and internet access, repatriation of a deceased seafarer's remains. Those entered into force on 23 December 2024, six months after this examination, and a candidate revising from a later syllabus will have them in mind as current. Second trap: giving only one half of the hours rule - it is an alternative between maximum work and minimum rest, and the examiner will ask for the half you omitted. Third: saying DMLC is one document - Part I is the flag State's, Part II is the shipowner's. Fourth: defining abandonment as the shipowner disappearing - he need not; two months of unpaid wages is enough on its own. Fifth: quoting a ratification figure - the available number is dated fifteen months after this sitting and cannot be carried back. Sixth: calling the MLC an IMO convention.
Q7 P&I Clubs - Funding, Cover and Claims Handling
16 marks		 What a P&I club is → Why it is not ordinary marine insurance → How the club collects funds - the tiers → Above the club - the International Group → "Protection" - liabilities to people and to the world → "Indemnity" - liability to cargo → The boundary - what P&I does not cover, and why → How the club handles a claim → The procedure for filing a claim There is no convention governing P&I - it is market practice, and saying so is correct rather than evasive. The Indian statutory hooks are what anchor the answer: the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, whose section 4(1)(s) makes an unpaid mutual insurance call a maritime claim supporting arrest, and the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 for utmost good faith and subrogation.
Hull and machinery classically covers three-fourths of collision (running-down) liability; the balance falls to the club		 Saying premium instead of call, which signals that the mutuality has not been understood - and then being unable to explain the supplementary or release call, both of which exist only because the members carry the risk themselves. Second trap: claiming P&I covers loss of hire or damage to the member's own ship. It does not - P&I covers liabilities, not the owner's own losses. Third: writing limb (c) as "tell the club and they pay". It is the largest limb at six marks, and what the club actually supplies is control and credit - a lawyer and surveyor on the quay, and a letter of undertaking that lifts an arrest without cash. Fourth: quoting pool or reinsurance figures, which are reset every policy year.
Q8 Salvage Law and General Average
16 marks		 Two doctrines, two questions → Limb A - the principles of modern salvage law → Limb B - what general average is → Limb C(i) - Entitlement → Limb C(ii) - Artificial general average → Limb C(iii) - Adjustment → Limb C(iv) - Contestation → Where the two doctrines meet Salvage Convention 1989 Articles 12, 13 and 14 carry the whole of limb A - no cure no pay, the ten criteria capped at salved value, and the environmental exception that qualifies the first. York-Antwerp Rule A carries limb B, and it must be introduced as CONTRACTUAL rather than legal, because that single fact is what makes limb C(ii) and the wrong-edition ground in limb C(iv) intelligible.
Salvage Convention 1989, in force 14 July 1996, replacing the Brussels Convention 1910		 Spending the answer on salvage, which candidates enjoy, and compressing limb C - which is half the marks and has four named sub-items needing four separate answers. Second trap: presenting "artificial general average" as a term defined in the York-Antwerp Rules. It is not - it is practitioner shorthand for common-benefit allowances the numbered Rules permit although they fail Rule A's common-safety test. Say so, and gain the mark the error costs. Third: saying the adjuster declares general average - the shipowner declares it, and the adjuster neither declares it nor decides liability. Fourth: taking contributory values at the time of the act rather than at the termination of the adventure. Fifth: treating the York-Antwerp Rules as law, when they apply only because the contract incorporates them - which is also why "which edition?" is a real question. Sixth, specific to this sitting: reaching for the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025, which commenced 15 March 2026, twenty-one months after this examination.
Q9 Lubricating Oil Condition Analysis Techniques
16 marks		 Why the oil is analysed, and what the four techniques are for → A) Elemental (spectrometric) analysis → What spectrometry cannot see - and why that governs its use → B) Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy → C) Particle count → D) Base Number against Acid Number → Correct interpretation - reading the four together → Corrective measures → Sampling and trending - without which none of it means anything None. No IMO or statutory instrument prescribes a lubricating oil analysis programme, and saying so expressly is correct rather than evasive. MARPOL Annex VI is named only for the 0.50 per cent sulphur limit that changed cylinder oil base number selection. Sampling and analysis are planned and recorded under ISM Code chapter 10 as part of the maintenance regime.
0.50 per cent m/m global sulphur limit for fuel oil outside emission control areas, in force 1 January 2020 - the only figure quoted in this answer		 Describing the four techniques and never answering the stem's express demand for correct interpretation and corrective measures - which the preamble states and which "discuss" rather than "briefly discuss" reinforces. That is roughly half the marks. Second trap: omitting the particle-size limitation of spectrometry, and so implying that a clean spectrometric report means a healthy machine. It does not - coarse debris from bearing fatigue, spalling and scuffing may not be seen at all. Third: assuming BN and AN cannot both be significant in the same sample, or applying BN to a turbine or hydraulic oil that has no alkaline reserve. Fourth: trending base numbers from two different laboratories or methods - the two standard methods give systematically different values on the same oil. Fifth: quoting condemning limits, ppm values or cleanliness codes as general rules. Every one is engine-builder, oil and application specific, and a figure carried between installations is a genuine hazard.
